# Creativity, ideation, and homogenization: literature screening, October 2026

## Scope and decision rule

This is a screening report, not a dataset update. It covers evidence on how generative AI changes human creative work: the rated creativity of what a person produces with AI at hand (`with_ai`), how alike different people's outputs become when they use AI (`across_people`), and what happens to unassisted creative work after a period of AI use (`after_ai`). The codebook (`docs/creativity-codebook.md`) fixes the vocabulary. No paper or estimate was added to the source files, the generated JSON, or the site. The site's creativity preview, which is hidden from the navigation, still holds the May 2026 collection.

The working eligibility rule for the core dataset has three criteria. People were randomly assigned to conditions that differ in generative-AI assistance. At least 50 people were analyzed across the arms of one randomized study. Creativity or diversity was scored on the output the participants produced, by raters or by a computed measure, so self-reports do not count. Papers that compare AI-generated output with human-generated output, observational studies of real output, and syntheses stay on the site as labeled context and never enter a pooled estimate. Randomized experiments with fewer than 50 people, non-random designs, model-only studies, theory papers, and studies that only count how much AI text exists are listed here and left out of the dataset.

"Unverified" in this report means that a required detail was not established from the material we could read, not that the study fails the criterion. "Needs clarification" means that a criterion rests on a passage the paper does not contain, usually how people came to be in each condition, or that a checker found a reason to doubt it. Author labels for papers that were not screened in full (experiments without full text and works excluded at triage) come from the search records and were not checked. Every other label was checked against Crossref, OpenAlex, arXiv, OSF, or the publisher.

## How the screening was done

**Search.** The search ran 18 agents, each on one source or angle and blind to the others: economics working papers, management, psychology and creativity journals, education, human-computer interaction, general science, recent arXiv, three observational domains, reference lists of reviews, replications and comments, AI-design contrasts, professionals in the field, writing and homogenization, design and engineering, and work after AI use. Four seed lists came first: the May 2026 collection, the included-study lists of three meta-analyses, the works that cite eight anchor experiments in OpenAlex, and the leads from a first scan. The lanes ran 1,636 queries. With the seed lists they returned 1,768 records, and after linking versions of one work by DOI, arXiv id, listed other versions, and near-identical titles, 1,002 works remained, each with a stable id (`c001` to `c1002`).

**Triage.** Two agents read each abstract independently and called it an experiment, context (AI versus human output, observational, or synthesis), or out, with the participant count and whether random assignment was stated. A work left the pipeline only when both agreed on "out". The 25 papers of the May collection went straight to the two-screener pipeline, and the other 145 works from the seed lists were triaged. Of the 832 works that only the search lanes found, the 424 whose search class was an experiment of any kind were triaged. The other 408 are listed in `docs/creativity-screening/identified-not-screened.md`.

**Full text.** A script tried arXiv, OSF, publisher PDF patterns, OpenAlex open-access locations, Semantic Scholar, Europe PMC, the PDF link on the DOI landing page, and an arXiv title search. Retrieval agents then searched preprint servers, repositories, and author pages for what the script could not fetch, under a hard rule against pirate sources, credentials, and bot-check evasion. Germán downloaded 30 papers by hand. Every PDF was converted to markdown with pymupdf4llm, and both files sit in `Research/ai-lab/literature/creativity/`.

**Two-screener pipeline.** For each experiment with full text, two agents filled the same form independently with different reading orders: one from the text to the tables, the other from the tables to the text. A reconciler listed every difference, settled each against the source with a quote and a page or table, and confirmed the eligibility facts even where the screeners agreed. For papers headed for the core, three challengers each tried to refute one criterion from the source (assignment, people, outcome). A finalizer then accepted or rejected each challenge against the source and stated the final verdict on the three criteria. A citation checker confirmed authors, title, year, venue, DOI, and versions against Crossref, OpenAlex, arXiv, OSF, or the publisher.

**Short-form pipeline.** Context papers, experiments under 50 people, designs that the abstract called non-random, and experiments whose abstract did not say how people were assigned went through one screener, an independent checker who found every passage again, a fixer when the checker disagreed, and the same citation check. The checker flags any paper that turns out to randomize people. Flagged papers with 50 or more people analyzed then went through the two-screener pipeline.

**Who did the work.** The search, the triage, and the first screening rounds ran as Claude Fable 5.1 agents on 4 to 6 October 2026. The finishing rounds, the short-form checks, and the experiments with unstated assignment ran as Codex jobs (gpt-6-astra) on 8 and 9 October, 1,121 jobs in all. Every judgment is a JSON file under `docs/creativity-screening/forms/` with the quote and locator behind it, so a research assistant can trace each cell of this report to a page. No human has yet verified the forms.

**Audit.** After the report was assembled, Codex jobs audited it in 6 rounds. In each round, finder jobs checked one part of the report against the forms, the source texts, and the registries: the prose, the coverage counts, the links, the tables, the status of each paper, the passages behind each recommended paper, and the author labels. A verifier job then checked each finding again on its own. We addressed every confirmed finding at its source (a form, a citation file, the report builder, or this text) and rebuilt the report. The rounds confirmed 65, 11, 6, 3, 4, and 1 findings, in that order. The audit records are in `docs/creativity-screening/audit/`.

## Coverage limits

Every search lane ran into the anonymous daily request budget of OpenAlex, so citation chasing and several keyword passes used Crossref, Semantic Scholar, Europe PMC, arXiv, and web search instead. Crossref carries no abstracts for Elsevier journals, and Semantic Scholar rate-limited some citation lists. ScienceDirect, Taylor and Francis, SAGE, INFORMS, Wiley, and Springer answered scripted requests with HTTP 403 or a JavaScript bot check. Papers in those venues were confirmed from Crossref or PubMed records, and their full texts must be downloaded by hand. The lanes ran on 4 and 5 October 2026 and cover work posted up to then.

Several candidates are trial registrations with no paper yet, for example the AEA registry entries of Contreras and Leatherbee, Heshmati and Li, Huang, Long, and Pi, and Zissimos, Markoulakis, and Maeda. They are listed so that the next screening checks them again.

## Results at a glance

The search identified 1,002 works. Of these, 569 were triaged from their abstracts, and the 25 papers of the May collection went straight to full-text screening. The other 408 were not screened, because their search class was not an experiment of any kind; `docs/creativity-screening/identified-not-screened.md` lists them (AI versus human output 164, observational 116, other or unclear 67, synthesis 55, model only 6). A screening form exists for 343 papers: 157 went through the two-screener pipeline to a final verdict, and 186 went through the short form. The core recommendation covers 87 papers. Another 43 need a clarification before they can enter, 28 are randomized experiments with fewer than 50 people, and 227 experiments have no full text on file and are listed with where to get them. Context papers number 104. Screening excluded 78 papers after reading and triage excluded 27.

## Decisions for Review 1

1. Which recommended papers enter, and which of the papers that need clarification we ask the authors about first.
2. Whether the 50-participant rule holds. The screening found 28 randomized experiments below the threshold. Their analyzed samples range from 9 to 47 people, and 9 of them have no analyzed count recorded. The rule also excludes Anderson, Shah, and Kreminski (2024) and Padmakumar and He (2024) from the May collection.
3. How to display diversity effects. The section on the diversity measure sets out three options and what each would show.
4. The standard-error rule when a paper computes its precision over ideas, texts, or pairs of outputs instead of people, and the order of preferred standardizers.
5. Which result leads the page: creativity with AI together with diversity across people, or creativity after AI.
6. Whether the site hosts PDFs for creativity papers. Proposed rule: link to the DOI or arXiv page, and host a PDF only when the paper is open access.
7. Whether Contractor and Reyes (2026) enters as an `across_people` record, flagged as our own paper. It would appear in both sections.
8. Whether exposure to AI-generated ideas, with no interaction with a model, counts as generative-AI assistance. This decides the `ideas_supplied` role, which covers Doshi and Hauser (2024) and Ashkinaze et al. (2025) among others.
9. How to treat experiments that randomize intact classes or teams with few clusters, for example Abu Asba and Al-Rababah (2026) with three classes, and the class-level lotteries in several education papers.
10. Whether a writing-quality score counts as usefulness. Gero et al. (2026) would become core if an IELTS-based writing-quality composite counts.
11. Whether within-person designs that randomize the order of conditions enter the core under the same 50-person rule.
12. Two recommended papers sit at the edge of the codebook's scope, and their forms leave the call to Review 1. McGuire, De Cremer, and Van de Cruys (2024) use a 2020 neural poetry generator that is not a large language model. Qin et al. (2025) ask writers for supporting arguments for a given health claim and score them as ideas.

## Recommended for the core dataset

The 87 recommended papers are randomized experiments in which people were assigned to generative-AI assistance, with at least 50 people analyzed and creativity or diversity scored on the output. By question, 79 answer `with_ai`, 35 answer `across_people`, and 16 answer `after_ai`; a paper can answer more than one. For 4 of them no question is coded yet, because the form does not record whether AI was available while the output was produced. The table gives the people analyzed in each randomized study, the design, the sample, the role of the AI, the task, and whether the paper's supplement is on file. The statistics quoted in the notes come from the PDF or, where no PDF exists, from the publisher's article text. The notes give the version read, the one-line finding, any sample overlap the form records, the first reported contrast for each question, and what would settle each open item.

### Notes on each recommended paper

**Agarwal, Naaman, and Vashistha (2025)** [c026], "AI Suggestions Homogenize Writing Toward Western Styles and Diminish Cultural Nuances". Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI '25), Yokohama, Japan, April 26-May 1, 2025, ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3713564

- Version read: arXiv 2409.11360v3 [cs.HC], 12 Mar 2025 (margin stamp, p. 1), typeset in the CHI '25 (April 26-May 1, 2025, Yokohama, Japan) proceedings format with ACM DOI 10.1145/3706598.3713564 (p. 1). This is the arXiv copy, not the ACM version of record. A later version exists.
- Finding: Random assignment to GPT-4o inline suggestions raised the mean cosine similarity between Indian and American essays from 0.48 (SD 0.11) to 0.54 (SD 0.13), t(6958) = −17.89, p < 0.001, d = −0.44 over essay pairs (pp. 9-10, Section 4.3.1), and lowered a classifier's accuracy at telling Indian from American essays from 90.6% to 83.5%, t(38) = 2.25, p = 0.03, d = 0.71 (p. 10, Section 4.3.2).
- `across_people`, Cross-culture similarity: pairwise cosine similarity between Indian and American essays on the same topic (embedding_similarity, unit pair): Mean cross-culture similarity No AI 0.48 (SD = 0.11) vs AI 0.54 (SD = 0.13); t(6958) = −17.89, p < 0.001; Cohen's d = −0.44. Pair count printed for No AI only (2784); people per arm in Table 1 (p. 3). 95% CIs only in Figure 6(b). Similarity is higher with AI, so diversity is lower. [p. 9, Section 4.3.1 (setup and means); p. 10, Section 4.3.1 (t-test and d); p. 10, Figure 6(b)-(c)]
- `with_ai`, Lexical diversity of each essay (type-token ratio) (lexical_overlap, unit output): Figure 9 caption: 'On average, AI increases the lexical diversity for Indians, taking it closer to American styles'. No AI-vs-No-AI test printed. Cohort-gap tests: without AI, Indian vs American t(210) = −2.94, p = 0.003, Cohen's d = −0.41; with AI, t(258) = −0.32, p = 0.75, Cohen's d = −0.04. [p. 12, Section 4.3.5 and Figure 9(a)]
- Needs authors: Assignment and attrition (open question 3): no count at assignment, and no count of assigned people who did not finish both parts or were dropped by the born-and-lived-in-country rule. The analyzed Indian split is 24 vs 36, and 58 US participants were analyzed against the paper's stated target of India's 60. Random assignment is stated four times and 118 people were analyzed, so criteria 1 and 2 hold. Attrition by arm would bear on the contrasts, not on eligibility. The document names no posted data.
- Needs authors: Precision (open question 4): the similarity tests treat essay pairs as independent and the classifier tests treat cross-validation folds as independent, with no participant-level or clustered standard error and no data or code link. This limits pooling (codebook Section 10), not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Public-figure counts (open question 6 and the unresolved reconciliation difference on S1-O7): the text gives 16/36 (44%) of AI-condition Indian participants choosing an Indian public figure, but the blue cells of Table 7's AI column sum to 23 of 36, and 16 is the number of distinct Indian names there. The No AI column's 13 of 24 matches the text. S1-O7 is a secondary 'other' outcome. The ACM version of record can be checked first (open question 8). If it prints the same numbers, only the authors can settle it.
- Needs journal version: Version (open question 8): the ACM CHI '25 version of record could not be opened (HTTP 403 on 2026-10-05) and was not compared with the arXiv v3 file on disk, which carries the CHI '25 reference format.

**Akben, Carignan, and Anyimah (2026)** [c696], "The Confidence Cage: How Creative Self-Efficacy Hinders Gen AI-Augmented Ideation". Proceedings of the 59th Hawaii International Conference on System Sciences. https://doi.org/10.24251/hicss.2026.013

- Version read: Published proceedings paper, Proceedings of the 59th Hawaii International Conference on System Sciences, 2026, printed pp. 95-103 (PDF pp. 1-9; proceedings identification on PDF p. 1).
- Finding: Among 300 analyzed participants, GPT-4o coaching increased automated AUT creativity versus web search (unadjusted M = 3.21 versus 2.98; reported d = 0.77), but adjusted arm labels conflict between the results prose and Table 2 (PDF p. 4, Section 3.1; p. 5, Section 4; p. 6, Table 2).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Not stated. No participant overlap or reanalysis of another paper is reported; the methods describe recruitment for this experiment. This does not establish absence of overlap.
- `with_ai`, Post-test composite creativity score on Frisbee alternative uses (automated_score, unit participant): Unadjusted post-test, Table 2 (analyzed participants): Human-AI n = 150, M = 3.21, SD = 0.22; Human-Search n = 150, M = 2.98, SD = 0.31. Baseline values in the same table: Human-AI M = 2.94, SD = 0.37; Human-Search M = 2.85, SD = 0.38 (PDF p. 6, Table 2). ANCOVA controlling for baseline creativity, age, work experience, gender, and race: F(1, 293) = 58.28, p < .001, η²p = .166. The results prose gives adjusted Human-AI M = 3.19, SE = 0.02, 95% CI [3.15, 3.24], versus Human-Search M = 2.99, SE = 0.02, 95% CI [2.95, 3.04]; adjusted mean difference = 0.20 points and Cohen's d = 0.77. R² = .251, adjusted R² = .235 (PDF p. 5, Section 4). UNRESOLVED SOURCE CONFLICT (flagged by both screeners): Table 2 instead prints adjusted Human-Search M = 3.19 [3.15, 3.24] and Human-AI M = 2.99 [2.95, 3.04], reversing the arm labels relative to the results prose. Both versions are retained as printed; no definitive adjusted arm labels are selected. The coded direction follows the unadjusted values, results prose, and Figure 1, not the contradictory adjusted column (PDF pp. 5-6, Section 4, Table 2 and Figure 1). Path analysis: direct condition effect on creativity after accounting for ideation B = 0.169, SE = 0.032, z = 5.25, p < .001; Table 3 gives 95% CI [0.110, 0.232]. Ideation-to-creativity path B = 0.030, SE = 0.012, z = 2.50, p = .013; Table 3 gives 95% CI [0.009, 0.055]. Creativity-model R² = .298. Table 3 also reports Condition × MOD for the creativity outcome: B = 0.051**, SE = 0.016, 95% CI [0.004, 0.104], where ** denotes p < .01 (PDF p. 6, Section 4 and Table 3; p. 7, Table 3 note). Indirect condition effect on creativity through self-reported ideation: B = 0.031, SE = 0.013, z = 2.38, p = .017, 95% CI [0.010, 0.062]. Conditional indirect effects: low CSE (−1 SD), B = 0.040, SE = 0.017, z = 2.40, p = .016, 95% CI [0.013, 0.080]; mean CSE, B = 0.031, SE = 0.013, z = 2.38, p = .017, 95% CI [0.010, 0.062]; high CSE (+1 SD), B = 0.021, SE = 0.010, z = 2.18, p = .029, 95% CI [0.006, 0.050]. Index of moderated mediation B = −0.008, SE = 0.004, z = −2.05, p = .040; Table 3 gives 95% CI [−0.021, −0.001]. High-minus-low difference B = −0.019, SE = 0.009, z = −2.05, p = .041; Table 3 gives 95% CI [−0.048, −0.003] (PDF p. 6, Section 4 and Table 3). The methods specify 5,000 bootstrap samples and 95% bias-corrected intervals for mediation (PDF p. 5, Section 3.4). The standardizer for the reported Cohen's d is not stated (PDF p. 5, Sections 3.4 and 4). Direct, indirect, and interaction estimates are ancillary results for the same creativity outcome, distinct from the main ANCOVA total contrast. [PDF p. 4 (printed p. 98), Section 3.1: analyzed participant counts; PDF p. 5 (printed p. 99), Section 3.4: bootstrap method, and Section 4: ANCOVA statistics; PDF p. 6 (printed p. 100), Table 2: unadjusted arm statistics and contradictory adjusted labels, Figure 1: direction, Section 4 and Table 3: path and indirect-effect statistics; PDF p. 7 (printed p. 101), Table 3 note: condition coding and significance symbols.]
- Needs authors: Shared unresolved source conflict: which adjusted arm labels are correct? Section 4 assigns 3.19 [3.15, 3.24] to Human-AI and 2.99 [2.95, 3.04] to Human-Search, whereas Table 2 assigns them to the opposite arms (PDF p. 5 [printed p. 99], Section 4; p. 6 [printed p. 100], Table 2). Figure 1 agrees with the narrative direction but does not settle the contradictory labels; a correction, underlying adjusted-results output, or author clarification is missing. This affects extraction of adjusted estimates, not any eligibility criterion. Author confirmation of the corrected arm labels and the underlying adjusted-results output would settle it.
- Needs authors: What were the total and arm-specific randomized counts before exclusions, and exclusions by arm? Section 3.1 gives 327 recruited and 300 analyzed people but no separate initial assignment counts (PDF p. 4 [printed p. 98]); randomized-count fields remain null. This affects participant-flow documentation, not eligibility: random assignment is explicit and 300 analyzed people exceed the threshold. Author counts at assignment and a breakdown of exclusions by arm would settle it.
- Needs authors: Which exact scoring model/version, score scale, and response-aggregation rule produced the participant creativity composite? These details are not stated in Section 3.3 (PDF pp. 4-5 [printed pp. 98-99]). This affects scoring replication and aggregation details, not criterion 3: the methods explicitly say that generated responses received a composite creativity score. Author scoring documentation, including the model/version, scale, and aggregation rule, would settle it.
- Needs authors: Which standard deviation was used for Cohen's d = 0.77? The paper reports the value without specifying its standardizer (PDF p. 5 [printed p. 99], Sections 3.4 and 4). This affects interpretation and later use of the standardized effect, not any eligibility criterion. The authors' exact effect-size formula and standard deviation would settle it.

**Ashkinaze et al. (2025)** [c002], "How AI Ideas Affect the Creativity, Diversity, and Evolution of Human Ideas: Evidence From a Large, Dynamic Experiment". Proceedings of the ACM Collective Intelligence Conference (CI 2025), San Diego, CA, USA, August 4-6, 2025 (ACM). https://doi.org/10.1145/3715928.3737481

- Version read: arXiv:2401.13481v3 [cs.CY], 31 Jul 2025 (side stamp, p. 1), typeset as the Collective Intelligence Conference paper (CI 2025, August 4-6, 2025, San Diego, CA), ACM, 29 pages, DOI 10.1145/3715928.3737481 (p. 1, ACM Reference Format). The text ends mid-sentence in SI 11.3 (p. 29) in both the PDF and the markdown. A later version exists.
- Finding: Among 844 online volunteers analyzed (3,414 AUT ideas analyzed), seeing ChatGPT example ideas did not change classifier-scored originality (joint F(4, 19.86) = 0.12, p = 0.97; p. 8, Section 5.4; per-arm coefficients in Table 18, p. 26), but both high-exposure conditions raised global idea diversity over the human-examples control (Cliff's delta 0.31 disclosed and 0.26 undisclosed, adjusted p = 0.001; p. 7, Section 5.2; Table 12, p. 22).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: None for the S1 participants. The S1 seed examples and the S2 human ideas come from the Organisciak et al. AUT dataset; those people are not S1 participants.
- `with_ai`, Individual creativity: predicted originality of the submitted idea (automated_score, unit output): conditionHiExposure_Undisclosed 0.050 (0.113), t = 0.443; Observations 3,271 [p. 26, Table 18]
- `across_people`, Global idea diversity: median pairwise distance among submitted ideas in a condition (embedding_similarity, unit set): Table 12 (median): Diff in Means 1.21, Adj P Value 0.001, Cliff's Delta 0.31 (text: delta = 0.31; p = 0.001). Table 11 (mean pairwise): 1.34, 0.001, 0.35. Table 13 (centroid): 1.48, 0.001, 0.35. [p. 22, Table 12; p. 7, Section 5.2 and Figure 7; p. 21, Table 11; p. 22, Table 13]
- Needs authors: Text on p. 8 credits [Low Exposure, Undisclosed] with higher global diversity than Control, but Table 12 (p. 22) and Figure 7 (p. 7) put the gain on Low Exposure, Disclosed. The form follows the table.
- Needs authors: The abstract says there were no main effects of disclosure, but Table 12 (p. 22) shows more global diversity in both Disclosed conditions than in the matching Undisclosed ones.
- Needs authors: Per-arm counts of people are not reported, so n_treatment and n_control cannot be filled for any contrast. Criterion 2 counts all 844 people analyzed and does not need them. The document names no posted data.
- Needs authors: The gap between 3,414 analyzed responses and the 3,271 (creativity, local diversity) and 2,618 (AI adoption) model observations is unexplained, and 11 participants lack AI feeling. The models still hold at least 655 people, so criterion 2 holds either way.
- Needs authors: No participant-level standard error for the diversity outcomes (Monte Carlo set samples with permutation tests for global diversity, 362 pool-level rows for its evolution). This limits pooling, not eligibility. The document names no posted data.
- Needs authors: Disclosure: p. 4 says participants are not told ideas are from AI, but the landing page (p. 5, Figure 3) told everyone that some ideas came from ChatGPT. The disclosure factor varies only the per-idea labels, which bears on the ai_design disclosure contrasts.
- Needs authors: Carryover: unless Control comes first, a participant's Control trial follows AI trials on other items, and the paper reports no first-trial (between-person) contrast. A within-person design with randomized order meets criterion 1.
- Needs journal version: The text ends mid-sentence in SI 11.3 (p. 29). The ACM version of record may complete it.

**Asirvatham et al. (2026)** [c191], "Training Novices to Think, or Giving Them LLMs? Evidence from an RCT". CEPR Discussion Papers. https://cepr.org/publications/DP21882

- Version read: Report dated August 2026 on the title page, 49 PDF pages. The retrieval record identifies this as the OpenAI-hosted version associated with CEPR DP 21882; the CEPR PDF and any later version were not independently compared. PDF page numbers are one greater than printed page numbers.
- Finding: ChatGPT increased recommendation usefulness by 0.862 points (SE 0.079) and idea count by 2.251 (SE 0.290), with no clear effect on between-person diversity (-0.002, SE 0.002); causal training increased diversity by 0.018 (SE 0.003) (Tables A.10 and A.12, PDF pp. 45-46).
- `with_ai`, Awareness and usage evaluation score (expert_raters, unit participant): Table A.10 OLS, clustered SEs in parentheses. No balance controls: GPT 0.834*** (0.100), Causal -0.101 (0.068), Causal x GPT 0.072 (0.125), constant 2.236*** (0.040). With balance controls: GPT 0.862*** (0.079), Causal -0.111*** (0.040), Causal x GPT 0.082 (0.084), constant 2.089*** (0.230). N=1053 in each model. Constants are regression intercepts, not reported adjusted arm means. Full-sample outcome mean 2.635, SD 0.843 (Table A.7). *** p<0.01. The GPT coefficient compares GPT with Control; the interaction is not the Causal&GPT-versus-Causal effect. [PDF p. 45 [printed p. 44], Table A.10 cols. 1-2 and note; PDF p. 42 [printed p. 41], Table A.7; PDF p. 8, Section 4.2.]
- `across_people`, Between-solution diversity of ideas (embedding_similarity, unit participant): OLS with balance controls; standard errors clustered by mother tongue x high-school type x degree class. Table A.12 col. 3: GPT -0.002 (SE 0.002), Causal 0.018*** (0.003), Causal x GPT 0.002 (0.004), constant 0.597*** (0.010); N=1053; 50 clusters. *** p<0.01. The GPT and interaction terms are not statistically distinguishable from zero according to Section 4.3; this does not establish equivalence or zero effect. [PDF p. 46 [printed p. 45], Table A.12 col. 3 and note; PDF p. 11 [printed p. 10], Figure 5 and Section 4.3.]
- Needs authors: Country of the participant sample is not explicitly stated; keep participants.country null. Author confirmation of the trial location would settle open_questions[0] and the unresolved reconciliation difference studies.S1.participants.country. The main text withholds the institution, while the Appendix names Bocconi registrar records and the case names Milan (PDF pp. 3, 30-31, 36); country is not an eligibility requirement.
- Needs posted data: The Causal&GPT-versus-Causal usefulness estimate, SE, and test are not printed separately (Table A.10, PDF p. 45). Participant-level data with assignment and model covariates, or the fitted model covariance, would permit that contrast. This covers the usefulness part of open_questions[1] and unresolved studies.S1.outcomes.S1-O1.contrasts.Causal&GPT_vs_Causal; the printed GPT-versus-Control contrast already supports an eligible comparison.
- Needs posted data: The Causal&GPT-versus-Causal idea-count estimate, SE, and test are not printed separately (Table A.12 column 1, PDF p. 46). Participant-level data with assignment and model covariates, or the fitted model covariance, would permit that contrast. This covers the idea-count part of open_questions[1] and unresolved studies.S1.outcomes.S1-O2.contrasts.Causal&GPT_vs_Causal; it does not change the randomized design, sample size, or output scoring.
- Needs posted data: The Causal&GPT-versus-Causal expert-alignment estimates, SEs, and tests are not printed separately for any expert reference (Table A.11, PDF p. 45). Participant-level data with assignment and model covariates, or the fitted model covariances, would permit those contrasts. This covers the expert-alignment part of open_questions[1] and unresolved studies.S1.outcomes.S1-O5.contrasts.Causal&GPT_vs_Causal; the other eligible output measures remain available.
- Needs authors: The AEA registry identifier/link and preregistered outcome priority remain unavailable (open_questions[2]; PDF pp. 3, 17). Authors would need to supply the registry entry or original analysis plan to verify priority beyond the human evaluation identified as the main result (PDF p. 7). Preregistration and outcome priority are not required for these eligibility criteria.
- Needs posted data: Assignment-class clustered precision is not reported (open_questions[3]). The paper randomizes 13 classes but reports SEs clustered by mother tongue, high-school type, and class, with 50 cells (PDF pp. 17, 37, 46-48). Participant-level outcomes, assignment, class identifiers, and model covariates would support re-estimation at the randomized class level. This affects suitability for pooling under codebook Section 10, not screening eligibility.
- Needs posted data: Arm-level outcome SDs are not reported; Table A.7 provides only full-sample outcome SDs (open_questions[4]; PDF p. 42). Participant-level outcomes and arm membership would settle the missing arm-level summaries. This concerns effect-size extraction and standardization, not eligibility.
- Needs posted data: Within-arm between-person semantic diversity is not reported (open_questions[4]). The recorded measure compares each participant with the full pooled sample (PDF p. 42; Table A.12 note, PDF p. 46). Participant texts or extracted ideas/embeddings, arm membership, and analysis code would permit a within-arm calculation. The existing pooled-reference measure already scores diversity across participants and satisfies criterion 3.
- Needs authors: The exact averaging weights across ideas and other responses in the pooled between-person distance measure are unspecified (open_questions[4]; PDF pp. 41-42, 46). An explicit formula or implementation from the authors would settle the weighting. This affects reproducibility and interpretation; the source already establishes that different participants' outputs are compared.
- Needs authors: Case-only duration remains inconsistent (open_questions[5]): the main text describes a 45-minute case, whereas the Appendix gives approximately 45 minutes for the full session (PDF pp. 3, 7, 17). Authors would need to clarify the task-specific time allowance or session protocol. Both descriptions are retained; the discrepancy does not affect randomization, the analyzed sample size, or output scoring.

**Atzori et al. (2026)** [c194], "Human-AI Interaction in Creative Tasks: an Experimental Investigation". Working Papers, Department of Economics, Ca' Foscari University of Venice. https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.6715818

- Version read: Ca' Foscari University of Venice, Department of Economics Working Papers No. 16/WP/2026 (PDF pp. 1-2); Online Appendix dated February 2026 (PDF p. 30), bound into this 54-page PDF. No separate main-text date or saved-version qualification is stated.
- Finding: Among 360 analyzed participants, mostly students, ChatGPT access increased mean rated creativity by 0.398 points (robust SE 0.139; PDF p. 23, Table 5, column 1).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: A separate, unnamed study of Stage 2 competition choices from the same laboratory experiment; its title, identifier, and exact analyzed overlap are not stated.
- `with_ai`, Average creativity score (mean of four Prolific raters' scores of the composition) (human_raters, unit participant): Table 5 column (1), OLS adjusting for Tournament and Piece Rate indicators: AI 0.398** (robust SE 0.139); constant 5.681*** (0.147); N = 360. Table 2 column (1), OLS adjusting for number of characters, male, DAT score, and DAT score squared: AI 0.299* (robust SE 0.131); N = 360. Table 5 supplies the headline estimate because Table 2 adjusts for text length and DAT measured after assignment. The Table 5 constant is a fitted intercept, not an observed No-AI arm mean. Significance thresholds as printed: ** p < 0.01; * p < 0.05; *** p < 0.001. [PDF p. 23 (printed p. 20), Table 5 column (1), coefficient and constant rows, Obs., and notes; PDF p. 16 (printed p. 13), Table 2 column (1) and notes; DAT timing PDF p. 8.]
- `after_ai`, Divergent Association Task (DAT) score, post-experiment questionnaire (automated_score, unit participant): Table 5 column (3), OLS adjusting for Tournament and Piece Rate indicators: AI -0.064 (robust SE 0.577); constant 83.760*** (0.583); N = 360. No significance marker accompanies AI; *** p < 0.001 applies to the constant. Table 1 cell means (SD, n): T 83.705 (6.323, 52); PR 82.582 (5.231, 47); L 84.233 (5.198, 49); T&AI 83.518 (4.834, 88); PR&AI 83.535 (5.070, 62); L&AI 83.323 (4.832, 62). [PDF p. 23 (printed p. 20), Table 5 column (3), AI, Constant, Obs., and notes; PDF p. 14 (printed p. 11), Table 1, DAT score and Obs. rows; questionnaire timing PDF p. 8; AI removal PDF p. 31.]
- Needs authors: Assignment-stage and per-arm randomized counts, attrition, and unequal analyzed arm sizes (open_questions[0]; unresolved reconciliation.differences on studies[0].participants.n_randomized): authors would need to supply assignment and participant-flow records. Criterion 2 is already met by 360 analyzed people (PDF pp. 14, 16, 23).
- Needs authors: Individual versus session assignment and required SE clustering (open_questions[1]): authors would need to supply the treatment-allocation protocol and session identifiers. Criterion 1 is met by explicit random assignment to AI access (PDF pp. 3, 8); the missing implementation detail affects precision assessment.
- Needs authors: Actual rating-scale minimum (open_questions[2]): main text says 1 to 10, while instructions say 0 to 10 (PDF pp. 9, 32, 34). Authors would need to confirm the administered rating instrument. Either scale supplies output-based creativity scores, so criterion 3 remains met.
- Needs authors: Rater payment currency (open_questions[3]; unresolved reconciliation.differences on open_questions.rater_payment_currency): authors would need to confirm the actual payment, given 3 Euro in the main text and 3 British pounds in the instructions (PDF pp. 9, 34). This does not affect any eligibility criterion.
- Needs posted data: Additional treatment-effect statistics outside the saved PDF (open_questions[4]; unresolved reconciliation.differences on source.supplement.holds.external_completeness): inventory and inspect the linked replication data and code at https://doi.org/10.6084/m9.figshare.32132980 (PDF p. 3). All eligibility criteria are already supported by the saved PDF; this concerns extraction completeness.
- Needs authors: Identity and exact analyzed overlap of the separate Stage 2 competition-choice study (open_questions[5]; PDF p. 8): authors would need to identify that paper and its analyzed participant set. This concerns sample deduplication, not any eligibility criterion for S1.

**Baltà-Salvador et al. (2025)** [c201], "Empowering Engineering Students Through Artificial Intelligence (AI): Blended Human–AI Creative Ideation Processes With ChatGPT". Computer Applications in Engineering Education. https://doi.org/10.1002/cae.22817

- Version read: Journal version of record: Computer Applications in Engineering Education, 2025; 33:e22817; DOI 10.1002/cae.22817. Received 9 July 2024, revised 12 December 2024, accepted 17 December 2024 (p. 1).
- Finding: Among 51 analyzed students who produced 728 ideas, ChatGPT assistance showed no significant peer-rated differences, while an observational prior-experience comparison found higher overall scores for experienced users (3.00 [SD 0.82] versus 2.75 [SD 0.94], p = 0.039; p. 16, Section 7; p. 7, Table 3; p. 9, Section 5; p. 11, Figure 7 and Table 4).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Possible shared sample with c031, Evaluating AI-assisted creative ideation: A crossover study in higher education (DOI 10.1016/j.tsc.2025.101958). Both reports describe 51 third-year UPC industrial design and product development engineering students, the same two tasks and opposite assistance orders, and the same idea counts: unaided T1 = 158 and T2 = 202; assisted T1 = 195 and T2 = 173; total = 728. These source matches strongly suggest overlap, but do not establish participant identity. Author confirmation is needed before treating the papers as independent samples; c031 participant counts and statistics are not substituted for c201 values.
- `with_ai`, Creativity (human_raters, unit output): The paper reports no significant differences in peer evaluations between ideas generated with and without ChatGPT (Mann-Whitney test). Figure 7 shows T1, T2 and total bars, but no exact numerical condition means, SDs, test statistics, p-values, or error-bar values are printed for this contrast. The text describes slightly higher scores with ChatGPT in T1 and higher scores without ChatGPT in T2 (p. 9, section 5). No SEs are supplied, and the Figure 7 error bars are undefined; the pooled comparison combines pre-exposure and post-exposure unaided controls (p. 7, Figure 1). [p. 9, sections 4.4 and 5; p. 11, Figure 7; p. 14, section 6, Research question 2; p. 7, Figure 1 for crossover timing]
- Needs authors: Allocation method, number randomized and per-sequence participant counts are unreported (open_questions[0]; pp. 4 and 6-7). Authors could provide the allocation protocol and participant flow. This does not unsettle criterion 1, because random assignment is explicit, or criterion 2, because p. 16 links the analyzed results to 51 students.
- Needs authors: Exact assistance-versus-unaided means, SDs, tests, p-values and the definition of Figure 7 error bars are unavailable (open_questions[1]; pp. 9 and 11). Authors could supply the numerical results and underlying data; p. 17 offers data and materials upon reasonable request. This affects effect extraction, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Table 4 lists 80 and 280 ideas for the prior-experience subgroups, whereas Table 3 reports 368 assisted ideas; subgroup participant counts and the reason for the discrepancy are unreported (open_questions[2]; pp. 7 and 11). Authors could reconcile the analysis rows and provide subgroup participant counts. This concerns an observational subgroup analysis and does not contradict the 51-person analyzed cohort used for criterion 2.
- Needs authors: Participant clustering, paired/crossover adjustment and participant-level uncertainty are not reported for the idea-evaluation comparisons (open_questions[3]; p. 9, section 4.4; p. 11, Table 4). Authors could clarify the analysis and provide linked participant, task and rating data or appropriately adjusted estimates. This affects pooling and precision, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Washout and carryover handling are unreported, and the T2 unaided condition follows prior ChatGPT use (open_questions[4]; pp. 6-7, section 4.3.1 and Figure 1). Authors could clarify timing and carryover procedures and supply results by task and randomized sequence. The reported design has no separate comparison of unassisted exposed and unexposed groups, so no isolated after_ai effect is recorded; randomized assistance order still meets criterion 1.
- Needs authors: Possible shared sample with c031 remains unconfirmed despite matching cohort, procedure and task-by-condition idea counts (open_questions[5]; c201 pp. 4 and 6-7; c031 pp. 4-5, including Figure 1). Authors could confirm whether the same students and outputs were reused and specify the overlap. This affects sample deduplication, not the three within-study eligibility criteria.

**Baltà-Salvador, Brasó-Vives, and Peña (2026)** [c031], "Evaluating AI-assisted creative ideation: A crossover study in higher education". Thinking Skills and Creativity. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tsc.2025.101958

- Version read: Journal version of record: Thinking Skills and Creativity 59 (2026) 101958, doi 10.1016/j.tsc.2025.101958; received 27 April 2025, revised 14 August 2025, accepted 16 August 2025, available online 18 August 2025 (p. 1). Open access under CC BY-NC-ND.
- Finding: Among 51 analyzed students, pooled within-person comparisons found more Existing Solutions with ChatGPT (1.00 vs 0.55, p = .011) and shorter descriptions (23.36 vs 25.57 words, p = .015), with no statistically significant differences in fluency, flexibility, or originality (p. 8, Table 4); thematic diversity was comparable (p. 10, Table 7 and Section 4.3), while semantic divergence was printed as higher with AI (0.29 vs 0.28) despite the narrative describing it as lower (p. 10, Section 4.3).
- `with_ai`, Fluency (number of ideas generated per participant) (category_count, unit participant): Unaided 7.06 (1.54) vs AI-assisted 7.22 (1.08); n = 51 per condition (N = 102); Wilcoxon signed-rank z = 0.77, p = .439, r = .11 [p. 8, Table 4]
- `across_people`, Group-level semantic divergence (cosine distance of each idea to its condition's centroid, LaBSE) (embedding_similarity, unit output): Divergence to centroid: unaided M = 0.28 (SD = 0.06) vs AI-assisted M = 0.29 (SD = 0.07); independent samples t(728) = -3.54, p < .001, d = -0.26, 95 % CI [-0.03, -0.01]. Direction follows the printed means and Fig. 3(a) (higher divergence, so more spread, with AI); the text reads the result as AI-assisted ideas being 'modestly more semantically similar to the group centroid'. [p. 10, Section 4.3; p. 11, Fig. 3(a); idea counts p. 10 (N = 368, N = 360) and p. 5, Fig. 1]
- `after_ai`, Flexibility on unaided work after AI use (category_count, unit participant): Unaided means: Group B Task 2 6.33 (SD = 1.30) (n = 27 analyzed) vs Group A Task 1 4.58 (SD = 1.81) (n = 24 analyzed). Different tasks and periods; no direct test, p value, or interval for this pair is reported. The direction records the higher printed mean after prior AI use; it is descriptive. [p. 9, Table 5 (Task 2 Group B and Task 1 Group A rows and participant-count note); task sequence p. 5, Section 3.2.1]
- Needs authors: S1-O7/S1-O8: semantic-divergence direction conflicts between printed means/Fig. 3 and narrative (pp. 10-11, 13); authors must correct the labels or interpretation, or supply the underlying data/code. This affects effect extraction, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: S1-O7/S1-O8: semantic-test degrees of freedom and independent units are unclear (pp. 7, 10); authors must identify the actual analysis units and treatment of repeated observations. Other participant-level creativity outcomes establish eligibility.
- Needs authors: S1-O7: pairwise-within-person versus centroid semantic-divergence definition conflicts (pp. 7, 10-11); authors or their analysis code must identify the implemented measure. This affects construct and precision coding, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: S1-O6 and the unresolved reconciliation difference: the image-elaboration scorer is unidentified (pp. 5-7). Authors must identify who or what scored the drawing rubric; measure is null. The outcome is clearly scored on drawings, so scorer identity does not affect criterion 3.
- Needs authors: Possible shared participants with c201 / DOI 10.1002/cae.22817 (c031 pp. 3, 13, 15): authors must confirm participant overlap. This affects sample deduplication, not whether c031 analyzes at least 50 people or meets the other criteria.
- Needs authors: S1-O10: the LV2 or LV3 level used for category size is unspecified (pp. 7, 10); authors or their analysis code must identify it. This affects measure detail, not output-based outcome eligibility.
- Needs authors: S1-O5: the word-counting method is unspecified (p. 7, Table 2); authors could identify it. The unsupported measure code has been replaced with null; any codebook convention for plain word counts is a separate coding decision. Output-based eligibility is unaffected.

**Bangerl et al. (2025)** [c032], "CreAItive Collaboration? Users' Misjudgment of AI-Creativity Affects Their Collaborative Performance". Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI 2025), ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3713886

- Version read: Published proceedings version of record: CHI '25 (CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems, April 26-May 01, 2025, Yokohama, Japan), ACM, 17 pages, doi:10.1145/3706598.3713886, CC BY 4.0. Received 12 September 2024, revised 10 December 2024, accepted 16 January 2025 (p. 17).
- Finding: The paper reports no significant main-test differences in AUT fluency (14.6 vs 12.7), originality (3.7 vs 3.7), or flexibility (10.0 vs 9.4), but lower AI-group elaboration immediately (2.1 vs 3.8; U = 924.0, p = 0.005) and after AI removal (2.8 vs 4.7; U = 970.0, p = 0.012), with no significant retention difference three weeks later (1.9 vs 2.2), although the elaboration tests have unresolved analysis units (pp. 8-10, Sections 5.1.1-5.1.3 and Figures 4, 8, 12).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: S1 shares no participants with another paper as far as the document states. S2 summarizes the pre-study published separately as Bangerl, Stefan, and Pammer-Schindler (2024), 'Explorations in human vs. Generative AI creative performances: A study on human-AI creative potential', CHI TREW 2024 workshop paper (reference [7]).
- `with_ai`, AUT flexibility, main test (with AI) (category_count, unit team): Figure 4 mean values (nAI = 26 dyads, nCONTROL = 26 dyads): AI group 10.0 vs control group 9.4. Text: 'no significant differences, but a slightly higher score mean in the experimental AI group'. No test statistic printed. Top quartile: AI mean = 14.90 vs control mean = 12.72 (difference > 1sd, sd = 1.90). H1 rejected. [p. 8, Figure 4 and Section 5.1.1; p. 9, Figure 6 (box plot)]
- `after_ai`, AUT flexibility, unaided immediate post-test (AI removed) (category_count, unit team): Figure 8 mean values (nAI = 26 dyads, nCONTROL = 26 dyads): AI group 11.2 vs control group 10.7. Text: 'Originality and flexibility results showed neither significant nor other notable group differences.' No test statistic printed. H2 rejected. [p. 9, Figure 8 and Section 5.1.2; p. 10, Figure 10 (box plot)]

- Needs authors: S1 retention count, attrition, and individual-versus-dyad analysis need confirmation; main-test eligibility is already established.
- Needs authors: S1 numbers randomized and excluded under the language rule need participant-flow records; 104 analyzed people and stated random assignment already meet criteria 1 and 2.
- Needs supplement: S1 missing AUT standard deviations and test statistics require inspection of the supplement; its coverage is unknown, and authors may still be needed. This affects effect extraction only.
- Needs supplement: S1 coder identities and reliability require inspection of the supplement scoring description; its coverage is unknown. The paper already confirms output scoring.
- Needs authors: S1 retention AI-use prevention/checks and interim AI-use questionnaire results require author clarification; this affects the after_ai interpretation, not main-test eligibility.
- Needs authors: S2 direct people count, population, and country require the original pre-study from its authors; only 20 dyads are directly reported in c032. S2 does not determine core eligibility.
- Needs authors: S2 observation unit and proposed 30 producer-score observations require the original pre-study or author-provided analysis details; the c032 summary does not settle them.

- Finding: Among 81 analyzed writers, post-edited AI drafts were less stylistically diverse than unassisted writing (similarity Hedges’ g = 1.42, 95% CI [1.33, 1.51], p = .0002; sample: p. 13, Appendix A.1; result: p. 6, section 6.2 and p. 16, Table 3, H2a).

CISR robustness: p = 0.0002, g = 1.03, 95% CI [0.91, 1.14]. Positive printed g denotes higher similarity (lower diversity) in post-edited text. Direction is coded for diversity; the printed effect has not been re-signed. [p. 6, section 6.2; p. 7, Figure 6; p. 16, Table 3, H2a]
- `with_ai`, Stylistic similarity to the author’s own unassisted writing (H1a) (embedding_similarity, unit output): H1a, LUAR-MUD: p = 0.0002, g = 0.55, 95% CI [0.38, 0.71]. (2026), Investigating student engagement with personalised generative AI feedback: Effects on creative problem-solving and feedback uptake, Australasian Journal of Educational Technology 42(4), 165-185, doi:10.14742/ajet.11512 (local PDF: C:/Dropbox/Research/ai-lab/literature/creativity/Chen et al (2026) - Investigating student engagement with personalised generative AI feedback.pdf). - Finding: Among 62 analyzed students, personalized GPT-4 feedback produced higher rated quality (3.50 vs 3.08, p = .001, r = .42) and elaboration (3.58 vs 2.48, p < .001, r = .62) than standard ChatGPT-4, with no significant originality difference (2.92 vs 3.13, p = .098, r = .18), using the values printed in c041 (PDF p. 8, printed p. 383, Sect. 4.1; Fig. 4A).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: c246: Chen et al. (2026), Investigating student engagement with personalised generative AI feedback: Effects on creative problem-solving and feedback uptake, Australasian Journal of Educational Technology 42(4), 165-185, doi:10.14742/ajet.11512. Treat as one shared sample for deduplication, not two independent experiments. This is an inference from source matching: both report 62 students, matching sex composition and rounded age statistics, the same two AI tools and product tasks, 31 students per sequence, identical condition-specific task times and rating-reliability values, and identical means, SDs, and p-values for all three creativity outcomes. Neither report explicitly cross-identifies or cites the other. Use one sample_id and only one primary record per sample, question, and comparison type; retain each report's statistics with its own provenance.
- `with_ai`, Creative performance: quality (human_raters, unit participant): Personalized M = 3.50, SD = 1.00 vs standard M = 3.08, SD = 1.12; Wilcoxon signed-rank p = .001; r = .42. No Z, SE, or CI printed; no n printed with the result (62 students did both tasks, Abstract and Sect. 3.1). [PDF p. 8 (printed p. 383), Sect. 4.1; PDF p. 9 (printed p. 384), Fig. 4A]
- Needs authors: Original question 3, aggregation of the three solutions into each participant's creativity score: still unspecified in both reports. Author clarification of scoring and the analysis unit would settle extraction/precision; it does not change the randomized design, 62-person sample, or output scoring.
- Needs authors: Original question 5, originality r/p consistency, expanded to the between-report elaboration and quality r discrepancies: c246 Table 2 supplies additional z/r/CI values but does not explicitly correct c041 or explain the differences. Authors' calculations and effect-size definition would settle which statistics to use for extraction and paired precision. The three eligibility criteria do not depend on the magnitude or significance of these effects.
- Needs authors: Original question 6, Fig. 4B error bars: the c041 caption says SD, while the self-efficacy bars look inconsistent with the SDs printed in Sect. 4.1. Authors would need to identify the plotted uncertainty. This concerns an excluded self-report outcome and affects no eligibility criterion.

- Finding: With 204 people analyzed per experiment, an LLM partner posting ERNIE Bot ideas raised expert-rated creativity over a scripted human partner on the simple task (2.549 vs 2.182, F(1,200) = 17.152, P < 0.001) and scored lower on the complex task (2.351 vs 2.441, F(1,200) = 1.021, printed with P = 0.028), while two ideas per response instead of ten raised complex-task creativity (2.520 vs 2.139) and lowered simple-task creativity (2.171 vs 2.466) (pp. 5 and 7).
- `with_ai`, Expert-rated creativity of the participant's ideas (overall novelty and usefulness) (expert_raters, unit participant): One-way ANOVA: F(1,202) = 4.638, P = 0.032, partial eta^2 = 0.022; M_human = 2.311, s.d. = 0.469 vs M_LLM = 2.45, s.d. = 0.453; t(202) = -2.154; P = 0.032; d = -0.301; 95% CI (-0.266, -0.012). Arm ns not printed (total N = 204). The paper does not identify the quantity covered by this CI; it does not contain the printed d, so its interpretation requires clarification. 2 x 2 ANOVA: partner main effect F(1,200) = 4.901, P = 0.028, partial eta^2 = 0.024; task complexity F(1,200) = 0.232, P = 0.631, partial eta^2 = 0.001; interaction F(1,200) = 13.272, P < 0.001, partial eta^2 = 0.062. [p. 5, Results, Experiment 1, Self-reported creativity (second paragraph) and Creativity]

- Needs authors: Open question 4: Experiment 2 fixation moderated-mediation index and interval conflict with the prose; the correct values need author clarification. Eligibility does not depend on this mediator result.

- Needs authors: Open question 6: The reference idea set for fixation scoring in human-partner arms needs author clarification. Expert-rated creativity independently satisfies the output criterion.

- Finding: Among 276 analyzed Prolific ideators (428 refinements), textual AI concretizations raised automated idea creativity by 0.29 SD over visual AI concretizations (Model 1a, SE 0.11) and by a reported, statistically significant 30% over the no-AI control (no coefficient printed), while visual versus control was 0.13 SD (SE 0.10, not significant) (PDF p. 9, article p. 8, Section 4.2 for the sample; PDF p. 10, article p. 9, Table 2 and Section 5).
- `with_ai`, Idea creativity (mean of novelty and usefulness, z-standardized at the iteration level) (automated_score, unit output): Backward-difference contrast ‘Textual Concretization’ (textual vs visual): Model (1a) 0.29** (0.11); Model (1b, controls) 0.23* (0.10); Model (1c, maturity interactions) 0.20* (0.08). N = 428 refinements; clustered robust SEs; * p ≤ 0.05, ** p ≤ 0.01. Text: β = 0.29, p ≤ 0.01, ‘an improvement in creativity of 18%’. Endnote 6: 0.29 SDs; adjusted predictions 0.06 SDs below the mean (51st percentile) with visual vs 0.23 SDs above the mean (60th percentile) with textual, ‘approximately 18% ((60 − 51)/51)’. Textual Concretization × Idea Maturity 0.24** (0.09) in Model (1c). Bootstrap robustness analysis (Online Appendix E1, summarized in Section 5): for ideas more than one SD below mean maturity, visual concretizations significantly outperformed textual concretizations in creativity without increasing effort; above this threshold, textual concretizations produced more creative ideas with greater effort. This is a reversal of the main-effect direction for very immature ideas; no numerical bootstrap effect, SE, or p-value is printed in the main text. [PDF p. 10 (article p. 9), Table 2 and Section 5; PDF p. 14 (article p. 13), endnote 6; PDF p. 11 (article p. 10), Figure 4 and Section 5, first robustness check; PDF p. 12 (article p. 11), Section 6, first paragraph]

- Needs authors: The cluster identifier for the reported clustered robust SEs is not explicitly named. Authors could confirm the clustering specification; this determines pooling suitability, not random assignment, analyzed sample size, or output scoring.

- Needs supplement: S2 sample size, recruitment and comparison procedures, scoring method, analysis unit, and numerical statistics need Online Appendix E2. These are gaps in coding the separate-sample context comparison; S1 independently meets all three criteria, so paper eligibility is unaffected.

- `with_ai`, Task quality: overall expert grade, with originality-only robustness measure (expert_raters, unit output): Task quality: unequal access M = 5.654, SD = 0.080; full access M = 5.250, SD = 0.118; t = 2.881, p < 0.01 (p. 8, Section 4.1). Table 1, Model (1), prints Full access to AI b = −0.4023**, adjacent column headed SD = −2.2771; N = 120, adj. R² = 0.170. Its footnote calls the adjacent numbers t statistics and ** means p < 0.05; no usable SE for this total comparison is explicitly supplied (p. 9). Indirect effect via cognitive diversity: b = −0.283, SE = 0.091, 95% CI [−0.513, −0.134]; serial indirect effect via negative socio-emotional reactions and cognitive diversity: b = −0.061, SE = 0.029, 95% CI [−0.148, −0.021] (p. 9, Sections 4.2-4.3.1; p. 10, Table 2). Originality-only robustness: serial indirect effect b = −0.105, SE = 0.050, 95% CI [−0.248, −0.036]; cognitive diversity-to-originality path b = 4.619, SE = 1.346, p < 0.01 (p. 11, Section 4.4.2). No arm means or total/direct AI contrast for originality are printed. Regression signs are copied unchanged: unequal access is the reference group, so negative full-access coefficients favor unequal access. SE/CI availability here refers to mediation results, not the main total contrast. Table 3 condition counts of 40 and 20 are baseline teams, not arm-level people counts or newly established AI-phase observation counts (p. 11; p. 10, Section 4.4.1). [p. 8, Section 4.1; p. 9, Figure 4A, Table 1 Model (1), and Sections 4.2-4.3.1; p. 10, Table 2; pp. 10-11, Section 4.4.2; p. 12, Figure 6; p. 6, Section 3.4.1 (reference coding); p. 11, Table 3 (arm N values are teams).]
- `across_people`, Cognitive diversity between the two teammates’ spoken contributions (embedding_similarity, unit team): Unequal access M = 0.631, SD = 0.011; full access M = 0.510, SD = 0.018; t = 5.938, p < 0.01. Mediation-model direct path for full access relative to the unequal-access reference: b = −0.107, SE = 0.025, p < 0.01 (p. 9, Section 4.2). SD labels are copied from the prose; the Figure 4 caption describes its error bars as ±1 standard error of the mean. Larger cosine distance means greater diversity, and the negative coefficient is full minus unequal access, not a negative effect of unequal access. The mediation model prints N = 60 (p. 8, Section 4); Table 3 reports 40 and 20 baseline teams (p. 11). Neither establishes independent person-level precision for these estimates. **Hosanagar and Ahn (2025)** [c793], "Designing Human and Generative AI Collaboration". arXiv. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2412.14199

- Version read: Undated manuscript headed 'Main Manuscript for', followed by Supporting Information. No publication date, journal citation, or arXiv version number is printed in the document; the filename's year is not treated as a confirmed manuscript date (PDF pp. 1, 17).
- Finding: Human Creativity and Copilot exceeded Human Confirmation in adjusted overall story quality by 0.3376 (SE 0.106) and 0.3158 (SE 0.105), respectively (both p < 0.01; PDF p. 51, Table S7), while semantic similarity was 0.710, 0.743, and 0.779, respectively (PDF p. 7).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No overlap with another paper is stated. S1's writing days use the same cohort; S2 reuses its baseline DAT scores, with no new human recruitment. Do not add S2's count to S1 or treat them as independent human samples.
- `with_ai`, Overall story quality (human_raters, unit rating): The main text states that overall story quality did not differ significantly between Human Creativity and Copilot on Day 2. No exact pairwise estimate, standard error, or p-value for this contrast is printed; it has not been calculated by subtracting the coefficients against Human Confirmation. [PDF p. 5, Writing Quality; PDF p. 51, Table S7.]
- `across_people`, Semantic similarity between different writers' stories (embedding_similarity, unit output): Human Creativity: mean = 0.710, 95% CI = [0.700, 0.720]; Copilot: mean = 0.743, 95% CI = [0.731, 0.755]. These are raw similarity means, not diversity scores. Direction is reversed to express greater or lesser diversity. The text describes the differences as statistically significant without printing a test statistic, p-value, or confidence interval for a between-condition difference. [PDF pp. 6-7, Content Diversity; PDF pp. 15-16, Figure 4C and caption.]
- Needs authors: open_questions[0]: Exact randomized and analyzed people by arm and regression, and the flow connecting the 285-person cohort, 280 initial-survey records, and 278/271 daily stories, remain unreported (PDF pp. 11, 18-21). Author-provided assignment and analysis flow would settle these counts. This does not leave criterion 2 unclear: the one-story-per-student Day 2 protocol and inclusion of all 271 Day 2 stories already establish at least 50 analyzed writers.
- Needs posted data: open_questions[1]: Tables S4/S7 and A.3 do not state whether standard errors account for repeated ratings of a story/participant or shared evaluators (PDF pp. 23, 48, 51). The posted analysis code and linked participant/story/evaluator data would identify the variance method and allow an independent-unit analysis. This affects use of precision in pooling, not any core eligibility criterion.
- Needs posted data: open_questions[2]: Confidence-interval and significance-test procedures for semantic and genre similarity, including dependence from each story's comparison with the other stories in its condition, are unstated (PDF pp. 6-7, 15-16). Posted analysis code would settle the procedures. This affects statistical interpretation and pooling, not the output-scored outcome criterion or other eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: open_questions[3]: The main text, recruitment section, and Figure S1 specify 105 minutes, whereas the day-specific SI task descriptions specify 75 minutes (PDF pp. 4, 18-20). Author confirmation of the implemented protocol would settle the discrepancy. Task duration does not affect any eligibility criterion.
- Needs authors: open_questions[4]: The saved manuscript has no printed date or arXiv/journal version identifier (PDF pp. 1, 17); its filename and file-creation metadata do not establish a publication version. Author identification of this exact manuscript revision would settle provenance. The missing version identifier does not affect the three eligibility criteria for the document screened.
- Needs posted data: Unresolved reconciliation difference studies[S1].participants.n_analyzed: the exact unique analyzed-person total across both days remains null because 285 is a participating-cohort count and observation counts do not provide a deduplicated writer total (PDF pp. 11, 18, 21). Participant identifiers and outcome inclusion in the posted data would settle the exact total. This overlaps open_questions[0] and does not affect criterion 2, whose threshold is established by the Day 2 protocol and included stories.

**Huang et al. (2026)** [c067], "Human–GenAI collaboration across creative phases: Cognitive mechanisms shaping novelty and usefulness". International Journal of Information Management. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.ijinfomgt.2025.102986

- Version read: Journal version of record: International Journal of Information Management 86 (2026) 102986; received 5 November 2024, revised 16 July 2025, accepted 27 September 2025, available online 3 October 2025 (p. 1).
- Finding: Among 198 employees analyzed in Study 3, expert-rated novelty favors ChatGPT collaboration during idea generation and usefulness favors collaboration during elaboration (Table 3, p. 8: phase dummy 1 = elaboration, r = −0.30 and 0.42, respectively); the smaller Studies 2a and 2b report higher GenAI-condition novelty (6.05 vs 5.44; 42 analyzed; Table 1, p. 6) and usefulness (5.89 vs 5.49; 44 analyzed; Table 2, p. 7).
- `with_ai`, Novelty of the proposal (expert-rated) (expert_raters, unit participant): Table 1 (N = 42): Novelty (GenAI condition) M = 6.05, SD = 0.59; Novelty (H condition) M = 5.44, SD = 0.55; correlation between the two conditions r = 0.14. No test of the total difference is printed. Indirect effect of collaboration in the idea generation phase on novelty through cognitive flexibility: estimate = 0.17, 95% CI = [0.033, 0.341] (5000 bootstraps); the CI is for the indirect effect only. [p. 6, Table 1 and Section 5.1.3]
- Needs authors: Original open question 2 and unresolved reconciliation difference for studies[3].ai_tool.how_used: whether ChatGPT was blocked in Study 3's non-assigned phase remains unknown (p. 7, Section 6.1; p. 14, Appendices G-H). Authors' phase instructions and access rules would settle availability. This concerns implementation detail; the randomized assignment to different collaboration phases is explicit, so criterion 1 remains met.
- Needs authors: Original open question 3: the exact proposal version scored, preliminary or final, is not explicitly identified (pp. 5-7, Sections 5.1.2 and 6.2). Authors can identify the submissions sent to experts. Either is participant-produced output, so criterion 3 is met; the uncertainty concerns timing and AI availability.
- Needs authors: Original open question 5: Study 3 lacks exact arm/cell sizes and arm-level outcome summaries (p. 8, Table 3). Authors' participant-level records or cell summaries would settle these; no data link is printed. Criterion 2 is already met by the explicit 198 people analyzed across all conditions.
- Needs supplement: Original open question 6: preregistered primary outcomes are unconfirmed because the external AsPredicted protocol is absent from the PDF and network retrieval failed (p. 7, Section 6.1). The linked protocol would settle primary-outcome status. The paper's randomization, analyzed sample size, and expert scoring already establish all three criteria.
- Needs authors: Original open question 7: Studies 2a-2b give condition means, SDs, and within-person correlations but no paired total-effect test or standard error (pp. 6-7, Tables 1-2). Authors' analysis outputs would supply these if needed. Both studies are below 50 people and do not determine paper-level eligibility.
- Needs authors: Original open question 8: Study 3 reports no direct novelty/usefulness contrast for the randomized perceived-intelligence factor; Table 3's PGenAII is the measured scale (pp. 7-9). Authors' factorial cell summaries or analysis outputs would settle an additional contrast. This does not affect the eligible phase-comparison evidence or any eligibility criterion.

- Finding: Divergent-stage AI and full-process AI improved human-rated team creativity versus no AI (4.86 and 4.81 vs 4.35; Hedges g=0.81, p=0.00265 and g=0.77, p=0.00424), while convergent-only and warning-framed AI showed no clear improvement (g=0.25, p=0.328 and g=0.43, p=0.104; Supplementary Table 5, PDF p. 31).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: not_stated
- `with_ai`, Human-rated team creativity composite (calibrated) (expert_raters, unit team): C3 mean 4.81 (SD 0.52, 30 teams) vs C4 mean 4.35 (SD 0.67, 29 teams); mean difference 0.465 [0.160, 0.770]; g=0.77 [0.24, 1.30]; p=0.00424 [PDF p. 31 (SI p. 13), Supplementary Table 5; arm SDs and analyzed Ns: PDF p. 27, Supplementary Table 1.]
- Needs authors: Rater identities and expert-only subset (open_questions[0]; unresolved reconciliation difference studies.S1.outcomes.human_rater_identity_and_expert_only_subset). - Needs posted data: Type of parenthetical dispersion in Supplementary Table 15 (open_questions[1]; unresolved reconciliation difference studies.S1.outcomes.S1-O7_and_S1-O8.parenthetical_dispersion; also S1-O10). PDF p. 41 gives descriptive means with unspecified dispersion. The posted table-generation code or documented processed data at https://osf.io/eapdn would settle whether these entries are SDs, SEs or another statistic. Retain the values without an inferred dispersion type. This affects later effect-size extraction, not random assignment, the analyzed people count or whether outputs were scored.
- Needs authors: Initial randomized people counts (open_questions[3]). PDF pp. 21-22, S1.1 gives planned sessions, setup deviations and two removed sessions; Table 2 (PDF p. 28) begins with the analyzed file. The actual pre-exclusion allocation counts, overall and by arm, require author confirmation. Retain n_randomized null. This does not affect criterion 1, whose randomization procedure is explicit, or criterion 2, which is met by the 444 analyzed people documented in Supplementary Table 1 (PDF p. 27).
- Needs authors: Saved-manuscript date and version (open_questions[4]). The complete PDF has no printed date or version number, and retrieval.json has no version_saved designation; PDF p. 23, S1.4 only identifies a corrected condition label. Author identification of the exact archived manuscript date/version would settle this provenance detail. Retain the undated-manuscript description; do not infer a version from candidate-record dates. The saved PDF independently establishes all three eligibility criteria.

[p. 20, Section 4.6; p. 21, Table 13; p. 22, Figure 6, Final grade panel]

- Finding: In the logo experiment (1,857 people analyzed; p. 47, Appendix C.5), DALL-E 3 (Verbatim) improved quality over DALL-E 2 by 0.8075 Bradley–Terry β (95% CI [0.7120, 0.9030], p < 10^−10; p. 18, section 4.2.1), with replay attributing approximately 92.7% to model capability and 7.2% to prompt adaptation (prompting p = 0.101; p. 19, section 4.2.2).

- `with_ai`, Expected image-replication fidelity to the assigned target (embedding_similarity, unit output): Overall ATE: cosine similarity difference = 0.0164, 95% CI [0.0104, 0.0224], p < 10^−5; approximately 0.19 SD (p. 14). CLIP Z-score robustness: z = 0.19, 95% CI [0.100, 0.271] (p. 64). DreamSim robustness: ΔDreamSim = 0.0306, p < 10^−7; z = 0.238, 95% CI [0.152, 0.324] (p. 63). Table 1's quantile model reports Total = 0.022 (SE 0.003), p < 10^−5, and Total × Outcome Quantile (Percentile) = −0.00012 (SE 0.00005), p = 0.015; the un-interacted coefficient is the estimate at the 0th percentile, not the ATE (p. 17). - Version read: Journal version of record: Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans 8 (2026) 100288; received 4 November 2025, revised 2 March 2026, accepted 16 March 2026, available online 18 March 2026 (p. 1). The search record lists no other version.
- Finding: After 8-10 minutes of ChatGPT-4.0 instruction, post-test AUT originality and flexibility on the trained item and flexibility on new items were higher than after human instruction or quiet rest (e.g., novel-item flexibility AI 9.67 ± 3.40, interaction F(2,92) = 7.182, p = 0.001; AI vs human p = 0.002, AI vs control p < 0.001), while no AI-versus-comparator post-test fluency difference is reported (pp. 5-6, Section 3.1; p. 6, Fig. 2).
- `after_ai`, AUT originality, identical item 'books' (creativity acquisition) (human_raters, unit participant): Identical item ('books'): time × instruction type F(2,92) = 6.12, p = 0.003, η2p = 0.12; main effect of instruction type F(2,92) = 3.46, p = 0.036, η2p = 0.12. - Finding: Creative gains after supportive-AI, contrarian-AI, or human-only collaboration showed no significant condition difference (Kruskal-Wallis all p > .05, PDF p. 18; overall OLS F = 1.20, p = .310, R² = .029, PDF p. 19).

Overall OLS model Q(Outcome) ~ C(cluster) + C(condition): F = 1.20, p = .310, R² = .029; creative performance gains were described as not predicted by either cluster or condition. These are omnibus/model results, not a statistic for this pairwise contrast. No pairwise creativity test, arm means or SDs, condition coefficients, numerical SEs, or confidence intervals are printed. Author-provided results or data would permit verification and effect extraction. The PDF supplies only omnibus creativity findings (PDF pp. 18-19, Section 4.4), and data are available on reasonable request rather than publicly posted (PDF p. 23, Data Availability). These missing statistics do not affect randomization, the study-level people count, or whether creativity is scored on output.

Arm levels only in Figure 4a. [p. 7, Section 3.2.1 (Test phase paragraph); p. 7, Figure 4a]
- `with_ai`, Originality, exposure rounds (AI available) (automated_score, unit output): Omnibus Kruskal-Wallis H(2) = 3.78, p = 0.151 ('the mean originality was similar across conditions'); no pairwise test printed. Text: List of Ideas 'performed slightly worse than other conditions'. [p. 6, Section 3.2.1; p. 7, Figure 4a]
- `across_people`, Group-level diversity of ideas, exposure rounds (AI available) (embedding_similarity, unit set): 'In the Exposure rounds, the average median cosine distance between ideas was similar across all conditions (p > 0.05)'. The paper had expected List of Ideas participants to converge and points to incomplete adoption of the AI ideas and to sampling 7 of 20 LLM ideas per object. [p. 8, Section 3.2.4; p. 7, Figure 5b]
