Experiment 2 does not say whether the 1 percent who failed the attention check were dropped, and Experiment 1 mentions no attention check, although both preregistrations (item 6) planned to exclude attention-check failures and non-completers. Criterion 2 holds either way (460 recruited with no exclusion reported; at least 630 analyzed in Experiment 2 from the error df).
- Needs authors: Unit of analysis: the paper names the observation in no Experiment 1 test. Originality (S1-O1, S1-O2) and creative flexibility (S1-O9) are defined per idea and coded output. Exposure-phase fluency and individual diversity (S1-O4, S1-O6) may be participant-rounds (three per person) or participant means and are coded participant. The Experiment 2 ANCOVA error df (1256) exceeds the 640 people recruited and fits one row per test answer (two per person) with no clustering by participant, while the preregistration defines the DV as the number correct out of two per participant. Exposure-round accuracy (S2-O2) has no test, so its unit is a convention. This matters for standard errors (codebook Section 10), not for eligibility.
- Needs authors: Tukey mean difference: the paper does not say whether -10.6 is adjusted for verbal fluency or whether its 95% interval is a simultaneous Tukey interval; this matters for deriving a standard error from the interval.
- Needs authors: No arm means, SDs, or standard errors are printed for any outcome. Arm levels exist only in Figures 4, 5, and 9, and the Figure 4 and Figure 9 captions do not say what their error bars show. Extraction will need digitizing or the authors' data.
- Needs authors: Group-level diversity: the test phase has no printed test; its error bars come from 150 Monte Carlo resamples, not from participants; the paper does not say how many set-level values enter each arm, and its claim that the 7-idea sample 'reflects the number of participants in each condition' is not explained (460 recruited across three arms). The unit is coded set, the nearest code; the codebook's unit list has no code for a resampled 7-idea subset.
- Needs authors: LLM Guidance content: the paper does not report how often the guidance words contained the solution. One of the three Table 2 samples does ('snow' for flake, mobile, cone, p. 18). This bears on reading LLM Guidance as a coach arm and on the LLM Guidance vs LLM Answer contrast.
- Needs authors: Creative flexibility (S1-O9): the paper implies, but does not state, that the Exposure set holds the participant's own exposure-round ideas. The outcome is coded flexibility; novelty (p-creativity) is the alternative reading.
- Needs journal version: Version check: confirm that the ACM CHI '25 version of record matches arXiv v2. arXiv v2 is the latest preprint version, but the ACM file was not compared.

**Lai et al. (2027)** [c158], "Creative or uncreative partner: Comparing humans and AI in collaborative creative tasks". Computers in Human Behavior. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chb.2026.109172

- Version read: PsyArXiv preprint version 2 (ey7u4_v2), posted 2025-12-01, per retrieval.json (file saved as 'Copy of Creative Partner or Uncreative Partner v2.pdf'). The document itself shows no date or version label. Retrieval found no journal version; the search record's URL points to v1 (posted 2025-10-02), which was not compared. A later version exists.
- Finding: With a GPT-4o partner instead of a human partner, AUT originality scored over all pair responses, GPT-4o's included (2,970 responses, 2,066 without GPT-4o's), was higher (Human-Human minus Human-AI B = -0.065, SE 0.007), did not differ once word count, fluency, and item were controlled (B = 0.010, SE 0.008, p = .201), and was lower for the participants' own responses (B = 0.029, SE 0.010, p = .005; Table 2, pp. 9-10).
- `with_ai`, AUT originality, all responses in the pair (including the AI's responses) (automated_score, unit output): Base model (Model 1): Condition (Human-Human) B = -0.065*** (SE 0.007), t = -9.69, p < .001; Role B 0.018*** (0.004), t = 4.22; Condition x Role B -0.021** (0.007), t = -2.87, p = .005; DAT (scaled) 0.021*** (0.002). Reference: Condition = AI-Human, Role = A. N observations 2,970; N groups 139. Predicted values with 95% CIs only in Figure 2a. [pp. 9-10, Table 2 (Base column); p. 10, text; p. 11, Figure 2a]
- `across_people`, Short story diversity (diversity contribution score across stories) (embedding_similarity, unit output): Mann-Whitney U = 2674, p = 0.347 (Human-AI n = 72 pairs, Human-Human n = 68 pairs); Kolmogorov-Smirnov D = 0.217, p = 0.061. Shapiro-Wilk: Human-Human W = 0.856, p < 0.001; Human-AI W = 0.946, p = 0.004. No arm medians, means, or condition-level diversity printed; distributions only in Figure 7a. Direction is coded no_clear_difference only because no level comparison is printed: the contribution scores average zero within each condition by construction, so these tests cannot show which condition's stories are more alike. [p. 7, Data Analysis; p. 15, Diversity; p. 15, Figure 7a]
- `after_ai`, Divergent Association Task score, taken alone after the collaborative tasks (automated_score, unit participant): Humans in the paired (Human-Human) group: average DAT 75.4; humans in the AI group: 76.7. No SD, n, or test printed; the paper does not compare the arms on DAT. [p. 4, Materials and Procedure (timing); p. 5, Verbal Creative Thinking Ability; p. 9, AUT Originality, first paragraph (means)]
- Needs authors: Covariates (open question 2): human-only originality and elaboration are printed only from models that control for fluency and, for originality, word count, both of which differ by condition. An estimate without them is not printed.
- Needs authors: AUT forward-flow typos (open question 3): SE = 0.094 with t = 6.34, an interaction called non-significant at p = .015, and 'figure 3a' for Figure 4a (p. 12).
- Needs supplement: Preregistered status of forward flow and Levene's test (open question 4): coded paper_primary because the paper names them as the tests of hypothesis 2. The SI notes deviations from the registry.
- Needs authors: People in the AUT mixed models and honesty-check removals (open question 8): between 137 and 205 people, 2 groups with only GPT-4o responses, and 68 responses dropped relative to the Levene tests, with no counts printed. Criterion 2 holds under any reading (208 in Table 1 and the story diversity test).
- Needs authors: Story model rows (open question 9): one row per participant, but whether each row's DSI or word count covers the whole story or only that participant's sentences, and whether GPT-4o has rows, is not stated.
- Needs authors: DAT arm comparison (open question 10): only means (76.7 vs 75.4), with no SD, n, or test; the DAT was taken after the collaborative tasks and serves as a covariate in every model.
- Needs supplement: Story diversity level (open question 13): the contribution scores average zero within each condition by construction, and no condition-level diversity is printed, so no across_people difference is available. Criterion 3 holds through other outcomes.
- Needs authors: Allocation mechanism (open question 15): not described, nor what happened when no partner could be matched. The 72 to 136 split of people fits equal pairs per arm, and criterion 1 rests on the stated random assignment.

**Lee and Chung (2024)** [c010], "An empirical investigation of the impact of ChatGPT on creativity". Nature Human Behaviour. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41562-024-01953-1

- Version read: Journal version of record: Nature Human Behaviour, Volume 8, October 2024, pp. 1906-1914; doi 10.1038/s41562-024-01953-1; received 11 September 2023, accepted 10 July 2024, published online 12 August 2024. PDF pp. 10-12 hold the Nature Portfolio Reporting Summary (last updated by authors Jun 13, 2024). The markdown conversion holds the main text, references, back matter and Reporting Summary (md lines 271-523), but it renders Table 1 and Fig. 1 as loose picture text, places the second half of the Experiment 3 Methods before the Experiment 2B Methods, and drops the Reporting Summary check boxes (the ticked n/a box for per-group sample sizes shows only on PDF p. 10).
- Finding: Across six online experiments, lay judges rated ideas produced with ChatGPT more creative than ideas produced with Google search (Cohen's d = 0.32 to 1.01) or with no aid (d = 0.56 in Experiment 2A, 1.88 in Experiment 2B) (Table 1, PDF p. 5, journal p. 1910).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Meincke, Nave, and Terwiesch (2025), 'ChatGPT decreases idea diversity in brainstorming', Nature Human Behaviour 9, 1107-1109 (atlas candidate c015), a Matters Arising that reanalyzes the posted data of every experiment in this paper (its Fig. 1 has panels for Experiments 1, 2a, 2b, 3, 4 and 5); the atlas codebook (section 2) records the shared participants. This PDF names no other paper that uses its samples and reanalyzes no other paper's sample.
- `with_ai`, Creativity score rated by lay judges (human_raters, unit participant): M ChatGPT-assisted = 4.28, s.d. = 0.81 versus M Web-search-assisted = 3.89, s.d. = 0.80; two-sided t-test: t231 = 3.68; P = 2.87 x 10^-4; Cohen's d = 0.49; 95% CI (0.22, 0.75). Arm ns not printed. [PDF p. 2 (journal p. 1907), Results, Experiment 1; PDF p. 5 (journal p. 1910), Table 1]
- Needs posted data: Per-arm and per-cell participant counts are printed for no experiment (open_questions 1)
- Needs posted data: Experiment 2B tests pool variance with the 100 ChatGPT-only responses, and how d was computed is not stated (open_questions 2)
- Needs posted data: Experiment 2B ChatGPT-only s.d. printed as 0.40 and as 0.30 (open_questions 3)
- Needs supplement: Experiment 2B newness-type result has no numbers and no named arms, so it is not coded (open_questions 4)
- Needs posted data: Experiments 4 and 5: the printed 'main effect' equals one simple effect, and no lay-judge contrast pooled over both levels is printed (open_questions 5)
- Needs posted data: Reporting Summary assigns the G*Power analysis to 2A (Methods: 2B) and gives no 2B exclusion count (open_questions 7)
- Needs authors: Experiment 3 judges printed with the participants' mean age and percent female (open_questions 8)
- Needs posted data: Experiment 1 number of ideas: t231 = 1.42 does not match P = 0.149 (open_questions 9)
- Needs authors: Preregistration not stated for Experiments 1 and 3 (open_questions 10)
- Needs supplement: Supplementary Notes E to I are not in this PDF (open_questions 11)
- Needs supplement: Unresolved reconciliation difference: arms of the Experiment 2B newness-type contrast (former S3-O3)

**Li, Zhou, and Mikel-Hong (2024)** [c446], "Generative AI Enhances Team Performance and Reduces Need for Traditional Teams". arXiv. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2405.17924

- Version read: Undated working paper saved from arXiv 2405.17924; the 55-page document prints no version number or date. PDF metadata creation date: 2024-05-28. This metadata date does not establish the arXiv version or whether it is latest; other versions were not compared.
- Finding: AI-assisted teams scored higher on quality (0.417, SE 0.148) and novelty (0.344, SE 0.150), with a weaker usefulness gain (0.250, SE 0.150; p < .10) and no clear advantage from multiple AI touchpoints (pp. 37-39, Appendix B Tables 1-3).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No other paper is identified as sharing participants. Within this paper, Experiment II reuses the 122 Experiment I teams as comparators and analyzes 139 individuals in its individual-AI arm. Whether any of those individuals had participated in Experiment I is not stated; the number of additional unique people across the two experiments is unconfirmed (p. 11, Section 2.2; p. 36, Appendix A Table 3).
- `with_ai`, Overall quality (judge rating, 1-10) (human_raters, unit output): AI Team (Single & Multiple) 0.417*** (0.148), N = 244 team-task outputs; R2 = 0.087 with AI condition versus 0.050 in the corresponding baseline model. Section 3.2.1 reports p < .01 and 3.7% additional variance explained. No mean or SD for the pooled AI arm is printed; separate-arm means, SDs and output counts are recorded in the following contrasts. Coefficient is in rating-scale units, with robust SE in parentheses. [p. 37, Appendix B Table 1, cols. 1-2; pp. 12-13, Section 3.2.1; p. 34, Appendix A Table 1 (separate-arm descriptives); p. 28, Figure 3 (graphical regression display)]
- Needs authors: Exact analyzed headcounts for Experiment I overall and by arm, including its human-only and pooled-AI teams reused in Experiment II, are unreported (pp. 8-11, 34). An explicit participant-flow statement from the authors would settle the three unresolved reconciliation entries on these counts. Criterion 2 is already met by the analyzed outputs from 122 distinct human teams.
- Needs authors: Experiment II occupation/student composition remains unstated (p. 11; pp. 35-36), as recorded in open_questions and the unresolved population reconciliation entry. An author description of the recruitment population would settle the category; population category is not an eligibility criterion.
- Needs supplement: Additional external supplement statistics remain unverified (source.supplement.holds and its unresolved reconciliation entry). Access to the OSF supplement linked on p. 8 would settle its contents; the saved PDF already documents the design and output ratings and includes the main descriptive and regression tables. Both named OSF requests failed with WinError 10013.
- Needs supplement: Preregistered outcome priority is unverified because the OSF registration linked on p. 4 was inaccessible. Access to that supplementary protocol would settle priority; preregistered primary status is not one of the three eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: The multiple-versus-single-AI Bayesian means and credible intervals differ between p. 13 and Appendix B Table 4 (p. 40). Both printed versions are preserved. Author confirmation of the intended estimates would settle the conflict; it concerns results rather than any eligibility criterion.
- Needs authors: The time-controlled AI-team novelty p-value contradicts its significance marker between p. 18 and Appendix B Table 9 (p. 45). Author confirmation of the intended p-value would settle the conflict; significance does not determine eligibility.
- Needs authors: The task time limit and completion-time units/transformation remain unstated (pp. 17-19; p. 43), including the unresolved task.duration reconciliation entry. The authors could supply the task instructions and time-variable definition. These details affect interpretation of time-adjusted analyses, not the three eligibility criteria.
- Needs posted data: Team/participant clustering is not stated for the robust output-level SEs (pp. 37-39, 42, 45, 47-48). Analysis code or output data with team/participant identifiers would permit checking the SE basis or obtaining independent-unit precision. This affects pooling, not core screening eligibility.
- Needs journal version: The saved PDF prints no version number or document date; creation metadata does not establish that it is latest. An identified journal version or dated latest document would settle version provenance and permit comparison. No version-dependent uncertainty about the three criteria is identified in the saved PDF.
- Needs authors: Whether Experiment II individuals had previously participated in Experiment I is unknown (p. 11; p. 36). An explicit author statement about cross-experiment participation would settle overlap. Experiment I independently meets all criteria, and the individual count is not added to its randomized sample.

- Finding: Human-rated adjusted final-draft creativity was lower for AI-first (M = 4.86, d = -0.95, p = .002) and AI-follow (M = 4.81, d = -1.41, p < .001) than Human-only (M = 5.65), with no clear difference for AI-parallel (M = 5.17, d = -0.33, p = .262; p. 8, Section 3.2).

Adjusted means: Human-only M = 5.65, SE = 0.10; AI-follow M = 4.81, SE = 0.10; p < .001, d = -1.41. DeepSeek-V3 robustness ratings: Omnibus F(3, 95) = 6.12, p < .001, partial eta-squared (η²ₚ) = .16. Human-only M = 6.40, SE = 0.20; AI-follow M = 5.61, SE = 0.20; p = .017, d = -0.70. Means are adjusted; SEs are standard errors, not SDs. - `after_ai`, Final-draft overall narrative creativity (independent final draft after AI-assisted first draft) (human_raters, unit participant): Human ratings: Omnibus F(3, 96) = 9.62, p < .001, partial eta-squared (η²ₚ) = .38. Adjusted means: Human-only M = 5.65, SE = 0.10; AI-first M = 4.86, SE = 0.10; p = .002, d = -0.95. DeepSeek-V3 robustness ratings: Omnibus F(3, 95) = 6.12, p < .001, partial eta-squared (η²ₚ) = .16. - Needs authors: The laboratory, online or coursework setting is not stated (open_questions[0]; unresolved reconciliation difference for design_class; pp. 4-7, Methods). Authors could confirm the RCT subtype. This does not unsettle criterion 1 because individual random assignment to AI-access schedules is explicit.
- Needs authors: The evaluator behind the numerical subdomain ANCOVAs is unspecified (open_questions[1]; unresolved reconciliation difference for S1-O5 through S1-O12.measure; p. 9, Section 3.4; pp. 18-19, Figures 3-4). Author identification of the score source would settle attribution. Either evaluator scores participants' narratives, and overall human-rated creativity independently establishes criterion 3.

Authors could provide the participant flow. These details affect attrition assessment; random assignment and more than 50 analyzed people are already established.

- Needs supplement: Supplement contents remain unverified because both direct file requests and the DOI fallback failed with WinError 10013 (open_questions[5]; p. 12, Data Availability; p. 20, Supplementary Files). - Finding: Students working with ChatGPT scored higher than Google users and the peer arm on flexibility (15.03 vs 12.83 and 12.38) and fluency (27.85 vs 23.78 and 23.42), and between them on originality (5.47 vs 3.88 Google and 7.00 peer); three-group F (2, 172) = 9.71, 7.91, and 18.31, Bonferroni p ≤ 0.001, with no pairwise test printed (p. 7, Table 3).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Possible overlap with c471: Liu et al. (2026), Exploring the impact of ChatGPT on college students' mathematical creativity: A quasi-experimental study, doi:10.14742/ajet.10564. The c089 ChatGPT arm and c471 experimental arm have identical reported demographics: age M = 19.20, SD = 1.36; male/female 39/21; sciences/liberal arts/engineering 16/16/28; prior ChatGPT experience never/seldom/sometimes/often/always 2/10/24/23/1. They also use the same baseline and four MCT tasks, but their creativity means and SDs differ. Neither full text explicitly confirms shared participants or independence. Reuse and the number of shared people remain unconfirmed; author clarification is needed before treating these samples as independent. Possible overlap does not establish that c089 is a reanalysis.
- `with_ai`, MCT flexibility (category_count, unit None): MCT (flexibility): Human–ChatGPT M 15.03 (SD 3.78, N = 60), Human–Google M 12.83 (SD 3.29, N = 60), Human–human M 12.38 (SD 3.35); three-group F (2, 172) = 9.71, η²p = 0.10, p < 0.001 (Bonferroni-corrected). Mixed ANOVA: time F (1, 172) = 0.01, η²p = 0.00, p = 0.907; condition F (2, 172) = 3.80, η²p = 0.04, p < 0.05; interaction F (2, 172) = 6.08, η²p = 0.07, p < 0.01. Bonferroni post hoc within-group tests, baseline to MCT: ChatGPT t (59) = 2.48, p < 0.050, d = 0.44; Google t (59) = 0.71, p = 0.483, d = 0.11. No pairwise between-arm test printed; direction from the arm means. [p. 7, Table 3 row 2; p. 7, Section 6.1; Ns from p. 4, Table 1]

- Needs authors: Peer-arm people-to-score mapping, analyzed-person count, common outcome unit, and handling of individual baselines remain unresolved (pp. 3-4, 7-8). Author analysis documentation must identify whether scores represent dyads jointly or person A alone. Criterion 2 already holds from 60 ChatGPT plus 60 Google participants analyzed.
- Needs authors: Possible overlap with c471: identical ChatGPT-arm demographics and tasks do not establish reuse. Authors must confirm independence or identify shared participants and any reanalysis. This affects sample deduplication, not whether c089 meets the three eligibility criteria.
- Needs supplement: External appendix not inspected: obtain the supplement to verify the scoring reference tables, Appendix Tables A1 and A3, and any additional contents (pp. 6, 11-12, 16). Main-text methods, people counts in the individual arms, and outcome scoring already settle eligibility.

**Liu et al. (2026)** [c091], "Generative AI-Supported Creative Writing Among Primary School Students: A Study of Creative Writing Performance, Writing Self-Efficacy, and Flow State". Journal of Computer Assisted Learning. https://doi.org/10.1002/jcal.70347

- Version read: Journal version of record: Journal of Computer Assisted Learning, 2026; 42:e70347, doi 10.1002/jcal.70347, Original Article. Received 4 September 2025, revised 15 September 2026, accepted 23 September 2026 (p. 1). The search record lists no other versions.
- Finding: Fifth graders who wrote with Deepseek scored 88.850 (SD 1.420, n = 20) on a researcher-rated 100-point creative writing rubric, against 86.825 (SD 2.719, n = 38) for students writing in pairs and 85.133 (SD 2.120, n = 20) for students writing alone (one-way ANOVA F = 13.095, p = 0.000***; LSD a > b, a > c), p. 7, Table 1.
- `with_ai`, Creative writing performance (rubric total score, out of 100) (expert_raters, unit participant): GenAI-supported writing group (a): N = 20, mean 88.850, SD 1.420; individual writing group (c): N = 20, mean 85.133, SD 2.120. Three-group one-way ANOVA: F = 13.095, p = 0.000*** (note: ***p < 0.001), partial η2 = 0.259, Cohen's f = 0.591. LSD: a > c (letter ordering only; no pairwise statistic printed). [p. 7, Table 1 and Section 4.1]
- Needs authors: Unit of randomization: the paper says participants were randomly assigned, but they came from three classes, and the arm sizes (20, 38, 20) are not explained. Were individuals randomized across classes, and how? Criterion 1 is met by the explicit participant-randomization statement (p. 4, Section 3.1). Author details on sequence generation, class handling, and allocation would settle implementation; this reporting gap does not make the stated random assignment unclear.
- Needs authors: Peer arm: 19 pairs wrote, yet Tables 1 to 3 count N = 38. Did each pair produce one essay whose score was entered for both members? If so, the peer arm's 38 scores are not independent, which affects its SD, the omnibus F, and the LSD tests. Criterion 2 counts 78 analyzed people, including all 38 peer-arm students (p. 7, Table 1). Author clarification or participant-level data linking partners to essays and scores would settle dependence, which affects precision and the peer contrast rather than the people threshold.
- Needs authors: Raters were three of the researchers. The paper does not say whether they were blind to condition, although all final texts were handwritten. Criterion 3 requires scoring of produced essays, which is explicitly reported (p. 6, Sections 3.3-3.4). The authors' rating protocol would settle blinding, a risk-of-bias question rather than an eligibility requirement.
- Needs authors: Only the rubric total is reported; the originality, flexibility, and elaboration subscores are not. The reported rubric total already satisfies criterion 3 (p. 6, Section 3.4; p. 7, Table 1). Author-provided dimension-level summaries or scoring data would settle subscore extraction.
- Needs authors: Pairwise LSD comparisons are reported only as letter orderings (a > b, a > c, b > c), with no pairwise p-values, standard errors, or confidence intervals. This concerns contrast precision rather than an eligibility criterion. Author-provided pairwise analysis output or data would supply the unreported statistics; the PDF provides letter orderings (pp. 7-8, Tables 1-3), and data are available on request (p. 11).
- Needs authors: The week-1 (unaided) baseline scores by arm are not reported; only the omnibus test (F = 2.56, p > 0.05) is given (p. 4). This concerns baseline comparability rather than an eligibility criterion. Author-provided baseline summaries or data would settle the missing arm means and SDs; Section 3.1 (p. 4) reports only F = 2.56, p > 0.05. This pre-intervention task is not an after-AI outcome.

**Liu et al. (2026)** [c463], "Borrowed or kept? How self-generation-first and direct-adoption AI workflows shape immediate unaided reasoning". Frontiers in Psychology. https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2026.1910545

- Version read: Journal version of record: Frontiers in Psychology 17:1910545, published 14 September 2026 (received 16 June 2026, revised 29 July 2026, accepted 17 August 2026), doi 10.3389/fpsyg.2026.1910545
- Finding: On the unaided essay 15-20 minutes later, direct-adoption AI reduced reasoning quality (-0.38, d = -0.28) and within-essay flexibility (-0.30, d = -0.23) versus No-AI, but not originality (q = 0.28); self-generation-first did not fall below No-AI (pp. 7-8, Section 3.4).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No overlap reported. The document explicitly states that the present sample is independent of both DACOS development samples in Zhu et al. (2026), Not all cognitive offloading is equal: distinguishing dependent and autonomous offloading to generative AI.
- `after_ai`, Phase-2 (unaided) reasoning quality (human_raters, unit participant): adjusted difference = -0.38, 95% CI [-0.59, -0.18], d = -0.28, two-sided p = 0.0002, FDR q = 0.0006; HC3: -0.38, robust SE = 0.10, p = 0.0002; 659 people analyzed (222/225/212). Per-protocol -0.41 [-0.62, -0.20], p = 0.0002, 585 people analyzed; all 900 randomized people analyzed by multiple imputation -0.39 [-0.57, -0.21], p < 0.0001; adjusted arm means and their SE bars appear graphically in Figure 2A. [p. 7, Section 3.4; p. 8, Figure 2A-B; p. 9, Sections 3.5-3.6; p. 10, Table 4]
- `with_ai`, Phase-1 (AI-assisted) reasoning quality (human_raters, unit participant): adjusted difference = +0.78, 95% CI [0.60, 0.96], p < 0.0001, d = 0.75; survived FDR correction [p. 7, Section 3.2]
- Needs authors: Separate supplement existence and additional outcome statistics (open_questions[0] and unresolved reconciliation.differences[0]): the complete PDF identifies no supplement; authors could confirm whether one exists and supply any additional arm-level means, SDs, and uncertainty estimates. Main-text evidence already settles eligibility (p. 5, Section 2.5; p. 13, Data availability statement).
- Needs authors: Residence country (part of open_questions[1]): the paper describes Chinese-speaking students and early-career workers but does not state their country of residence; author confirmation would settle this population descriptor without changing eligibility (p. 3, Section 2.2; p. 13, Section 4.5).
- Needs authors: Raters professional credentials (part of open_questions[1]): author clarification would settle expert-versus-lay status. Blind ratings of submitted essays are explicit and qualify regardless of professional credentials (p. 6, Section 2.6).
- Needs authors: Exact pinned model identifier and sampling parameter values (part of open_questions[1]): the author-held configuration and instruction materials would settle these reproduction details; the PDF already establishes use of the generative model Qwen3.5 (p. 5, Section 2.5).
- Needs authors: Origin of the achieved-cell range 209-225 in the power paragraph remains unexplained. Authors could clarify that source inconsistency; the explicit analyzed counts 222/225/212, totaling 659, are repeated in the participant section and Tables 1-2 and settle the 50-person criterion (p. 3, Section 2.2; p. 4, Tables 1-2; pp. 6-7, Section 2.8).

**Liu et al. (2026)** [c471], "Exploring the impact of ChatGPT on college students’ mathematical creativity: A quasi-experimental study". Australasian Journal of Educational Technology. https://doi.org/10.14742/ajet.10564

- Version read: Published journal version: Australasian Journal of Educational Technology (2026), 42(2), 39-59; DOI 10.14742/ajet.10564. Journal identification and citation appear on PDF pp. 1 and 21 (printed pp. 39 and 59). No revision/version date is stated in the article.
- Finding: Among 124 analyzed students, the ChatGPT group scored higher than controls on mathematical flexibility (d = 0.73), fluency (d = 0.66) and originality (d = 0.42) (PDF p. 11, printed p. 49, Table 5; sample size: PDF p. 7, printed p. 45, Participants).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Possible, unconfirmed overlap with c089: Liu et al. (2025), Who Is the Best Helper for University Students' Mathematical Creativity? (DOI 10.1002/cae.70100). Its Human-ChatGPT group and c471's Experimental group have the same analyzed size and identical reported age mean/SD, gender, major and prior ChatGPT experience counts. This cross-paper match suggests possible overlap but does not establish shared participants. Neither examined recruitment account identifies a shared sample; author confirmation is needed for shared-sample handling in pooled estimates.
- `with_ai`, MCT flexibility (category_count, unit participant): Experimental M = 15.12, SD = 3.71; Control M = 12.50, SD = 3.49; t(122) = 4.05, d = 0.73, p = 0.000 (as printed in Table 5). Analyzed arm sizes: Experimental N = 60 and Control N = 64 (Figure 1). ANCOVA controlling for prior ChatGPT experience: F(1,121) = 16.60, η²p = 0.12, p < 0.001. The Table 5 note and Data analysis section state that p values were adjusted using the Benjamini-Hochberg false discovery rate correction. [PDF p. 11 (printed p. 49), Table 5 row 4 and Results, H1a-c; PDF p. 5 (printed p. 43), Figure 1, for arm sizes; PDF p. 8 (printed p. 46), Data analysis. PDF p. 7 (printed p. 45), Participants/Table 2, confirms the analyzed sample.]
- Needs authors: Randomization mechanism and allocation concealment are not described, and the title/abstract use the quasi-experimental label. Author clarification would settle these reporting details; criterion 1 is met by the explicit statement that all students were randomly assigned, and the codebook does not require concealment details (PDF pp. 1 and 4, printed pp. 39 and 42).
- Needs authors: Initial randomized totals and withdrawals by arm remain unreported. Author-provided allocation and attrition counts would settle this; criterion 2 is met by the documented 124 analyzed people, including 60 in the ChatGPT group and 64 controls (PDF pp. 4-5 and 7, printed pp. 42-43 and 45, Procedure/Figure 1/Participants/Table 2).
- Needs authors: The originality-category reference pool and exact frequency-band boundaries remain unspecified. Authors could provide the complete scoring rule; this affects replication and interpretation of originality scores, while criterion 3 is already met by the documented scoring of produced solutions (PDF pp. 9-10, printed pp. 47-48, Mathematical creativity scoring).
- Needs authors: Scorer qualifications and blinding to condition remain unreported. Authors could provide these details; they affect assessment of scoring bias, while criterion 3 requires output scoring rather than a particular scorer qualification or blinding procedure (PDF pp. 9-10, printed pp. 47-48, Mathematical creativity scoring).
- Needs authors: Possible overlap with c089's Human-ChatGPT group remains unconfirmed despite identical reported group size and demographic distributions. Authors could confirm participant identity across the two papers; this affects shared-sample handling and pooling, not random assignment, the analyzed-person threshold or output scoring in c471 (c471 PDF pp. 4-5 and 7, printed pp. 42-43 and 45; c089 PDF pp. 3-4, Procedure/Participants/Table 1).

- Finding: Among 51 analyzed researchers, unique proposals per hour did not differ significantly across Intensive, Medium, and Low control: means 1.58, 1.88, and 2.19, respectively; F(2, 48) = 1.52, p = 0.22 (p. 14, Table 4; analyzed counts on p. 13, Section 5.3.1).

- `with_ai`, Unique research proposals produced per hour (# Unique_research_proposals) (category_count, unit participant): Intensive 1.58 (SD 0.77) versus Low 2.19 (SD 1.11); analyzed n = 17 per condition. Table 4 reports means and SDs per hour. Omnibus one-way ANOVA across all three conditions: F(2, 48) = 1.52, p = 0.22. This is not a pairwise test; no pairwise test, confidence interval, standard error, or standardized effect is printed for this outcome. [p. 14, Table 4, # Unique_research_proposals and table caption; p. 13, Section 5.3.1 (analyzed counts) and Section 6.1 (interpretation as number of proposals)]

Author clarification of the logging/deduplication rule would settle it; retain the printed hourly normalization (pp. 12-14, Sections 5.3.1 and 6.1; Table 4).
- Needs authors: S1 Generate_seed_ideas counting unit (open_questions[1]): whether this row counts invocations or individual returned ideas remains unresolved. - Needs authors: S1 actual counts at random assignment (open_questions[2]): total and arm-specific assignment counts remain unreported. An author-supplied participant flow or allocation record would settle the missing counts (p. 11, Section 5.1; pp. 12-13, Sections 5.2-5.3.1).
- Needs authors: S1 implemented task duration (open_questions[3]): the main procedure and limitations state 35-40 minutes, whereas Appendix C.2 says 30 minutes. Appendix D reports approximately 40 minutes for P10 but does not establish timing for every participant. None of the three eligibility criteria requires a particular duration. Author clarification of the implemented protocol and participant timing would settle the discrepancy (pp. 11-12, Section 5.1; p. 21, Section 8.5; p. 29, Appendices C.2 and D).
- Needs authors: S2 randomization (part of open_questions[4]; reconciliation.differences: studies[S2].assignment.randomized): the allocation/generation/presentation procedure remains unspecified, so not_stated is retained. This paired model-output benchmark has two topic providers/evaluators and does not supply the qualifying human experiment; randomizing presentation order alone would not establish assignment to human AI assistance. Authors would need to provide the evaluation protocol (p. 27, Appendix B.1).
- Needs authors: S3 randomization (part of open_questions[4]; reconciliation.differences: studies[S3].assignment.randomized): allocation and generation/tool/presentation order remain unspecified, so not_stated is retained. This benchmark ranks initial model drafts with three topic providers/evaluators and cannot supply the at-least-50-person experiment. Authors would need to provide the evaluation protocol; S1 eligibility is independently established (pp. 27-28, Appendix B.2 and Table 7).
- Needs authors: S3 total ranked-draft observations (open_questions[5]; reconciliation.differences: studies[S3].outcomes[S3-O1].n_units): the paper identifies three research ideas and per-system ranks but does not explicitly print a total of ranked output observations. Retain n_units=null with unit=output. Output counts do not determine the people criterion. Authors would need to confirm the number of drafts included in the rankings (pp. 27-28, Appendix B.2 and Table 7).
- Needs authors: S4 pilot reporting and sample overlap (open_questions[6]): analyzed sample size, condition-order randomization, exact model version, and overlap among development pilots and S1 remain unspecified. Authors would need to supply the pilot protocol, participant flow/overlap, and model configuration. The four design collaborators are not substituted for an analyzed cohort (p. 4, Section 3.1; p. 11, Section 4.2; p. 27, Appendix B).

**Luchini et al. (2025)** [c159], "The Roles of Idea Generation and Elaboration in Human-AI Collaborative Creativity". PsyArXiv. https://doi.org/10.31234/osf.io/xm2f5_v3

- Version read: PsyArXiv preprint (DOI 10.31234/osf.io/xm2f5_v3), version 3 (xm2f5_v3), posted 2025-05-26 according to retrieval.json, which lists it as the latest PsyArXiv version (v1 and v2 were .docx uploads the same day). No journal version found. The document itself shows no date, journal, or version label.
- Finding: Within person, stories written with GPT-3.5 available scored higher on MAoSS originality (M = .56 vs .52; t(262) = 5.56, p < .001, d = .34; p. 14) and contributed less to semantic diversity (t(262) = -1.99, p = .047, d = -.123; p. 17) than the same 263 analyzed students' stories written without AI (Figure 2, p. 15).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: c475: Luchini, Kaufman, and Beaty (2025), 'Generative AI Does Not Erase Individual Differences in Human Creativity' (PsyArXiv, DOI 10.31234/osf.io/jszrn_v1, v1 posted 2025-11-14). Its Study 1 reanalyzes this paper's 263 analyzed participants and cites this paper as Luchini et al. (2025c), so the two papers share one sample. The c159 PDF (v3, posted 2025-05-26) names no other paper using these participants.
- `with_ai`, Story originality (MAoSS score) (automated_score, unit participant): Paired-samples t-test: AI-help M = .56, SD = 08 vs No-AI M = .52, SD = 08 (both SDs printed without a decimal point); t(262) = 5.56, p < .001, d = .34, 95% CI [.021, .045]. n = 263 analyzed in each condition (within person). The paper does not say what the CI bounds or which SD d uses. [p. 14, Results, Story Originality; Figure 2 (left panel), p. 15]
- `across_people`, Diversity contribution of each story (embedding_similarity, unit participant): Paired-samples t-test: AI-help M = -.000026, SD = .00033 vs No-AI M = .000027, SD = .00034; t(262) = -1.99, p = .047, d = -.123, 95% CI [-0.0001, -0.0000006]. n = 263 analyzed in each condition (within person). Higher = more diverse, so no sign reversal is needed: AI-help stories contributed less diversity. [p. 17, Results, Story Diversity; Figure 2 (middle panel, values x 10^3), p. 15]
- Needs authors: Carryover: every participant completed a practice story with ChatGPT available before both conditions, about half wrote the No-AI story second, and the paper reports no order or carryover analysis (pp. 9-10). The posted CSV has no order variable.
- Needs authors: Removals by order: the paper gives no count at assignment or by order for the 128 people removed, and 'an equal number' doing each order first cannot be exact for 391 or 263 (pp. 8-9). The posted CSV holds only the 263 analyzed. Criterion 1 rests on the stated randomized order, and each removal drops a person from both arms.
- Needs posted data: Originality SDs printed as 'SD = 08' without a decimal point (p. 14). The posted CSV holds both originality scores for all 263 analyzed participants.
- Needs posted data: Prompt-count outliers: the text says 2 generation outliers were removed, but t(254) and t(253) for originality vs generation count imply 7 (pp. 16-17). Not a coded outcome. The posted CSV holds the prompt counts and difference scores to rerun the correlations.

Three-arm omnibus ANOVA: F(2, 477) = 165.23, p < .001, eta^2 = .409. Text reports Tukey pairwise p < .001, d = -1.34. Abstract instead prints d = -0.58, p < .001 for this contrast. Both conflicting values are retained; neither is corrected. [PDF p. 6 (printed p. 184), Table 1; Section 4.1, post-hoc Tukey HSD paragraphs; PDF p. 1 (printed p. 179), Abstract; d = -1.34 repeated in PDF p. 9 (printed p. 187), Discussion box]
- `across_people`, Across-student semantic diversity: mean pairwise cosine similarity and dispersion (embedding_similarity, unit None): Mean pairwise cosine similarity (SD): Unconstrained GenAI Access 0.689 (0.061), dispersion 0.311; Unaided Control 0.412 (0.084), dispersion 0.588. The correct analysis is not established by the PDF. Author confirmation of the intended fluency analysis would settle the conflicting statistics; the conflict affects effect extraction, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Originality effect conflicts: unconstrained vs control d = -0.58 in Abstract (PDF p. 1), but d = -1.34 in Section 4.1 and Discussion (PDF pp. 6, 9). Both are preserved without recomputation. Author confirmation of the intended originality contrast and standardized effect would settle the conflict; output scoring and eligibility are already established.
- Needs authors: The diversity analysis unit, aggregation into observations, SD basis, and t-test subtype are not given (PDF pp. 5, 7). The pairwise definition and reported t(318) do not resolve these omissions. Also covers the unresolved reconciliation difference for studies[0].outcomes[6].unit and diversity_detail.uncertainty. The authors would need to identify the observations, aggregation, SD basis, and test. This affects diversity-effect precision and pooling, not paper eligibility.
- Needs authors: The task underlying each TTCT score and Table 2 similarity statistic is not identified. CAT ratings are explicitly of project briefs (PDF p. 5, Section 3.4). The authors would need to map each reported score to its task and describe any combination across tasks; both tasks produce eligible creative outputs.
- Needs authors: The exact random allocation procedure and any attrition or exclusions are not described; stratified random sampling concerns recruitment (PDF p. 4, Sections 3.1-3.2). - Needs authors: Which participants used GPT-4o versus Claude 3.5 Sonnet, and whether model choice was assigned, is not stated (PDF p. 4, Section 3.1). Both named interfaces provide generative-AI assistance, so this concerns intervention detail rather than eligibility.
- Needs authors: Section 4.1 names four primary dependent variables, but Table 1 lists six variables and no Semantic Distance row; the omnibus result is Pillai's Trace = 0.941, F(12,946) = 68.34, p < .001, partial eta^2 = .470 (PDF p. 6). The actual MANOVA specification needs clarification. The authors would need to specify the MANOVA dependent-variable set and corresponding analysis output. This affects statistical interpretation, not the presence of eligible output scores.

The printed star legend is also malformed: '* p < .05, p < .01, * p < .001'. Correct significance levels cannot be selected from the PDF. The numeric tables remain the extraction source; the graphic does not decide eligibility.
- Needs authors: The final reference entry ends at 'Bose#' and the PDF stops at printed p. 190 (PDF p. 12). The saved document supplies no continuation or supplement reference. The authors would need to supply a complete reference list or corrected proceedings PDF. The truncated bibliography does not remove the methods and results establishing eligibility.
- Needs authors: The 71% recurring-theme result has no essay denominator, explicit coding rule, comparator, or uncertainty (PDF p. 8, Section 5.2). The authors would need to supply the essay denominator, coding rule, and any comparison or uncertainty. This limits use of the descriptive recurring-theme finding; other output scores establish criterion 3.

**McGuire, De Cremer, and Van de Cruys (2024)** [c096], "Establishing the importance of co-creation and self-efficacy in creative collaboration with artificial intelligence". Scientific Reports. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-024-69423-2

- Finding: Professional poets rated poems submitted by people who received an AI-generated poem to edit as less creative than poems written alone (Study 1: 12.55 vs 18.23 on a 1 to 40 scale, p < 0.001, p. 4 and Table 1; Study 2: 12.53 vs 15.74, p = 0.001, p. 6 and Table 2 on p. 7), while writing line by line in turns with the AI did not differ from writing alone (14.70 vs 15.74, p = 0.278) and scored higher than the editing condition (p = 0.026).
- `with_ai`, Expert evaluations of creativity (human-AI vs human) (expert_raters, unit output): Human condition M = 18.23 (SD = 4.94, n = 48) vs human-AI condition M = 12.55 (SD = 4.32, n = 48); post hoc pairwise p < 0.001. Omnibus one-way ANOVA over the three groups of poems, including the 50 AI-only poems: F(2, 145) = 63.48, p < 0.001, partial η2 = 0.47. No pairwise test statistic or standard error is printed. [p. 4, Results, Study 1 and Table 1]
- Needs posted data: Study 1 counts (open question 3): 101 recruited minus 6 excluded is 95, but the paper prints 96 remaining (48 + 48), and the ANOVA prints error df 145 where 146 poems give 143. Rebuilt from Table 1, the printed F = 63.48 matches neither df (62.30 at 143; arithmetic, not printed). Either people count exceeds 50.
- Needs posted data: Study 1 exclusions (open question 4): the paper does not say whether the 6 attention-check failures were removed before or after allocation, or from which arm, so n_randomized stays null. The OSF data may keep the six with their condition. If not, only the authors can say. Criterion 2 counts people analyzed (96).
- Needs posted data: Editing extent (open question 9, new): editing the AI poem was optional (p. 3), and the paper reports no data on how many editor-arm participants changed it or how much. The randomized contrast does not depend on it. The OSF data may hold the submitted poems and the system's drafts.
- Needs posted data: Rating aggregation (open question 10, new): the paper does not say how the 10 poets' ratings were combined per poem and reports no inter-rater reliability. The OSF data may hold the separate ratings. If not, the authors can say.

**Mei et al. (2025)** [c161], "If ChatGPT can do it, where is my creativity? generative AI boosts performance but diminishes experience in creative writing". Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chbah.2025.100140

- Version read: Journal version of record: Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans 4 (2025) 100140; received 30 December 2024, revised 12 March 2025, accepted 19 March 2025, available online 19 March 2025 (p. 1). Elsevier typeset PDF, open access under CC BY-NC-ND.
- Finding: In an online experiment that randomly assigned UK university students to use or not use ChatGPT on a 10-minute task listing uses for a 'printer that can print anything in 2097' (225 analyzed), the AI arm's mean counts of uses and of conceptual domains were above Control's in all four subgroups (Fig. 2, p. 6), with no significant difference in frequency in Group 2 (b = 0.91, SE = 2.30, p = .69) or on either count in Group 4 (p. 6), and the AI condition's pooled outputs scored higher on group-level frequency (635 vs 552) and flexibility (442 vs 313) (Fig. 5, p. 9); pooled regressions are only in SI Table 3, not in this PDF.
- `with_ai`, Frequency (fluency): number of different uses of the printer in a participant's piece (category_count, unit participant): Pooled across groups the text says AI participants had 'higher frequency and flexibility of creativity' (p. 6); the pooled regression is only in SI Table 3. Fig. 2 plots arm means with 95% CIs for each of the four groups, with no numbers printed; the AI mean is above Control in all four. Group 2 (Native, Inexperienced): 'no significant differences were observed for frequency (b = 0.91, SE = 2.30, p = .69)'. Group 4 (Native, Experienced): 'No significant differences were found in flexibility, correctness, frequency, or clarity'. Fig. 3 (p. 7) repeats the comparison in 21 demographic subgroups. [p. 6, Section 3.2 and Fig. 2; p. 7, Fig. 3; SI Table 3 (not in this PDF)]
- `across_people`, Group-level frequency: number of different uses, scored for each condition's pooled outputs (Fig. 5, bottom panel) (category_count, unit set): Fig. 5, bottom panel (Frequency), Control vs AI: Grp 9 (all participants) 552 vs 635; Grp 1 (Non-Native Inexperienced) 193 vs 140; Grp 2 (Native Inexperienced) 189 vs 198; Grp 3 (Non-Native Experienced) 136 vs 189; Grp 4 (Native Experienced) 161 vs 221; Grp 5 (Non-Native) 302 vs 315; Grp 6 (Native) 312 vs 397; Grp 7 (Inexperienced) 344 vs 312; Grp 8 (Experienced) 283 vs 370. The text prints the Grp 9 pair under the other label, 'Flexibility (635 vs. 552)' (p. 7); the frequency exceptions it names (Grp 1: 140 vs. 193; Grp 7: 312 vs. 344) match the figure's frequency panel. No test statistic or uncertainty is reported. [p. 9, Fig. 5 (bottom panel); p. 7, Section 3.2.3; SI Table 5 (not in this PDF)]
- Needs supplement: Arm sizes (AI vs Control) are not printed in the main text, and the paper describes SI Table 1 only as a demographic breakdown. Criterion 2 counts all people analyzed and does not need them.
- Needs supplement: Pooled across-group coefficients and SEs for frequency, flexibility, inaccuracy and unclarity are only in SI Table 3 (online). The PDF prints b and SE for three within-group contrasts.
- Needs authors: Sample arithmetic: 266 - 43 = 223, not the reported 225, and the p. 8 t-tests imply 222 people. Every count is far above 50, so criterion 2 holds under any of them.
- Needs authors: Where the attention check sat in the study is not stated. The 43 excluded people completed the study, so they were dropped after assignment. The paper states random assignment either way, so criterion 1 holds.
- Needs authors: Whether randomization was stratified by the four groups is not stated. The paper states random assignment either way.
- Needs supplement: How the Fig. 5 group-level counts were computed (removal of duplicates across participants, any scaling by arm size) is not described. Criterion 3 rests on the individual counts S1-O1 and S1-O2 in any case.
- Needs authors: Which LLM's counts (GPT-4o or GPT-4 Turbo) enter the analysis is not stated. Human validation covered 40 of the 225 responses, with the ICCs printed on p. 5.
- Needs authors: Which ChatGPT model or tier the AI arm used, how participants reached ChatGPT, and whether any instruction limited how they used it are not stated. ChatGPT is generative AI under any model, so criterion 1 holds.

- Finding: In a reanalysis of Lee and Chung's (2024) experiments, ChatGPT-aided idea pools had a lower fraction of unique ideas (Universal Sentence Encoder cosine threshold 0.8) than comparison pools in every experiment, for example 0.74 vs 0.96 with web search (Experiment 1), 0.70 vs 0.92 unaided and 0.91 with web search (Experiment 2a), and 0.41 vs 1.00 unaided (Experiment 2b); the paper reports the difference as significant in four of five experiments, all but Experiment 4 (p. 1108, Fig. 1 and text).

Error bars are 95% CIs for individual proportions, not printed as numbers. Text: AI-aided sets are less diverse in all five experiments, significant in four of five comparisons, the non-significant one in experiment 4. - Needs posted data: Whether an Experiment 1 entry that lists several ideas was embedded whole or cut to one idea (Supplementary Table 1's 233 units equal Lee and Chung's analyzed participants).
- Needs authors: Whether the 95% CIs for differences in Supplementary Table 1 are Bonferroni-corrected, as the Fig. 1 legend says; the Experiment 4 low-constraint CI excludes zero while the corrected p is 0.096.

The text also reports mean=4.115 for category coverage without identifying it as an arm-specific mean. No arm SDs, SEs or CIs are printed; rB is the reported rank-biserial effect, not a standardized mean difference. [PDF p. 6 (printed p. 426), section 4.1 and Figure 3; inclusion of accepted AI ideas begins on PDF p. 5.]

- Needs authors: open_questions[2]: Resolve the conflicting significance claims for human-origin idea quantity in sections 4.1 and 5.1 (PDF pp. 6 and 8). Authors’ correction and the underlying quantity test would settle which statement is correct. Eligibility requires a scored output, not a statistically significant effect, so no criterion depends on this discrepancy.

Authors’ specification of accepted-versus-logged ideas and their original analysis would settle it. S1-O5 is now descriptive corpus information; participant-level flexibility scores independently satisfy criterion 3.

No supplement or public data repository is identified; authors would need to supply the missing statistics or underlying participant-level scores. This concerns effect extraction and precision, not any of the three eligibility criteria.

**Memmert et al. (2025)** [c098], "Brainstorming with a Generative Language Model: Effect of Exposure to AI Ideas on Brainstorming Performance and Cognitive Load". Business & Information Systems Engineering. https://doi.org/10.1007/s12599-025-00974-y

- Version read: Journal version of record: Bus Inf Syst Eng 68(5):1023-1047 (2026), doi 10.1007/s12599-025-00974-y; received 3 February 2025, accepted 19 September 2025, published online 18 November 2025 (PDF p. 1).
- Finding: With on-demand GPT-3.5 ideas, participants' final idea lists (own plus copied GLM ideas) were longer (mdn 7 vs 5; U = 963.5, p = 0.006) and covered more categories (mdn 5 vs 3; U = 1080.0, p < 0.001), and their shortlists were rated more novel (mean 3.396 vs 2.876; t(73) = 2.889, p = 0.005, d = 0.667) and more valuable (mdn 4.900 vs 4.425; U = 994.0, p = 0.002), but they wrote fewer ideas themselves (mdn 4 vs 5; U = 475.0, text p = 0.015; Fig. 7a instead marks **, defined as p < .01) and their own ideas were not rated more novel (U = 699.5, p = 0.406) or valuable (U = 737.5, p = 0.204) (PDF pp. 12-13, Sects. 4.1-4.2, Figs. 7-8).

These matches support possible shared data, but neither source establishes the identity or number of overlapping quantitative participants. c098 cites c861 and Memmert (2024, ECIS) as prior work without declaring sample overlap. Independence remains unconfirmed; obtain author confirmation before assigning independent sample IDs.
- `with_ai`, Human-level fluency (human_fluency, H1-a): number of the participant's own ideas, copied GLM ideas excluded (category_count, unit participant): Mann-Whitney U = 475.0, p = 0.015; human-only mdn = 5 vs human + GLM mdn = 4; rank-biserial rB = -0.324; 'This corresponds to a Cohen's d = 0.581', below the study's sensitivity threshold of d >= 0.671 for Mann-Whitney tests. No means or SDs printed. Internal source discrepancy: Fig. 7a marks this comparison **, and its caption defines ** as p < .01, conflicting with the text p = 0.015. The text value is retained as printed; which source is erroneous remains unconfirmed. [Text statistics: PDF p. 12 (journal p. 1034), Sect. 4.1; conflicting ** marker and caption: Fig. 7a, PDF p. 13 (journal p. 1035); direction and significance at 95% confidence: Table 2 row 1-a, PDF p. 15]
- Needs authors: Design setting: authors could clarify supervision and whether the seminar task was ordinary coursework, refining lab_rct versus field_rct. Random assignment is explicit in either case (original open question 2; PDF pp. 8-10, Sects. 3.1-3.4).

If Appendix D does not resolve the duplication, authors must clarify; eligibility is unaffected (original open question 6; PDF pp. 11, 13).
- Needs authors: Shared sample: authors must identify quantitative overlap with c861 and any related ECIS report. Matching quotations and c861's stated prior-study reuse support possible shared data but do not give an overlap count. This affects independent sample IDs and pooling only (original open question 7; c098 PDF p. 15; c861 PDF pp. 1, 4-5, 7).

A later version exists.
- Finding: Among 60 Prolific participants, randomized and analyzed 30 per arm, unlocking GPT-4 only after independent ideation (C_after) rather than from the start (C_before) gave lower TF-IDF similarity to the LLM's ideas (0.648 vs 0.899; p = 0.006, d = 0.747; the printed t = 4.778 is inconsistent) and no clear difference in the number of ideas (5.567 vs 3.867; t(58) = -1.897, p = 0.063, d = -0.490, which the paper calls marginal) or in within-person idea diversity (t(36.472) = 1.337, p = 0.189) (p. 7, Section 4.1; p. 8, Figure 3).
- `with_ai`, Quantity of supporting ideas (category_count, unit participant): Student t-test: t(58) = -1.897, p = 0.063, d = -0.490 (the paper calls the effect marginal). C_before M = 3.867, SD = 2.285; C_after M = 5.567, SD = 4.345; n = 30 per condition at assignment. Signs of t and d run C_before minus C_after (C_before mean is lower and t is negative). SEM (Table 2), Quantity of Ideas on LLM Usage Timing: estimate 1.563, Std. Error 0.905, z-value 1.727, p-value 0.084, 95% CI [0.120, 3.210] (bootstrap, 1000 samples), with creative self-efficacy, autonomy, and ownership as co-predictors. Coded no_clear_difference: p = 0.063 is above 0.05, Figure 3(a) marks it 'ns', Table 3 calls it 'marginally significant', and the SEM row is not bold (the paper bolds significant results, p. 9) and is absent from Figure 6. The point estimate favors C_after. [p. 7, Section 4.1; p. 8, Figure 3(a); p. 9, Section 4.3.2; p. 10, Figure 6; p. 11, Tables 2 and 3]
- Needs journal version: Similarity t (open question 1): the printed Welch t(48.36) = 4.778 conflicts with the printed means, SDs, df, p = 0.006, and d = 0.747, which all fit t = 2.894 at 30 per arm. Extraction should not use the printed t. The ACM version of record may print a corrected value. If it does not, only the authors can settle it.
- Needs authors: Similarity score scale (open question 3): the score is a vector magnitude, so it can rise with the number of LLM ideas a participant received, and C_before had the LLM for about 20 minutes against about 10 in C_after. The paper does not report LLM ideas per arm, and Table 1, step 6.1.1 calls the score the magnitude of 'the TF-IDF vector'. The document names no posted data.
- Needs authors: Sign conventions (open question 8): the coding of LLM Usage Timing in the SEM is not stated (the text implies C_after is the higher value), and neither is the sign convention of the diversity test's t and d. A signed diversity effect needs it, but the coded direction does not. The document names no posted data.
- Needs journal version: Version (open question 9): the file is the arXiv preprint in CHI '25 format with a placeholder DOI and ISBN. The ACM version of record (DOI 10.1145/3706598.3713146) was not compared.

**Rayan et al. (2025)** [c109], "Cueing the Crowd: LLM-Driven Conversational Cues Across Different Meeting Modalities Increase Topical Diversity of Generated Ideas". CI '25: Proceedings of the ACM Collective Intelligence Conference (San Diego, CA, USA, August 4-6, 2025). https://doi.org/10.1145/3715928.3737486

- Version read: ACM version of record: CI '25, Proceedings of the ACM Collective Intelligence Conference (San Diego, August 4-6, 2025), printed pp. 297-316, published 4 August 2025, CC BY 4.0, DOI 10.1145/3715928.3737486, retrieved from the NSF Public Access Repository (PAR 10654070). PDF p. 1 is an ACM Digital Library cover page dated 16 December 2025; PDF page = printed page minus 295. The markdown conversion on file (written 4 October 2026) holds the whole text: references [1] to [137] and Appendix A.1 to A.6 (printed pp. 310-316, markdown lines 671-1079), with the figure captions. It omits the figure images, so the values in Figs. 5, 6, and 10 were read from the PDF.
- Finding: Dyads that received GPT-4o-mini cues produced ideas with higher topic diversity (entropy 2.3, SD 0.3 vs 2.1, SD 0.4; F(1, 67.03) = 6.51, p = 0.013) and conversations covering more topics (9.6 vs 8.0 unique topics; F(1, 50.76) = 6.25, p = 0.016), with no significant difference in the number of ideas and no printed test of across-group homogenization (PDF pp. 7-9, Sec. 5.1, Fig. 5; PDF p. 17, Fig. 6).
- `with_ai`, Topic diversity of generated ideas (BERTopic topics, Shannon entropy; one score per dyad) (category_count, unit team): Cues μ = 2.3, SD = 0.3 vs without cues μ = 2.1, SD = 0.4 (arm ns not printed); REML Access-to-Cue F(1, 67.03) = 6.51, p = 0.013; Tukey HSD p ≈ 0.012 (Fig. 5a caption: p = 0.012). Modality F(1, 67.17) = 0.79, p = 0.38; no cue x modality interaction found (no statistic printed). Fig. 5a box plot: Cue Max 2.784, Q3 2.525, Med 2.351, Q1 2.095, Min 1.494; Without Cue Max 2.805, Q3 2.322, Med 2.098, Q1 1.839, Min 1.075. [PDF p. 7 (printed p. 302), Sec. 5.1.1; PDF p. 8 (printed p. 303), Fig. 5a]
- `across_people`, Homogenization of ideas across groups: cosine distance of each idea to an average idea embedding (per condition in Sec. 6.3, pooled over both arms in App. A.4.1) (embedding_similarity, unit output): No test statistic, p-value, mean, or n is printed for ideas. Sec. 6.3 names the Wilcoxon test and then reports only that 'across groups, participants were exposed to semantically similar cues' (182 of 578 divergence cues repeated verbatim in other groups). Fig. 6 ('Distribution of Cosine Distances: Cue vs No-Cue Ideas') is the only evidence: the violins overlap, with medians about 0.55 (Cue) and 0.56 (No Cue) and quartile bars about 0.47-0.64 (Cue) and 0.46-0.67 (No Cue), all read from the figure, not printed. The caption does not say which arm is more concentrated. Direction coded from the figure; the paper states none. [PDF p. 11 (printed p. 306), Sec. 6.3; PDF p. 16 (printed p. 311), App. A.4.1; PDF p. 17 (printed p. 312), Fig. 6]
- Needs posted data: Number of people and dyads analyzed per arm and per outcome is not printed; the df imply at least about 71 (ideas) and 55 (conversation) dyads, and footnote 12 does not say whether the 16 researcher-only recordings are among the 70 audio sessions (open_questions 1). Criterion 2 is met on the arithmetic lower bound of 138 people.
- Needs posted data: Wilcoxon result for idea homogenization is not printed; Fig. 6 is the only evidence (open_questions 2)
- Needs authors: Topic-diversity score of ideas: per idea or per dyad, and how the dyad score is built (open_questions 3)
- Needs authors: Allocation procedure: random assignment is stated three times, but the pre-survey also served 'balancing across conditions'; criterion 1 is met as printed (open_questions 8)
- Needs authors: Centroid of the homogenization measures: per condition (Sec. 6.3) or pooled over both arms (App. A.4.1 and A.4.2) (open_questions 9)
- Needs authors: Whether Cue dyads also received cues during the familiarity-building task (open_questions 10)

**Rosenbaum et al. (2025)** [c539], "Scaffolding Creativity: How Divergent and Convergent LLM Personas Shape Human Machine Creative Problem-Solving". arXiv. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2510.26490

- Version read: arXiv:2510.26490v1 [cs.HC], 30 Oct 2025, as printed on PDF p. 1.
- Finding: Persona-guided ChatGPT increased between-person semantic distance versus standard ChatGPT (M=0.34 vs 0.28; p=2.20 × 10−6), with no significant fluency difference (M=8.55 vs 9.23) or within-person distance difference (M=0.59 vs 0.59; p=.90; pp. 9-10, section 5.3 and Figure 6).
- `across_people`, Between-participant semantic differentiation (paper: participant-level originality) (embedding_similarity, unit participant): Same-condition originality: Treatment M=0.34, Control M=0.28; Welch’s t, p=2.20 × 10−6 (text); Figure 6 prints Welch p = 2.2e-06. All-participant originality: Treatment M=0.33, Control M=0.31; p=.02 (text); Figure 6 prints Welch p = 0.0215. Cross-condition nearest neighbor distance: Treatment M=0.20, Control M=0.17; p<.01 (text); Figure 6 prints Welch p = 0.00249. Figure 6 labels every comparison treatment n=70 and control n=31, conflicting with the 69/32 final-dataset split on p. 5. No numerical SD, SE, t statistic, degrees of freedom, standardized effect, or confidence-interval endpoints are reported for these contrasts. The type of plotted error bars is not defined. [p. 10, section 5.3, paragraphs beginning Three complementary originality measures and Same-condition originality; p. 10, Figure 6, exact p-value and sample labels; p. 5, section 5, conflicting sample counts]
- `with_ai`, Idea fluency (category_count, unit participant): Treatment M=8.55, SD=3.85; Control M=9.23, SD=3.37. The text states no significant between-condition differences but gives no p-value or numerical test statistic for fluency. No fluency-specific arm counts are printed. The overall final-dataset counts are reported as 69 treatment and 32 control on p. 5, with a conflicting 70/31 split shown for originality in Figure 6; neither split is assumed to be the confirmed fluency sample. [p. 9, section 5.3, Computational Creativity Assessment (means, SDs, and direction); p. 5, section 5 (overall analysis counts); p. 10, Figure 6 (conflicting originality counts)]
- Needs authors: Which analyzed arm counts are correct: 69 treatment/32 control in the results text or 70/31 on every Figure 6 comparison? Confirm outcome-specific counts for fluency and within-participant distance as well (p. 5, section 5; pp. 9-10, section 5.3 and Figure 6). No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: both reported arm splits total 101 analyzed people, and Figure 6 is explicitly participant-level. Author-confirmed allocation and outcome-inclusion records would settle the arm split and the outcome-specific counts.
- Needs authors: The paper states four exclusions but lists reason counts of 1, 2, 1, and 1. Were reasons overlapping, or is a count incorrect (p. 5, section 5)? No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: the explicitly reported analyzed total is 101, independently consistent with Figure 6. The authors need to identify overlapping exclusion reasons or correct the reported counts.
- Needs authors: Was the randomized session supervised in a laboratory, administered remotely, or part of ordinary coursework? The RCT setting is not stated sufficiently to select a subtype (pp. 4-5, section 4). No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: random assignment is explicit regardless of the RCT subtype. An author description of where and how the session was administered would settle the setting.
- Needs authors: Which ChatGPT model/version and complete persona prompts were used for participant-facing chat? GPT-4.1 is named only for later idea extraction (p. 4, section 4; p. 9, section 5.3). No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: both assigned conditions explicitly used generative ChatGPT. Author-provided model identifiers and complete system/persona prompts would settle replication details.
- Needs authors: What do the Figure 6 error bars represent, and what numerical participant-level SDs, SEs, or interval bounds underlie the distance comparisons (p. 10, section 5.3 and Figure 6)? No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: the outcomes are explicitly scored on participant-produced ideas. Author-provided error-bar definitions and numerical participant-level uncertainty would settle precision extraction.
- Needs authors: What observations do the behavioral-analysis counts represent: treatment/control n=63/51 for divergent interactions and 59/51 for convergent interactions, despite only 32 controls being randomized? Clarify the unit or correct the counts; these process-analysis counts are not used for the creativity-output sample (p. 8, section 5.2; p. 4, section 4). No eligibility criterion remains unsettled: these question-asking process analyses are excluded from the qualifying creativity outcomes and are not used to count unique people. Author clarification of the observation unit or corrected behavioral-analysis counts would settle the discrepancy.

**Röttger et al. (2026)** [c526], "Measuring and Mitigating Persona Distortions from AI Writing Assistance". arXiv. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2604.22503

- Version read: arXiv:2604.22503v2 [cs.CL], 29 June 2026, as printed on PDF p. 1; saved preprint.
- Finding: In the fixed-order comparison, AI-assisted paragraphs were rated +12.5 points more original (p. 5, Figure 2B) and showed lower dispersion on 22 of 29 attributes (p. 4, Section 2.2), while randomized reranking attenuated the AI-induced clarity increase by 26.9% (p. 8, Figure 4D).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No reuse of another paper's participants is stated. Within this paper, 1,469 Study 3 readers also participated in Study 1; writers are distinct across studies and separate from readers. Study 3 reward models reuse Study 1 training data. Studies 4 and 5 reuse Study 1 paragraph material, with new reader samples; their writer source corpus must not be treated as new independent writers.
- `across_people`, Dispersion of perceived writer confidence across paragraphs (human_raters, unit None): Perceived writer confidence: human-written paragraphs 24.1 SD versus AI-assisted paragraphs 20.5 SD. No attribute-specific p-value, CI, SE, or final analysis count is printed. [p. 4, Section 2.2, paragraph beginning "AI writing assistance also homogenised perceived writer personas"; details referred to SI:5.9, not in this PDF.]
- `with_ai`, Perceived paragraph originality (human_raters, unit rating): +12.5 pts more original for AI-assisted versus human writing; *** denotes p<.001 after Bonferroni correction across all 29 attributes. AME on the original 0-100 scale; no numeric SE, CI, arm means, or arm SDs for this outcome are printed. [p. 5, Figure 2B and caption]

- Needs supplement: Study 1 dispersion analysis unit, rating aggregation, repeated-output dependence, uncertainty methods and attribute-specific SD/entropy results, including omitted attributes, require SI:5.9 (p. 4, Section 2.2). These are contextual outcome-extraction gaps; Study 3 independently meets the output criterion.
- Needs supplement: Initial assignment totals and participant attrition require SI:1.4 (p. 11, Section 4.1). They do not leave criterion 2 unresolved: the reported analyzed Study 3 sample comprises 769 writers, with completer status explicit (pp. 11 and 13, Sections 4.1 and 4.10).
- Needs posted data: Exact distinct-writer counts by outcome and arm after output-based restrictions are not printed (p. 4, Section 2.2; pp. 12-14, Sections 4.6-4.10). Posted data linking included ratings and paragraphs to writer IDs would settle them. These counts are needed for extraction; the reported Study 3 study-level sample already establishes criterion 2.
- Needs posted data: Uncertainty suitable for pooling must respect writer-level assignment and repeated outputs; reader-perception regressions use reader random effects only (pp. 12-14, Sections 4.7 and 4.10). Reanalysis of posted data with writer-linked observations would settle this. It affects pooling, not any of the three screening criteria.
- Needs supplement: Study 1 randomized AI-model/input contrasts may be extractable from SI:5.7-5.8 (p. 13, Section 4.7). This could add eligible configuration contrasts but is not needed for criterion 1, already met by Study 3; the recorded pooled AI-versus-unaided comparison remains nonrandomized.

- Version read: arXiv:2503.01631v1 [cs.HC], 3 Mar 2025 (left-margin stamp on p. 1), typeset as the CHI '25 paper (CHI '25, April 26-May 1, 2025, Yokohama, Japan; ACM reference and DOI 10.1145/3706598.3713273 on p. 1). - Finding: Of 456 designers randomized to reframe a design problem without an LLM or with GPT-4o in three ways, 280 were analyzed, and rater-scored novelty and usefulness of the frame each person picked as best did not differ between MANUAL (62 analyzed) and LLM-ALL (218 analyzed) or among the four approaches (all p > 0.05), reported only as box plots (p. 6, Figure 4 and Section 4.2; p. 8, Figure 5; pp. 8-9, Section 5.2).
- `with_ai`, Novelty of the problem frame (expert rated) (expert_raters, unit output): Box plots only (Figure 5, left panel). Mann-Whitney U test: 'there were no statistically significant differences for either, between the manual and the llm-all group but also among all the individual approaches (all p > 0.05)'. - Needs authors: Statistics (open question 1): no arm means, SDs, or test statistics for novelty or usefulness are printed, only the Figure 5 box plots and 'all p > 0.05'. The paper names no supplement or data repository. Effect sizes need arm-level data from the authors, or figure digitizing as a fallback.
- Needs authors: Attrition by arm (open question 2): per-arm counts at assignment and per-arm exclusions are not printed, and only the LLM arms had a no-use filter. Random assignment is stated and 280 were analyzed, so criteria 1 and 2 hold either way. The gap bears on risk of bias, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Rating procedure (open question 3): the scale, the number of raters per frame, and how ratings were combined are not stated. Raters blind to condition scored each analyzed frame, so criterion 3 holds either way.
- Needs authors: Pairwise tests (open question 8): no pair-specific post hoc result is printed, so the FREE-FORM vs MANUAL record that rule 3 makes primary, and every other single-arm contrast, rests on the blanket 'all p > 0.05' and Figure 5. Pair-level statistics need the authors' data.
- Needs journal version: Version (open question 9): the file is arXiv v1 of 3 March 2025, typeset as the CHI '25 paper with its DOI. The ACM version of record was not compared.
