- `across_people`, Semantic similarity among participants' first ideas within each condition (embedding_similarity, unit pair): Cosine similarity (higher = more alike; sign reversed for diversity): human-only M = 0.81 (SD 0.08, N ideas 45) vs LLM-assisted human M = 0.74 (SD 0.13, N ideas 45); M difference = 0.07, 95% CI [0.04, 0.10], p = 0.00, Cohen's d = 0.91 (permutation test, 10,000 iterations). No SE printed. The AI arm's ideas are less similar, i.e. more diverse. [p. 8, Section 4.6.2; p. 11, Table 7]
- Needs authors: Study 2: establish whether the out-of-instruction-scope category enters main-analysis breadth despite exclusions (p. 9, Sections 5.1 and 5.4; Appendix C, p. 16). Authors can provide the inclusion rule or analysis data; the confirmed analyzed count is 117 people.
- Needs authors: Per-arm exclusions: obtain explicit allocation and reasons from the authors. The PDF already establishes total exclusions and randomized and analyzed counts by arm, so the 50-person criterion is settled (pp. 6, 8-9, 11).
- Needs authors: Similarity SD basis, standardized-effect denominator and participant-level uncertainty: obtain the analysis specification or code from the authors before pooling; Table 7 does not identify the SD unit (pp. 8, 10-11). This affects extraction, not whether similarity was scored on outputs.
- Needs authors: Study 2 similarity idea selection: confirm whether the first-idea rule was applied; the PDF says analyses were parallel to Study 1 but does not restate selection (p. 10, Section 5.5.2; p. 11, Table 7). Eligibility does not depend on which participant idea was selected.
- Needs authors: Study 2 similarity contrast sign: obtain the sign convention and unrounded arm means behind the printed difference of 0.01 when both means are 0.87 (pp. 10-11). This affects effect direction, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Study 1 stimulus-pool comparison: obtain the actual pool mean and preprocessing used for the difference of -0.10; the separate cleaned-pool mean of 0.69 is not established as the same analysis (pp. 7-8). This is context evidence.
- Needs authors: Study 1 usefulness-item composition, also the unresolved reconciliation difference for S1-O6: authors must clarify whether the third item enters the reported usefulness and creativity averages (Appendix D, p. 18). Other output measures already establish criterion 3.
- Needs authors: Possible sample overlap with c917 (AOM 2022, DOI 10.5465/ambpp.2022.12146abstract): the AOM acknowledgement and local leads do not confirm identity; obtain author confirmation linking the samples. This affects deduplication, not the within-study eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: Possible sample overlap with the cited Sorin and Pagani 2023 chapter: authors must establish whether it reports the same participants (pp. 2, 20). This affects deduplication, not the within-study eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: Study 1 recruitment country: obtain author confirmation; language and international-student status do not establish country (p. 6, Section 4.1). Country is not an eligibility criterion.
- Needs authors: Idea length/elaboration: authors must identify the study, measurement procedure, aggregation unit, analyzed sample and numerical results behind the qualitative shorter-ideas statement (Appendix D, p. 19). It remains paper-level and does not affect eligibility because multiple other outcomes are directly scored on outputs.

**Su et al. (2026)** [c570], "Who Thinks First? Designing Productive Friction with Engage-to-Unlock GenAI". arXiv. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2610.01518

- Version read: arXiv:2610.01518v1 [cs.HC], posted 1 Oct 2026; manuscript date on title page 2026-09-11 (p. 1).
- Finding: Among 398 analyzed writers, essay semantic dispersion was descriptively similar across conditions (Human-Only 0.179, Engage-to-Unlock 0.187, Standard Chatbot 0.188, Time-Matched Unlock 0.188), without a formal between-condition test (p. 6, section 3.2; p. 13, Table 1; p. 9, section 3.5.3).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Cross-paper participant reuse is not stated. Phase 2 explicitly excluded Phase 1 participants. The PERSUADE 2.0 corpus supplied classifier-validation essays, not an additional experimental participant sample.
- `across_people`, Within-condition semantic dispersion (D_c), with homogenization index (HI_c) (embedding_similarity, unit set): Standard Chatbot: analyzed n = 98, D_c = 0.188 [0.178, 0.194], HI_c = -0.050 (-5.0%); Human-Only: analyzed n = 99, D_c = 0.179 [0.167, 0.188], HI_c = 0.000 (baseline). Brackets are within-condition 95% bootstrap CIs. The treatment point estimate is higher, but the paper presents dispersion descriptively and does not test a between-condition difference or equivalence. [p. 13, Table 1 and section 4.2.1; p. 9, section 3.5.3]
- `with_ai`, Final-essay word count (None, unit participant): Standard Chatbot M = 308.32, SD = 138.92, Mdn = 253.5, analyzed n = 98; Human-Only M = 277.11, SD = 102.70, Mdn = 248.0, analyzed n = 99. Across all four participant conditions, Welch F(3, 216.55) = 1.43, p = .234, omega-squared = .004, eta-squared = .012. These are omnibus statistics; no pair-specific significance test is printed. [pp. 14-15, section 4.2.3; p. 15, Table 2; p. 6, section 3.2 (arm counts)]
- Needs authors: Assignment-stage totals, phase-specific pre-exclusion counts, and per-arm attention-check exclusions are not reported; 445 is the session-completer count, not a randomized count (p. 6, section 3.2). Authors would need to supply assignment and exclusion counts by phase and arm, including any losses before completion. This does not affect criteria 1 or 2: random assignment in Phase 1 and sufficient analyzed people are explicit; it does not concern criterion 3.
- Needs authors: The methods promise discourse differences, SEs, degrees of freedom, and CIs (p. 8), but most discourse outcomes lack these numerical results in the main text and included appendices. No external supplement or data/code repository is identified. Authors would need to supply the missing discourse arm summaries and contrast estimates with precision. No eligibility criterion depends on these ancillary discourse statistics; criterion 3 is established by final-essay semantic dispersion.
- Needs authors: Dispersion has condition-level bootstrap intervals, but no arm-difference SE or interval and no formal between-condition test; the precise bootstrap resampling procedure is not specified (p. 9, section 3.5.3; p. 13, Table 1). Authors would need to identify the bootstrap resampling unit and procedure and provide arm-difference uncertainty or the data and code needed to estimate it. This affects extraction and pooling, not criteria 1-3; an output-scored diversity measure is present even without a formal between-condition test.

**Sun et al. (2025)** [c574], "How and for whom using generative AI affects creativity: A field experiment.". Journal of Applied Psychology. https://doi.org/10.1037/apl0001296

- Version read: Journal of Applied Psychology, Online First Publication, June 16, 2025, DOI 10.1037/apl0001296; APA cover page plus printed pp. 1–13 (14 PDF pages). Version identified from PDF p. 1; this is the saved Online First copy. A later version exists.
- Finding: Among 250 analyzed employees, ChatGPT access increased supervisor-rated creativity (d = 1.25) and written-response novelty (d = 0.35), while usefulness favored AI in the unadjusted comparison (d = 0.33) but was not significant with controls (p = .136; PDF pp. 6, 8, 10, Results and Tables 2–3).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No shared sample with another paper is stated in this document.
- Needs authors: AI availability while composing the privacy-protection response (S1-O2/S1-O3, also the unconfirmed reconciliation agreed-field check): authors' task instructions or access records would settle with_ai versus after_ai classification. No eligibility criterion is unresolved.
- Needs authors: Arm-specific randomized and analyzed counts: authors' allocation and analysis records would settle them. Total analyzed N = 250 already establishes criterion 2.
- Needs supplement: Missing supplement and inconsistent no-controls locators (Supplemental Table S5 Model 4 versus Supplemental Materials S4): the supplement would verify the locations and additional robustness statistics. The main PDF establishes all three eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: External-rater qualifications: authors' recruitment or qualification information would distinguish lay/crowd from expert raters. The unknown subtype does not alter criterion 3 because human scoring of written output is verified.

**Urban et al. (2024)** [c123], "ChatGPT improves creative problem-solving performance in university students: An experimental study". Computers & Education. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.compedu.2024.105031

- Version read: Author accepted manuscript for Computers & Education, dated March 9th, 2024. The boxed notice on p. 1 says it is not the copy of record and gives the final article's DOI, 10.1016/j.compedu.2024.105031. Per retrieval.json, this file is the PsyArXiv posting (10.31234/osf.io/9z2tc, OSF file version 3, uploaded 2024-03-18), and retrieval found no differences in studies or sample from the journal version of record (Computers & Education 215, 105031), which is not on file. Printed page numbers match the PDF page index. A later version exists.
- Finding: Students randomly assigned to solve the Product Improvement Task with ChatGPT received higher expert ratings than students without it on quality (3.60 vs 2.99, d = 0.69), elaboration (2.79 vs 2.21, d = 0.61), and originality (2.74 vs 2.21, d = 0.55), all p < .001 (77 vs 68 students analyzed; means, d, and p in Table 2, p. 16; Ns in Table 1, p. 11).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Urban et al. (2025), 'Prompting for creative problem-solving: A process-mining study', Learning and Instruction 99, 102156 (atlas candidate c594). It analyzes the 77 experimental-arm participants of this study: the coded transcripts of their ChatGPT conversations and the expert ratings of their PIT solutions, with the same weighted kappas (.88, .93, .85). It has no no-AI arm. Its sample description matches this paper's Table 1 experimental column on N, sex (24 men, 53 women), mean age (22.4), study subject (57, 17, 3), and level (53 undergraduates, 24 graduates), but it prints age SD 4.5 where Table 1 prints 4.1. This PDF predates c594 and names no other paper that uses its sample. Codebook section 2 gives the two papers one sample. c594 is not in retrieval.json and was read in its PsyArXiv preprint, not its journal version.
- `with_ai`, PIT quality (expert_raters, unit participant): Table 2, '4. PIT (quality)': Control M = 2.99 (SD 0.86, N = 68) vs Experimental M = 3.60 (SD 0.89, N = 77); t(143) = 4.16, p < .001, d = 0.69. ANCOVA (covariate: prior ChatGPT experience): F(1,142) = 19.07, p <.001, partial eta squared = .12. [p. 16, Table 2 row 4 and H1 text; Ns from p. 11, Table 1]
- Needs authors: Sessions by condition (open question 1): each condition ran in its own sessions of up to five people, so session is confounded with condition, and the paper does not say how sessions were scheduled. Individual random assignment before the experiment is stated (p. 10), so criterion 1 holds. If sessions were the assigned unit, standard errors would need clustering (codebook section 10), which bears on pooling, not eligibility.
- Needs authors: Assignment counts (open question 2): no count at assignment, no randomization mechanism, and no statement on no-shows, here or in c594. Random assignment is stated and 145 people were analyzed (each arm above 50), so criteria 1 and 2 hold either way.
- Needs posted data: Primary outcome (open question 3): the paper names none among quality, elaboration, and originality (H1a-c). The OSF preregistration (osf.io/q6wtu), not opened in this screening, may name one. Otherwise codebook section 8, rule 2 picks originality (S1-O3). Criterion 3 holds for all three outcomes.
- Needs authors: PIT raters (open question 4): neither this paper nor c594 says who the two PIT experts were or how their two ratings became one score. Both raters were experts blind to condition, so criterion 3 holds either way.
- Needs authors: Scoring unit (open question 5): whether one score covers each participant's three solutions or each solution is scored and averaged. The df of 143 put one score per participant in the analysis, so unit stays participant, while c594 says experts rated 'each idea'. Criterion 3 holds either way.
- Needs journal version: Version (open question 8): the version of record (Computers & Education 215, 105031) is not on file and may add data availability, funding, or conflict-of-interest statements. Retrieval found no differences in studies or sample.

**Villarreal, Sachdeva, and Brandimarte (2026)** [c126], "LLM Randomness and Creativity: An Experiment on Human-AI Collaboration". 2026 49th MIPRO ICT and Electronics Convention (MIPRO), Opatija, Croatia. https://doi.org/10.1109/mipro70003.2026.11592010

- Version read: MIPRO 2026 conference proceedings paper, HCI track, printed pages 1770-1775, open copy posted by MIPRO (docs.mipro-proceedings.com). The retrieval record marks it as the latest version. The IEEE Xplore version of record (DOI 10.1109/mipro70003.2026.11592010) was not compared page by page.
- Finding: Neither LLM temperature nor AI availability changed AUT semantic distance (HighRandomness -0.008, SE 0.037; NoAI -0.002, SE 0.044; Table II, PDF p. 4) or DAT scores (not significant and not reported, PDF p. 3), and Table III shows no significant condition coefficient for RAT accuracy after controlling for AI use (HighRandomness -0.074, SE 0.049; NoAI -0.044, SE 0.055; PDF p. 5), though the Discussion reports that AI availability raised RAT accuracy from 45 to 55 percent (p < .05, PDF p. 3).
- `with_ai`, AUT average semantic distance among a participant's own responses (automated_score, unit participant): Table II, Avg Semantic Distance: NoAI -0.002 (0.044), not significant; (Intercept) 0.623 (0.040)***; duration_seconds -0.000 (0.000)***; AI_used 0.040 (0.039). Sign reversed: the coefficient is NoAI relative to Low randomness. The regression controls for AI_used, a post-assignment variable. Regression N not printed. [PDF p. 4 (printed p. 1773), Table II]
- Needs authors: The Table II and III regressions control for AI_used, a post-assignment variable, so their condition coefficients are not intention-to-treat contrasts, and no SDs and almost no arm means of the performance outcomes are printed. This matters for effect sizes and standard errors, not for eligibility, which rests on the assignment, the analyzed count, and how outputs were scored.
- Needs authors: Regression N, unit (participant or participant-round), estimator, and standard-error type are not printed. This matters for codebook Section 10 (standard errors), not for eligibility.
- Needs authors: Whether each AUT and DAT semantic-distance score pools a participant's three rounds or is computed per round (the DAT asks for 10 words per round). The score is computed on the participant's output under either reading, so criterion 3 holds.
- Needs authors: How many of the 185 exclusions happened after randomization, so no count at assignment exists. Criterion 2 counts people analyzed, and 775 is far above 50.
- Needs authors: Unequal task totals (AUT 213, DAT 245, RAT 317) against near-equal cells within each task, with no allocation ratio or exclusions by cell. Every AI contrast compares cells within one task, so random assignment of AI (criterion 1) is not in question.
- Needs authors: Whether the Table II and III significance stars are Holm-adjusted, as the Section IV statement about 'this and all following results' suggests, or plain, as the table notes suggest.
- Needs authors: The AI-user subsample comparisons (AUT 0.51 vs 0.31 and the 'consistent' maximum-distance result; RAT 64 percent against 45 percent without AI; RAT 72 vs 57 percent) condition on post-assignment AI use and print no SE, SD, or n. They are secondary to the assigned-arm contrasts.
- Needs journal version: Version check: confirm that the IEEE Xplore version of record (DOI 10.1109/mipro70003.2026.11592010) matches this open MIPRO copy, which the retrieval record marks as the latest version.

**Wadinambiarachchi et al. (2024)** [c127], "The Effects of Generative AI on Design Fixation and Divergent Thinking". Proceedings of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing Systems (CHI '24), Honolulu, HI, USA, May 11-16, 2024, ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/3613904.3642919

- Version read: arXiv:2403.11164v1 [cs.HC], 17 Mar 2024 (side stamp, p. 1), typeset as the CHI '24 proceedings paper (CHI '24, May 11-16, 2024, Honolulu, HI, USA; DOI 10.1145/3613904.3642919; 18 pages). The file is the arXiv copy, not the ACM Digital Library file. A later version exists.
- Finding: Compared with no support, Midjourney access raised the design fixation score (probit posterior mean .32, SD 0.13, 89% CI [.11, .54]; Table 1, p. 8) and lowered originality by an amount the authors call negligible (-.03, SD .02, 89% CI [-.06, -.01]; Table 4, p. 10), while the fluency (-.21, [-.66, .26]) and variety (-.29, [-.74, .17]) estimates have 89% intervals that include zero (Tables 2-3, p. 9).
- `with_ai`, Design fixation score (DFS): share of the example avatar's 14 salient features repeated in each sketch (expert_raters, unit output): Table 1 (probit scale; posterior mean (posterior SD), 89% compatibility interval): GenAI .32 (0.13) [.11, .54]; Intercept -.71 (.10) [-.86, .56] as printed. Text: 'mean = .32, 89% CI [.11, .55]', '100% probability of leading to higher Design Fixation', 'Bayes Factor of 124'. Higher DFS means more copying of the example (less creative). Figure 6 plots arm-level posterior predictions (median ± S.E.) with no printed values. [p. 8, Section 4.2, Table 1, Figure 6]
- Needs authors: open_questions[2]: the paper prints no participant-level SD, no arm mean except the GenAI sketch total (92), and no GenAI-minus-Image-Search estimate. Standardized effects need digitized Figures 6, 7, and 9 or the authors' numbers. The paper links no data or code.
- Needs supplement: open_questions[3]: how exactly 10 women and 10 men reached each arm (about a 5 percent chance under complete randomization), which points to randomization within gender. Criterion 1 stays met: the paper states random assignment (p. 6), and randomization within strata still qualifies. The full study protocol in the supplementary material (p. 4) may describe the allocation.
- Needs authors: open_questions[4]: no outcome model prints its N, and the paper does not say whether the design fixation model has one row per sketch or one per rater. Criterion 2 rests on 60 people with no reported loss (per-participant scoring, F(2, 57), 117 prompts at 5.85 per GenAI participant), so this bears on standard errors, not on eligibility.
- Needs authors: open_questions[5]: Table 2's fluency levels sit about one sketch per participant below the printed totals (3.63 vs 4.6 per GenAI participant; about 217 vs 277 sketches in all). A model of sketches minus one would fit, but the paper does not say so. The coded directions hold on either reading.
- Needs authors: open_questions[6]: what Figure 8 divides by. Its variety levels are about 2.4 times the Eq. 3 values that Table 3 implies, so Figure 8 should not be digitized for arm levels until the authors explain it.

**Wan and Kalman (2026)** [c128], "Diverse AI personas can mitigate the homogenization effect in human-AI collaborative ideation". Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.chbah.2026.100289

- Version read: arXiv 2504.13868 author manuscript, retrieved from the unversioned URL arxiv.org/pdf/2504.13868 (retrieval.json). The document prints no arXiv version number or date. Page 1 reads '(accepted and forthcoming in Computers in Human Behavior: Artificial Humans)'. Not the journal's typeset version of record (DOI 10.1016/j.chbah.2026.100289, per the search record). The search record lists an arXiv v1 titled 'Using Generative AI Personas Increases Collective Diversity in Human Ideation'; this file carries the later title, so it is not v1. A later version exists.
- Finding: Among 97 analyzed UK Prolific writers (100 randomized), optional access to plot ideas from 10 GPT-4o personas did not raise story similarity: similarity to the condition centroid was 90.588 (no AI), 90.095 (1-plot) and 90.233 (5-plot), F(2, 94) = 0.573, p = 0.57 (Tables 2-3, pp. 12-13), and pairwise similarity was lower, that is diversity higher, in the 1-plot arm (-1.073, 95% CI [-1.971, -0.175], p = 0.019) but not significantly in the 5-plot arm (-0.488, p = 0.291) (Table 4, p. 14).
- `across_people`, Story similarity to the condition centroid (replication of Doshi and Hauser 2024) (embedding_similarity, unit output): Similarity measure, sign reversed for diversity (a negative coefficient means the AI arm is less similar). Table 2: Human with 1 GenAI idea -0.493 (0.5); Constant 90.588*** (0.3); Observations 97; F-Stat 0.57; Adj R-squared -0.009 (values in parentheses printed to one decimal and not labeled). Text: b = -0.493, p = 0.301. Table 3: 1-plot Count 33, Mean 90.095, std 1.864 vs 0-plot Count 32, Mean 90.588, std 2.383. The printed test statistics are omnibus: F-Stat 0.57 (Table 2) and ANOVA F(2, 94) = 0.573, p = 0.57. [p. 12, Section 3.3.1 and Table 2; p. 13, Table 3]
- `with_ai`, Total words per story (NLTK) (automated_score, unit output): Not a randomized contrast as printed: the printed means and t fit groups of 56 and 41 stories, not the assigned arms (S2-O3 measure_detail; open question 1). Table 5, Total Words: Human Mean 144.634, AI-Assisted Mean 158.625, Human Std 55.89, AI-Assisted Std 53.602, t-statistic -1.247, p-value 0.215. Group sizes not printed. [p. 16, Table 5; p. 11, Section 3.1 and p. 13, Table 3 (analyzed arms 32 / 33 / 32)]
- Needs authors: Groups behind Tables 5-6 (open question 1): the printed statistics fit 41 / 56 and 27 / 29 stories, not the assigned arms, so the 14 NLTK contrasts are coded as non-randomized; how the groups were formed is not stated. Eligibility rests on S2-O1 and S2-O2, which compare the assigned arms (32 / 33 / 32 stories; 496 / 528 / 496 pairs).
- Needs authors: Take-up of the plot ideas is not reported, so the AI contrasts are effects of offered access (open question 2). Criterion 1 concerns random assignment of access, which the paper states twice.
- Needs journal version: Story-level ANOVA F = 2.33 in the text but 2.720 in Table S3, whose F and eta-squared imply 94 observations (open question 4). If the journal version does not fix it, the authors.
- Needs journal version: Table 2 prints the centroid-regression values in parentheses to one decimal and unlabeled (open question 5). If the journal version does not give exact SEs, the authors.
- Needs authors: Per-arm counts at assignment are not printed (open question 6); 100 randomized and 97 analyzed (32 / 33 / 32) are.
- Needs authors: The outside-AI verification question appears only after the 0-plot condition, and all 3 exclusions come from the control arm (open question 7).
- Needs authors: The ten-word task on the consent page is not described or reported (open question 9).
- Needs authors: Level of the Bayesian model is not stated (open question 11); arithmetic places it at the story level, and the record labels it so.
- Needs journal version: The journal version of record is not on file (open question 12); it is the latest version under codebook section 2.

**Wong and Qiu (2026)** [c130], "Think First, ChatGPT Later: Guiding Human–AI Collaboration for Learning Gains in Independent Human Creativity". Educational Psychology Review. https://doi.org/10.1007/s10648-026-10118-7

- Version read: Journal version of record: Educational Psychology Review (2026) 38:45, https://doi.org/10.1007/s10648-026-10118-7; received 1 August 2025, accepted 8 January 2026, published online 15 April 2026 (p. 1; the online date is printed at the page foot and is missing from the markdown); open access, CC BY 4.0 (p. 42). The search record lists an NUS bachelor's thesis (Qiu 2025) as an earlier version; this article does not mention it.
- Finding: With ChatGPT available, free use raised expert-rated originality over no AI (4.08 vs 3.06, d = 0.79, p < .001) while guided 'think first, ChatGPT later' use did not (3.30, d = 0.18, p = .90); on the next task, done without AI in the same session, free users matched the no-AI group (2.69 vs 2.63, d = 0.04) and the guided group scored higher than both (3.42; d = 0.52 vs no AI, d = 0.48 vs free use) (p. 20, Fig. 2A on p. 21).
- `with_ai`, Originality of solution on the product improvement task (intervention; ChatGPT available to the two AI arms) (expert_raters, unit participant): general-AI M = 4.08, SD = 1.22 (n = 67) vs human-only M = 3.06, SD = 1.37 (n = 64); p < .001 (Bonferroni), d = 0.79 [p. 20, Main Analyses, Originality on Creative Tasks; Fig. 2A, p. 21; arm ns p. 8]
- `after_ai`, Originality of solution on the post-intervention product invention task (no AI for anyone) (expert_raters, unit participant): general-AI M = 2.69, SD = 1.44 (n = 67) vs human-only M = 2.63, SD = 1.45 (n = 64); p = 1.00 (Bonferroni), d = 0.04 [p. 20, Originality on Creative Tasks; Fig. 2A, p. 21]
- Needs authors: Regulated-AI access to ChatGPT during Phase 3 remains unstated: the paper calls refinement independent, but describes the product improvement task as AI-assisted (pp. 9, 13-14; Appendix A, p. 35). Authors could confirm whether ChatGPT was closed or further use prohibited during this phase. This affects the guided arm's with_ai/after_ai classification, not any core criterion: general-AI versus human-only independently qualifies.
- Needs authors: Counts at randomization and the excluded participant's arm are not reported (p. 8, Participants and Design). Authors' allocation and exclusion records would settle them. Randomized counts remain null; the confirmed 196 people analyzed, with analyzed arm counts 64/67/65, already meet the sample-size criterion (p. 8; Table 4, p. 20).
- Needs authors: Possible participant overlap with the Qiu 2025 NUS thesis is an unverified search-record lead; the journal article does not identify the thesis, and the capstone funding statement alone cannot establish shared participants (p. 41). Author confirmation comparing the thesis and journal samples would settle deduplication. This does not alter any of the journal study's eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: The randomization method and whether sessions mixed conditions are unstated. Participants were described as randomly assigned, but attended lab sessions of up to five (p. 8; pp. 12-13). Authors could clarify individual versus session allocation and any clustering needed for standard errors. This does not negate the reported random assignment or the confirmed number of people analyzed, so it does not change the present eligibility decision.

**Wu et al. (2026)** [c132], "The Role of Human Agency in Human-AI Co-Creativity". Proceedings of the 2026 Conference on Creativity and Cognition (C&C '26), London, United Kingdom, ACM. https://doi.org/10.1145/3803784.3816857

- Version read: PsyArXiv preprint tbvq2_v1 (doi:10.31234/osf.io/tbvq2_v1), posted 2026-07-03 per retrieval.json. The document is an APA-format author manuscript watermarked 'UNDER PEER REVIEW' and prints no date. It is not the version of record: the ACM Creativity & Cognition 2026 paper (doi:10.1145/3803784.3816857, published 2026-07-13) returned HTTP 403, so its body text, sample, and statistics were not compared. - Finding: Evaluator ratings of creativity and originality did not differ significantly by condition (ps > .60, p. 15; Table A2, p. 35), high-agency ideas were less similar to their condition-object centroid than low-agency ideas (cosine similarity -0.050, SE 0.021, Table A3, p. 36; Tukey t(125) = -2.45, p < .05, p. 16), and low-agency ideas formed fewer clusters, had a lower noise ratio, and had more unequal cluster sizes than control ideas (permutation p = .04, .02, and .005, p. 17).

Text: no significant differences in overall idea creativity or originality across the treatment conditions relative to control, ps > .60. [p. 35, Table A2; p. 15, Results, Individual-Level Creativity; p. 30, Figure 1a]
- `across_people`, Homogeneity: cosine similarity of each idea to the leave-one-out condition-object centroid (embedding_similarity, unit output): Table A3 (reference Low-Agency; outcome cosine similarity to centroid): (Intercept) 0.441*** (0.015); Control -0.014 (0.019), no star; Num.Obs. 337. No Tukey test for this pair is printed; the text says low-agency and control participants 'produced ideas that were similarly homogeneous'. Direction coded on diversity (similarity sign reversed). [p. 36, Table A3; p. 16, Results, Homogeneity; p. 31, Figure 2]
- Needs journal version: Analyzed and per-arm counts of people, and the trials kept per arm, are not printed (128 analyzed by subtraction; Table A3 needs at least 113 people). Extraction needs them for n_treatment and n_control. If the version of record does not print them, the authors can.
- Needs journal version: Number randomized (also the unresolved reconciliation difference on participants.n_randomized): no count at assignment is given, the timing of the attention check is not stated, and no other attrition is reported. Criterion 2 counts people analyzed, which exceed 50 under any reading. Fallbacks: the OSF preregistration or the authors.
- Needs journal version: Sample of the five 'idea data logging' exclusions is not named (placement suggests evaluators). Criterion 2 holds under any reading.
- Needs journal version: Usefulness, Low-Agency vs Control: the p. 15 'β = 0.20, p = .04' names no comparison group and matches no Table A2 coefficient. It is coded no_clear_difference from Table A2, the abstract, and the Discussion. This is a conflict in the results, not in eligibility. Fallback: the authors.
- Needs journal version: Clustering and centroid inputs: the paper does not say which ideas entered HDBSCAN and the leave-one-out centroid (all submitted ideas or only the sampled ones), or which embedding model the clustering used. This matters for comparing diversity effects, not for eligibility. Fallback: the authors.
- Needs journal version: Setting (remote or supervised) is not stated. design_class online_rct is provisional, and lab_rct applies if the session was supervised. Both codes are core.
- Needs journal version: Version: the form rests on the PsyArXiv preprint. The ACM Creativity & Cognition 2026 version of record (HTTP 403) was not compared, and the codebook takes the latest version as the paper. It reports the same experiment with the same abstract, so eligibility is not expected to change, but every number should be checked against it.

**Wu, Aridor, and Timoshenko (2026)** [c131], "Guided Creativity: AI Intermediation for Enhancing Originality and Quality in Visual Design". Working paper (SSRN 5371575; revision posted on author website). https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.5371575

- Version read: Working paper dated March 2, 2026 (title page; the Web Appendix title page carries the same date), from coauthor Artem Timoshenko's website; newer than the 2025 SSRN posting (SSRN 5371575). Main text and Appendix A-E occupy PDF pp. 1-44 (printed p. n = PDF p. n+1); the Web Appendix occupies PDF pp. 45-69 (WA-k = PDF p. 44+k).
- Finding: In the main logo contest (292 analyzed designers), AI variations increased mean click-attractiveness quality versus Blind (beta_Var - beta_Blind = 0.0665, SE 0.0197) and top-50 submission originality versus human exemplars (beta_Open - beta_Var = -0.0139, SE 0.0053), with no clear quality difference versus human exemplars or originality difference versus Blind, and the restaurant replication (208 analyzed) showed the same direction pattern (PDF pp. 20 and 29, Tables 1-2; PDF p. 64, Tables WA-C.3/WA-C.4; PDF pp. 68-69, Tables WA-D.4/WA-D.5).
- `with_ai`, Embedding-based originality of high-quality submissions (embedding_similarity, unit output): Top 50 each condition (Table WA-C.2, N = 200): Variation(1) 0.0512*** (0.0078), Blind 0.0547*** (0.0068). Table WA-C.4: beta_Var - beta_Blind = -0.0035 (0.0054). Text: Delta Originality Variation, Blind = -0.004, p = 0.516 (Figure 6). Robustness, beta_Var - beta_Blind: top 100 each -0.0049 (0.0045); top 200 in all -0.0009 (0.0054); top 400 in all -0.0048 (0.0042). [PDF p. 23 (printed p. 22), Section 4.3; PDF p. 24 (printed p. 23), Figure 6; PDF p. 63 (WA-19), Table WA-C.2; PDF p. 64 (WA-20), Table WA-C.4]
- Needs posted data: Original question 3, distinct designer counts in top-quality originality subsets and selection by quality: participant-linked submission data would identify each subset. Criterion 2 is already met by full-sample scored quality (PDF pp. 62-63 and 67-68).
- Needs authors: Original question 6, exact Study 2 assignment timing: authors could confirm implementation beyond the stated use of the Section 4 design and randomization checks. Random assignment is supported, and Study 1 independently meets criterion 1 (PDF p. 29).
- Needs authors: Original question 7, designer overlap across contests: authors should confirm overlap before independent sample_ids or pooled sample totals are assigned. Each study independently exceeds 50 analyzed people (PDF pp. 20 and 29).
- Needs posted data: Original question 8, three displayed condition terms versus Study 1 sample sizes consistent with four arms: analysis data/code would identify the fitted specification and omitted Variation(4) coefficient. This affects extraction, not randomized design, people counts or output scoring (PDF pp. 23 and 62-63).
- Needs authors: AI-provenance disclosure to designers: authors could confirm what participants were told; awareness is not an eligibility requirement for ideas_supplied (PDF pp. 18 and 36).
- Needs authors: Appendix E Variation arm composition and all-submission N: authors must identify included arm(s) and counts. Pooling is plausible but unconfirmed; secondary alignment is not needed for eligibility (PDF pp. 43-44).
- Needs authors: Final quality-rating survey design and rater counts: authors could supply the outcome protocol distinct from feedback. Survey-scored participant output is explicit, so criterion 3 is met (PDF pp. 5, 18-20 and 65-66).
- Needs authors: Replication quality p-values versus coefficient/SE pairs: authors should reconcile Figure 11B and Table WA-D.5 and identify the testing procedure. This affects inference and extraction, not eligibility (PDF pp. 30 and 69).
- Needs posted data: Perception-based originality sample membership, output N and designer N: participant-linked rating data would settle missing counts. Eligibility is established by other scored outcomes (PDF pp. 37-39).
- Needs posted data: Fluency arm means/SDs and numerical tests, especially the unreported Study 2 submission-depth comparison: participant-level submission records would permit extraction. Missing statistics do not affect eligibility (PDF pp. 20, 29 and 36).

- Needs authors: S2 continue-option scorer (open_questions and reconciliation studies.S2.outcomes.S2-O3.measure, retained in notes): authors must identify the coding procedure for the continuation counts and shares in Table G.2 (PDF pp. 112, 117). Retain measure = null with elaboration confirmed by Table 6 col. 2 (PDF p. 42). Other confirmed output outcomes satisfy criterion 3.
- Needs posted data: S2 Gender coding (open_questions and reconciliation studies.S2.participants.who.gender_description): a data dictionary defining the Gender indicator in Table G.2 would establish whether it denotes female participants (PDF p. 117). - Finding: Following the discussion, creative-writing gains showed no clear advantage from AI teammates: H2_S versus H3 beta = 0.336, CR2 SE = 0.184, p = .074, Holm p = 1.00; the other creative AI-versus-H3 contrasts also had Holm p = 1.00 (p. 52, Table 28).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: No reuse of another paper's participants is stated; the main paper describes new Prolific recruitment. The independent pilot explicitly does not overlap the main analysis sample.
- `after_ai`, Creative-writing individual performance gain (composite) (expert_raters, unit participant): Primary full-sample model, Creative task: beta = 0.336, CR2 SE = 0.184, Satterthwaite df = 61, p = .074, Holm p = 1.00. H2_S EMM = 0.212, 95% CI [-0.049, 0.472], SE = 0.133, df = 519; H3 EMM = -0.124, 95% CI [-0.336, 0.089], SE = 0.108, df = 210. These CIs are for the marginal means, not for their difference. BDI-controlled variant: beta = 0.434, CR2 SE = 0.193, Satterthwaite df = 61, p = .028, Holm p = .475. Table 2 analyzed people: H2_S n = 57, H3 n = 90. [p. 52, Table 28, CR2-robust condition row (Creative reference task); p. 53, Table 29, Creative EMM rows; p. 54, Table 31, BDI-controlled CR2 row; p. 6, Table 2, Creative Writing column (analyzed people)]
- `with_ai`, Group discussion quality during creative ideation (composite) (expert_raters, unit team): Primary standardized discussion-quality model, Creative task: beta = 0.590, CR2 SE = 0.166, Satterthwaite df = 67, p = .001, Holm p = .012. H2_S EMM = 0.712, 95% CI [0.395, 1.028], SE = 0.161, df = 554; H3 EMM = 0.121, 95% CI [-0.200, 0.443], SE = 0.164, df = 554. These CIs are for the marginal means, not for their difference. BDI-controlled variant: beta = 0.587, CR2 SE = 0.169, Satterthwaite df = 68, p < .001, Holm p = .016. [p. 47, Table 24, CR2-robust condition row (Creative reference task); p. 48, Table 25, Creative EMM rows; p. 49, Table 26, BDI-controlled CR2 row]
- Needs authors: The underlying generative model, provider, version and sampling settings are not identified (pp. 7-8, sections 3.4-3.5; pp. 29-31, Appendix B). Author-supplied implementation details would settle this. Criterion 1 is already met by the documented generative-AI teammate intervention and random assignment; a model identifier is not needed to establish it.
- Needs supplement: The coded-exemplar supplementary dataset and external XLSX moderation outputs are named without an access URL and are absent from the supplied PDF (p. 10, section 3.7; pp. 56-58, Appendix G). Obtaining those supplements would settle their contents and availability. The PDF itself establishes assignment, analyzed people counts and scored creative outputs, so no eligibility criterion depends on them.
- Needs authors: The exact aggregation of creative-writing rubric dimensions and the reference population/denominator for standardizing performance gain remain unspecified (pp. 9-13, sections 3.7-3.11; pp. 51-55, Appendix F). The authors' scoring and standardization specification would settle this. Criterion 3 is met because originality, elaboration and coherence are scored on participants' submissions regardless of the unresolved aggregation details.
- Needs posted data: Raw arm means/SDs and separate treatment effects for originality, elaboration, coherence and discussion components are not printed (p. 11, Table 6; p. 33, Tables 8-9; pp. 47-55, Tables 24-33). Posted participant/group-level ratings with task, condition, phase and group identifiers would permit their recovery. These missing extraction statistics do not affect any eligibility criterion; the PDF reports qualifying output scores and composite contrasts.
- Needs authors: Methods define BDI as Balanced Detection Index (pp. 10-11, section 3.8), while Appendix E calls it baseline depression (p. 38) and Appendix G calls it baseline depressive symptoms (p. 56). Authors must confirm the variable used in the adjusted/moderation analyses. No eligibility criterion depends on this discrepancy, and the primary creative-performance model does not condition on BDI (p. 52, Table 28).
- Needs supplement: Table 37 prints the exploratory H2_S minus H2_C discussion-quality estimate as 3.52 (SE 0.12) for every task and both BDI levels (p. 57). Its transcription is confirmed, but its repeated values and scale remain unverified. SI_GDQ_CR2_BDI_CR2.xlsx, named in section G.5, is needed to check the intended estimates. This secondary reporting issue does not affect eligibility, which is independently established by the individual movie-plot scores.

**Yuan, Liu, and Yang (2026)** [c141], "The Impact of Generative AI Dependence on University Students’ Creativity: The Mediating Role of Critical Thinking and the Moderating Role of Thinking Orientation". Journal of Intelligence. https://doi.org/10.3390/jintelligence14080168

- Version read: Journal version of record: J. Intell. 2026, 14, 168 (MDPI, CC BY license), DOI 10.3390/jintelligence14080168. Received 6 June 2026, revised 15 July 2026, accepted 27 July 2026, published 1 August 2026 (p. 1).
- Finding: In Study 2, students randomly told to adopt ChatGPT's AUT solutions directly scored lower on rater-scored creativity than students told to use ChatGPT only as a supplementary reference (4.78 vs 5.20, t(130) = 4.03, p < 0.001, d = 0.70; p. 6, Table 2), and in Study 3 students assigned to high AI dependence through a trust-game instruction scored lower under both thinking-orientation primes (Table 4, Model 2: B = -1.35, SE = 0.09; cell means 2.99 vs 4.52 outcome-oriented and 4.40 vs 5.55 process-oriented; p. 9).
- `with_ai`, Creativity index on the AUT (CAT, mean of three raters) (expert_raters, unit participant): Creativity: High AI Dependence 4.78 (0.66) vs Low AI Dependence 5.20 (0.53), SDs in parentheses; t(130) = 4.03, p < 0.001, Cohen's d = 0.70 (Table 2 prints t and d as low minus high); 66 randomized and 66 analyzed per group (p. 5; no exclusions reported). Mediation via critical thinking: total effect c = -0.42, direct effect c' = -0.02, indirect effect ab = -0.40, bootstrap 95% CI [-0.70, -0.14]. [p. 6, Section 3.3 Creativity performance, Table 2, and Mediation analysis; group ns p. 5]
- Needs supplement: Study 3: whether participants used ChatGPT or any AI on the Phase 3 AUT, and under which instructions (open question 1, and the one unresolved reconciliation difference, studies[2].ai_tool.role: A other, B open_chat). The text says only that the AUT was 'identical to the AUT used in Study 2' (p. 8). Until it is settled, the S3 arms' ai, its ai_tool.role, S3-O1's AI availability and question, and its contrasts' comparison type stay not_stated. It decides Study 3's criterion 1, and so whether Study 3 (160 analyzed) is a second core sample; the paper is core through Study 2 either way.
- Needs supplement: Study 3: the trust-game 'AI investment advice' (p. 7) is not described; whether it comes from a generative AI, and whether the low-dependence group saw the advice, are not stated (open question 2). It describes the Study 3 arms but decides no criterion, because the trust game is not a creative task. If the supplement's Study 3 instructions do not say, the authors.
- Needs supplement: Study 3 setting (laboratory vs online) is not stated; lab_rct is kept by analogy to Study 2's laboratory procedure (open question 3). Every randomized design class counts for criterion 1, so the setting changes only the design code. If the supplement's Study 3 instructions do not say, the authors.
- Needs posted data: Study 3 process-oriented SDs (0.19 and 0.25) and d = 5.09 (p. 9) look implausible for CAT ratings, and the analyzed cell counts are not printed (t(77) for the 81 people randomized to the outcome-oriented cells) (open question 8). They bear only on Study 3's effect sizes.

- Needs posted data: Unique people analyzed across AUT and PIT and corresponding arm totals (open_questions[0]; reconciliation.differences[0], studies[0].arms[0].n_analyzed). Authors' analysis specification and numerical results would settle what Supplementary Figure 1 omits (p. 39). Authors' effect-size definition and numerical summaries or analysis outputs would settle these omissions. They affect effect extraction and precision, not any eligibility criterion.
- Needs journal version: Correspondence of the undated English manuscript with the journal version in the search record (open_questions[4]; p. 1). Compare the journal version, particularly its Chinese methods and results, with the saved translation; the document says the Chinese version prevails. This is a version-verification question, with no demonstrated discrepancy affecting the three criteria established in the saved full text.

- Finding: Creativity judgments of FIERO's bundled card-game/AI condition differed by evaluator (net advantage: Claude -27.2%, Gemini 50.6%, GPT-5.2 -7.4%), while overall-preference net advantages favored FIERO (45.7-65.4%; p. 29, Figure 14).

This affects extraction of the overall result, not any eligibility criterion; the creativity outcome remains an output score.
- Needs posted data: Uncertainty accounting for repeated story use: posted story-level judgments with story and team identifiers would permit an analysis that respects the dependence among comparisons (p. 17, Section 4.5.2; pp. 27-29, Section 5.4). This is a reporting-validation gap and does not establish that the explicitly reported study-level analyzed N=60 is below the eligibility threshold; no replacement N is inferred.

(January 18, 2024), p. 30. The retrieval record lists no other versions.
- Finding: Compared with participants assigned to Google Search, those assigned to ChatGPT submitted more valid ideas (3.533 vs 1.533, F = 27.65), ideas rated more novel (z-scores 0.010 vs -0.057, F = 4.88, p < 0.05) but less functionally appropriate (0.055 vs 0.213, F = 10.77, p < 0.01), and ideas more semantically divergent within their condition (0.134 vs 0.123, F = 25.68, p < 0.01) (Table 3, p. 20).

Text p. 19: 'participants in the LLM condition generated more diverse ideas than those in the Search Engine condition (0.134 vs. 0.123, p < 0.01)'. Higher = more divergent, so no sign reversal. No arm SDs. Table 2's Divergent Thinking row (Obs 67, mean -0.018, median -0.008, STD 0.126) repeats the Functional Appropriateness row, so no valid pooled SD is printed. Not the arm contrast: Table A5 ChatGPT coefficient conditional on one mediator, .0107992 (SE .0038407, p 0.007) with Information Volume. [p. 20, Table 3 Panel C; p. 19 text; p. 17, Table 2; p. 37, Table A5]

**Zhou and Kim (2027)** [c148], "Long-term impact of ChatGPT-4 on music students' creative and analytical skills: A follow-up study". Thinking Skills and Creativity. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.tsc.2026.102344

- Finding: Among 74 music students analyzed in a study reporting class-level random assignment, the ChatGPT-4 group had higher adjusted expert-rated creativity than the traditional-instruction group (4.71 vs 4.35; F(1, 69) = 7.216, p = .009, η² = .096; p. 12, Tables 5-6) and a steeper reported creativity growth slope (0.315 vs 0.210; p. 9, Table 3).
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Zhou, W., & Kim, Y. (2024). Innovative music education: An empirical assessment of ChatGPT-4's impact on student learning experiences. Education and Information Technologies, 29, 20855-20881. Same cohort. The introduction describes that study as having '74 undergraduate music students in Korea' (p. 2). The present paper collects new data in a new 24-week intervention and does not reanalyze the earlier data. In the earlier study only the AI-condition students received structured ChatGPT-supported learning, so prior ChatGPT use is a covariate here. Whether students kept their earlier condition is not stated.
- `with_ai`, Creativity rubric score (expert-rated) of submitted creative products (expert_raters, unit participant): Follow-up univariate test after the MANCOVA (covariates: prior ChatGPT use, Week 1 creativity, Week 1 analytical), Group on Creativity: df1 = 1, df2 = 69, F = 7.216, p = .009, η² = .096 (Table 5). Adjusted means: Experimental 4.71 (Std. Error 0.18, N = 36) vs Control 4.35 (Std. Error 0.17, N = 38) (Table 6). Multivariate Group effect on creativity and analytical skills together: Pillai's Trace = .047, df1 = 2, df2 = 68, F = 3.982, p = .023, η² = .047 (Table 4). The paper prints η² without saying whether it is partial. [p. 9, Results RQ2; p. 11, Table 4; p. 12, Tables 5 and 6]
- Needs authors: open_questions[0]: Class counts, allocation implementation, and class-adjusted uncertainty are unreported (pp. 5, 8-9, 12). Authors' allocation records, class identifiers, and statistical output would settle these. On the paper's explicit random-assignment report, these missing details do not overturn criterion 1; the supplied rules contain no minimum class count.
- Needs authors: open_questions[1]: The MANCOVA outcome's time point or aggregation is unspecified (p. 9). An author description of the dependent variable and analysis script would settle extraction; repeated expert-rated creative outputs already establish criterion 3.
- Needs authors: open_questions[2]: Week 1 baseline timing relative to ChatGPT access is ambiguous (pp. 5-6, 9). An assessment schedule would settle it; later creative products were scored during AI-supported coursework regardless.
- Needs authors: open_questions[3]: The cited baseline balance tests are absent, while initial group levels differ (pp. 1, 5-6, 9). Original baseline test output would settle the reporting discrepancy. Baseline balance is not one of the three eligibility requirements.
- Needs authors: open_questions[4]: Weekly and biweekly timing labels conflict (pp. 2, 5, 7-10). Dated assessment records and an explanation of the figure labels would settle the schedule; the participant count and output-scoring criteria remain met.
- Needs authors: open_questions[5]: Earlier-condition membership and its relation to current allocation are unspecified (pp. 5, 8). Authors' participant/class allocation crosswalk would settle this. The same-cohort overlap is recorded, and the present paper explicitly reports current class randomization.
- Needs authors: open_questions[6]: Participant-level arm SDs and class identifiers are unavailable in the PDF (p. 12, Table 6); data are available on request (p. 17). Authors' data and code would permit appropriate effect/precision extraction, which is separate from the three screening criteria.
- Needs authors: open_questions[7]: Rubric/rater aggregation and output-score inter-rater reliability are unspecified (pp. 6-7). The scoring protocol and individual ratings would settle these measurement details. The paper explicitly establishes expert scoring of creative output.
- Needs authors: open_questions[8]: The printed Group Pillai trace and F statistic are inconsistent (p. 11, Table 4). Authors' original or corrected statistical output would settle which values to extract. This does not negate the reported allocation, 74 analyzed people, or expert-rated creative products.

**Zhou et al. (2026)** [c012], "Creative scar without generative AI: Individual creativity fails to sustain while homogeneity keeps climbing". Technology in Society. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.techsoc.2025.103087

- Version read: Undated preprint: the document shows no date, venue, or version number (PDF metadata creation date 2024-01-11). The search record gives it as arXiv 2401.06816 and, as updated on 2026-10-04, treats it as an earlier version of the journal article Zhou et al. (2026), 'Creative scar without generative AI: Individual creativity fails to sustain while homogeneity keeps climbing', Technology in Society 84, 103087. The journal version is not the document screened here. A later version exists.
- Finding: With ChatGPT on Days 2 to 6, the treatment group scored higher on AUT novelty (Day 6: 39.84 vs 14.80; t = -4.84, P 0.000; Table 2a p. 8, Table 1 p. 11) and on the expert rating of solution novelty ('Creativity' in Table 2b), but not on Day 7 without ChatGPT (AUT novelty 17.60 vs 13.02, P 0.053; solution novelty P 0.122; Tables 2a and 2b p. 8, Table 1 pp. 11-12), while its AUT answers stayed more similar at Day 30 (SBERT 0.39 vs 0.33, p ≤ .001; p. 14, Table 3 p. 16).
- `with_ai`, AUT (Task 1) novelty, Days 2 to 6 (ChatGPT available to the treatment group) (human_raters, unit participant): Table 2a novelty, Control vs GPT: Day 2 30.77 vs 41.55; Day 3 28.37 vs 40.58; Day 4 17.17 vs 35.32; Day 5 18.17 vs 41.44; Day 6 14.80 vs 39.84. Table 1 (Estimate = Control minus GPT, Statistic, P Value): Day 2 -10.78, -2.21, 0.031 (*); Day 3 -12.21, -2.60, 0.012 (*); Day 4 -18.16, -3.51, 0.001 (**); Day 5 -23.27, -4.34, 0.000 (***); Day 6 -25.04, -4.84, 0.000 (***). Figure 2a P: 0.031, 0.012, 0.0012, 1e-04, 2.4e-05. Baseline Day 1 (no AI in either arm): 7.50 vs 8.78; -1.28, -0.90, 0.373 (ns). No SDs or per-day ns printed; Figure 2a bars carry error bars that the paper does not define. [p. 8, Table 2a; p. 11, Table 1; p. 9, Figure 2a]
- `across_people`, Semantic similarity (SBERT) of AUT answers within each group, Days 2 to 6 (ChatGPT available to the treatment group) (embedding_similarity, unit pair): Direction refers to diversity: GPT similarity is higher. Table 3 Task 1 SBERT, Control vs GPT: Day 2 0.39 vs 0.48; Day 3 0.32 vs 0.48; Day 4 0.3 vs 0.45; Day 5 0.48 vs 0.59; Day 6 0.38 vs 0.53 (Day 1 0.36 vs 0.35, n.s. in the text). Text p. 14: 'the similarity of MC = [0.3, 0.48], the similarity of ME = [0.35, 59], ps ≤ .01'. String matching, Control vs GPT: Day 2 2.73 vs 5.9; Day 3 3.17 vs 8.24; Day 4 3.33 vs 7.12; Day 5 3.4 vs 8.73; Day 6 3.13 vs 7.95; text p. 15: 'the redundancy of MC = [3.13, 3.4], the redundancy of ME = [5.90, 8.73], ps ≤ .01'. Table 4a, Control vs GPT: SD Day 2 0.40 vs 0.33, Day 3 0.34 vs 0.41, Day 4 0.37 vs 0.32, Day 5 0.36 vs 0.33, Day 6 0.37 vs 0.32; sum of eigenvalues 0.16 vs 0.11, 0.12 vs 0.17, 0.14 vs 0.10, 0.13 vs 0.11, 0.14 vs 0.10; ellipse area 0.24 vs 0.14, 0.17 vs 0.14, 0.22 vs 0.16, 0.19 vs 0.17, 0.21 vs 0.14. [p. 14, Section 3.2; p. 15, text; p. 16, Table 3; p. 17, Table 4a; pp. 18-19, Figure 4a]
- `after_ai`, AUT (Task 1) novelty after ChatGPT was removed, Day 7 and Day 30 (human_raters, unit participant): Day 7: Table 2a Control 13.02 vs GPT 17.60; Table 1 Estimate -4.58, Statistic -1.98, P Value 0.053 (ns); Figure 2a Day 7: ns. [p. 8, Table 2a; p. 11, Table 1; p. 9, Figure 2a]
- Needs journal version: Version: c012 is the 2026 Technology in Society article (Zhou et al., 84, 103087), but the PDF screened is the 2024 arXiv preprint 2401.06816. Unverified search notes give the journal version 3,593 ideas, 427 solutions, and two follow-ups (preprint: 3302 ideas, 427 solutions, one follow-up). The randomization, the 61 people, and the rated outputs belong to the same seven-day experiment, so the journal version matters for the extracted numbers, not for the three criteria.
- Needs journal version: Day 30 AUT novelty (open question on Day 30 novelty and the unresolved reconciliation difference on S1-O9): Table 1 gives Estimate -3.69, Statistic -2.12, P 0.038, but the Table 2a means are 2.40 vs 2.56 and the text reports no significant difference. The direction treatment_higher is provisional. If the journal version keeps the conflict, the authors or posted data.
- Needs journal version: Day 30 AUT usefulness: the Table 1 estimate (-6.26) does not equal the Table 2a difference (5.62 vs 5.55).
- Needs journal version: Day 7 Task 2 usefulness is significant in Table 1 and Figure 2b (P 0.021), but the text says all Day 7 p-values were insignificant.
- Needs journal version: Task 2 rating (1) is 'creativity' in Section 2.3 and Table 2b but 'novelty' in Table 1 and Figure 2b. The final form codes it novelty. The answer decides whether a Task 2 outcome becomes the primary with_ai record. Criterion 3 holds either way.
- Needs journal version: How Task 2 'usefulness' was rated: it appears in Table 1 and Figure 2b but not among the four ratings of Section 2.3 or in Table 2b. If the journal version does not say, the authors.
- Needs journal version: Which pairs of answers enter SBERT similarity and string matching, and which test, on what units, gave 'ps ≤ .01' and 'p ≤ .001'. A diversity standard error needs it (codebook sections 10 and 11). If the journal version does not say, the authors or posted data.
- Needs journal version: Figures 2a and 2b have error bars the paper does not define, and participant-level arm SDs or SEs of the creativity scores are not printed (pp. 8-12 and 28-30). Tables 4a and 4b print SDs only for the projected semantic embeddings (pp. 16-17). Participant-level arm SDs of the creativity scores are the preferred standardizer (codebook section 9).
- Needs journal version: How many people took the Day 30 follow-up survey, by arm. Criterion 2 rests on the 61 analyzed for Days 1 to 7, so this count matters only for the Day 30 records.
- Needs posted data: The preregistration (osf.io/hea8r) is not described in the paper, and no primary outcome is named. The OSF page would settle it.

**Zhou et al. (2026)** [c147], "Group-AI Collaboration Enhances Creativity Performance: The Roles of Perspective-Taking and AI Utilisation Strategies". Journal of Computer Assisted Learning. https://doi.org/10.1002/jcal.70223

- Version read: EdArXiv preprint, version 2 (DOI 10.35542/osf.io/5a3mn_v2, posted 2025-08-04; file dated 2025-07-29), per the retrieval record. The PDF itself carries no date or DOI; a note on p. 1 says the manuscript is under double-blind review at a journal. The journal version of record (Journal of Computer Assisted Learning, 10.1002/jcal.70223, 2026-03-09) is not on file. A later version exists.
- Finding: Pairs in which each member had ChatGPT-4o produced ideas that two LLM judges rated more useful than human-only pairs (M = 4.09 vs 3.24; β = 0.84, SE = 0.04) and than people working alone with ChatGPT-4o (3.34; β = 0.74, SE = 0.06), and more original (3.68 vs 2.74 and 2.70; β = 0.94, SE = 0.05 and β = 0.98, SE = 0.07), while fluency did not differ and working alone with AI did not differ from human-only pairs (p > 0.05) (pp. 15-16, Section 3.1; p. 17, Figure 3).
- `with_ai`, Creative usefulness (automated_score, unit None): Group-AI M = 4.09 (SD = 0.35) vs human-only group M = 3.24 (SD = 0.31); fixed effect β = 0.84, SE = 0.04, p < 0.001. Separate PROCESS mediation analysis: total effect G-AI vs G on CU 0.85*** (SE 0.07) (Figure 5A). The numbers of observations underlying these means and models are not printed; pair-condition aggregation is unconfirmed. The arm-level people counts are not outcome-level sample sizes. [p. 15, Section 3.1; p. 17, Figure 3A; p. 21, Figure 5A (caption p. 22)]
- Needs posted data: Creativity-score aggregation, model observation counts, and dependence adjustment, including the unconfirmed participant-unit item in reconciliation.agreed_fields_checked. Affects precision, not the three eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: Exact condition-order randomization method. Criterion 1 is already supported by the assignment text and Figure 1B; authors could supply sequence allocation.
- Needs authors: Precise exclusion stage, counts at assignment, and handling of excluded participants' partners. The 96 analyzed people already establish criterion 2.
- Needs posted data: Covariates in the mixed-effects models and the source of differences from the separate PROCESS total effects. Model code and data would resolve this extraction issue.
- Needs supplement: Whether Supplementary Figure 2A/2B are separate material or references to main-text Figure 2. The references concern coding schemes, not missing eligibility evidence or outcome statistics.
- Needs journal version: Post-review changes and internal numerical discrepancies in the saved preprint. The journal version would permit comparison; all three eligibility criteria are supported by the saved preprint.

**Zhu and Zou (2026)** [c150], "Generative AI adoption in human creative tasks: Experimental evidence". Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jebo.2026.107414

- Version read: Journal version of record: Journal of Economic Behavior and Organization 242 (2026) 107414, doi 10.1016/j.jebo.2026.107414; received 21 May 2025, revised 31 October 2025, accepted 1 January 2026, available online 15 January 2026 (p. 1). The document cites one earlier working paper of the project, Zhu and Zou (2023), 'The role of generative AI in human creative processes: experimental evidence', SSRN 4676053, which reported wave 1 with two evaluators (p. 2, fn. 2; p. 22). The search record (candidates.json other_versions) also lists an SSRN working paper 5196748 (2025); the document does not mention it.
- Finding: Access to ChatGPT 3.5 raised the image-text creativity grade of students' picture ideas by 0.24 points on a 1-10 scale (0.32 SDs, SE 0.062, p < .01; control mean 5.93) relative to working alone (p. 7; Table 3 col 2, p. 8), while the text-only effect was 0.090 (SE 0.094) and not significant.
- Sample overlap, as the form records it: Zhu and Zou (2023), 'The role of generative AI in human creative processes: experimental evidence', SSRN 4676053: the earlier working paper on the wave-1 participants, graded by two evaluators (p. 2, fn. 2; p. 22). The search record also lists SSRN 5196748 (2025) as a version of this paper; the document does not mention it. Holzner, Maier, and Feuerriegel (2025), a meta-analysis, includes this study (p. 3, fn. 3).
- `with_ai`, Creativity grade, image-text evaluation (mean of 30 evaluators, 1-10) (human_raters, unit output): Table 2 Panel A, non-standardized grades: Control 5.93 (2.30), 116 submissions; HAT 6.18 (2.20), 116 submissions. Standardized grades: Control -0.14 (1.03), HAT -0.00 (1.00). Table 3, HAT: col (1) no controls 0.260*** (0.052), N = 442; col (2) unbalanced controls 0.240*** (0.062), N = 548; col (3) full controls 0.229*** (0.060), N = 548. Text: 'improves creative performance by 0.24 points (0.32 SDs, p < .01) relative to the control treatment, whose mean grade is 5.93 points.' Mann-Whitney U tests p < .01 or close. Table 2 does not say what its SDs are computed over (see open questions). [p. 8, Table 2 Panel A and Table 3 cols 1-3; p. 7, Section 3.2; p. 2, Section 1]
- Needs supplement: Assignment unit (original open question 1; assignment challenge): random assignment is explicit, but individual versus session allocation is unresolved (pp. 2, 6, 19). The supplementary allocation protocol or preregistration must state how people entered treatment-specific sessions. This affects the design detail and precision, not S1 criterion 1 on the reported evidence.
- Needs authors: Training randomization contradiction (original open question 2): Appendix B.2 calls all four groups randomly distributed, whereas pp. 3 and 5 describe a separately recruited second wave and caution against causal comparisons. Authors must reconcile these accounts and describe allocation across waves. This concerns S2 criterion 1; paper-level core eligibility is already secured by S1.
- Needs authors: Table 2 SD aggregation (original open question 3 and the unresolved reconciliation difference): the level over which the displayed SDs are computed is not established sufficiently for standardization (p. 8, Table 2; p. 18, Appendix C). Authors must identify whether the SDs describe individual ratings, idea means or another aggregation and provide the relevant standardizer. No SD level or new standardized effect is inferred.
- Needs authors: Table 5 underlying grade and aggregation (uncertainty within original open question 7): the source does not identify image-text versus text-only grades or the exact observations used for the CV and Gini (p. 10). Authors must specify these definitions and any uncertainty estimates; the principal creativity outcomes already establish criterion 3.
- Needs supplement: DAT AI availability (three outcome challenges): the original and modified DAT were repeated after Questionnaire II, but availability of ChatGPT during those tests is unstated (pp. 4, 16). Supplementary test instructions must establish whether AI was unavailable. This settles with_ai versus after_ai classification, not the paper’s three eligibility criteria.
- Needs authors: Exact DAT coefficients, SEs and analyzed samples: Figure 4b supplies unlabeled estimates and 95% intervals; Section 3.4 prints only the 0.19-SD modified-DAT gain for HAT/HHT (pp. 11-12). Authors must provide regression tables with exact coefficients, SEs and per-arm analyzed people counts. These are extraction gaps, not barriers to the eligible picture-task comparison.
- Needs authors: Distinct-person counts for image-text analyses: Table 2 reports submissions and Appendix B.2 describes missing images and grades, but neither gives the exact outcome-specific people count by arm (pp. 8, 17). Authors must provide an outcome-level participant flow or identifiers linking retained submissions to people. Criterion 2 is already met by the text-only grade cohort.
- Needs supplement: Scope of preregistration for Training: the paper refers to the preregistration and deviations in Supplementary Section 1 without establishing whether wave 2 was included (p. 6). Inspect that section to confirm scope; preregistration is not an eligibility criterion.

**Čok et al. (2026)** [c101], "Evaluating TRIZ with and without LLM support: an experimental study on engineering problem-solving". Proceedings of the Design Society (DESIGN 2026). https://doi.org/10.1017/pds.2026.10409

- Version read: Journal version of record: Proceedings of the Design Society, Volume 6: DESIGN 2026, pp. 507-516, https://doi.org/10.1017/pds.2026.10409 (header of p. 507; page numbers 507-516 in the footers). The document prints no license or open-access statement.
- Finding: In a two-way ANOVA of expert-rated design quality (1-7), the LLM's main effect across the three problems was not significant (F(1, 108) = 3.21, p = .076), and method interacted with problem (F(2, 108) = 7.58, p = .001): the LLM raised quality on the general WT problem (6.2 vs 5.0, F(1, 108) = 34.67, p < .001) but not on the specialized GT (4.6 vs 4.9, p = .479) or MB (4.2 vs 4.2, p = .809) problems (p. 512, Section 4.2 and Table 2).
- `with_ai`, Design quality (expert-rated; mean of feasibility, requirements, innovativeness, elaboration) (expert_raters, unit participant): Two-way ANOVA (Problem x Method), Method main effect: F(1, 108) = 3.21, p = .076, 'which is not significant'. Problem x Method interaction F(2, 108) = 7.58, p = .001. Problem main effect F(2, 108) = 27.89, p < .001. Levene's test F(5, 108) = 2.88, p = .018. The paper prints no arm means for this test. The Total rows of Table 2 (TRIZ-C 4.8, SD 0.90, N 51 vs TRIZ-C+LLM 5.6, SD 1.13, N 63) weight each problem by its cell size, and WT, the highest-scoring problem, is 30 of 51 TRIZ-C and 43 of 63 TRIZ-C+LLM students. The paper does not test the gap between the Total rows. A back-calculation from Table 2 (not printed) matches F = 3.21 to the unweighted mean of the three per-problem gaps, about 0.3 points (the rounded cell means give 4.7 vs 5.0), and not to the 0.8-point gap between the Total rows, which would give F of about 27.5. Do not pair the Total rows with this test or compute an effect size from them. Contrasts 2 to 4 carry the arm means, SDs, and Ns. [p. 512 (PDF p. 6), Section 4.2 and Table 2 (Total block and the six problem-by-method cells)]
- Needs authors: Randomization (open question 3): random assignment of each student is stated, but no procedure is given. The WT arms are 43 vs 30, a split with a two-sided probability of about 0.16 under 1:1 assignment (checkers' arithmetic). Criterion 1 rests on the stated random assignment.
- Needs authors: Count at assignment (open question 5): one GT student was excluded for not understanding the problem, and the paper does not say whether this student had been assigned a method, or to which arm, so n_randomized stays null. Criterion 2 counts the 114 analyzed either way.

## Candidates that need clarification

For these 43 papers one criterion rests on a passage the paper does not contain, usually how people came to be in each condition, or a checker found that a criterion fails. Each row says what is unsettled and, where the form records it, what would settle it (a supplement, posted data, the authors, or the journal version).

| Paper | People analyzed, by study | What is unsettled |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Abdelghani et al. (2024), "GPT-3-Driven Pedagogical Agents to Train Children’s Curious Question-Asking Skills", International Journal of Artificial Intelligence in Education (https://doi.org/10.1007/s40593-023-00340-7) [c173] | not reported; not reported | The child study uses an unspecified pseudo-randomized allocation method after baseline profiling, so actual random assignment is unconfirmed (p. 18, section 6.2.1). It explicitly reports 75 recruited children and assigned group sizes of 24, 26, and 25, but not final analyzed counts; the analyzed-person threshold therefore remains unclear and is not inferred from the reported degrees of freedom (p. 18, section 6.2.1; pp. 24-25, sections 8.2.1-8.2.2; p. 27, section 8.4.1). Divergent-question percentages, syntactic/semantic question quality, and offline divergent-question fluency are scored on children’s actual output, meeting criterion 3 (pp. 18-20, sections 6.2.2-6.2.3 and 6.4.1; results pp. 24-25 and 27). Core eligibility remains unclear. The ai_vs_human context classification applies only to the separate cue-output comparison (pp. 5, 17-18, 21-23), not to the child study with unresolved allocation. |
| Abu Asba and Al-Rababah (2026), "Effectiveness of a Training Program Based on Artificial Intelligence Tools in Developing Creative…", Dirasat: Educational Sciences (https://doi.org/10.35516/edu.2026.13368) [c176] | not reported (75 randomized) | Criterion 1 is met: the paper explicitly reports random assignment of three intact classes to generative-AI creative problem-solving training, classroom training without AI, or no training (p. 1, Methods; pp. 5-7, limitations, sample and training program). Class-level assignment supports field_rct despite the authors quasi-experimental label. Criterion 2 and core eligibility remain unclear: the 75 students and 25 per arm are sample/allocation counts, and administration of tests to all participants does not explicitly state how many were analyzed (pp. 1 and 6). No final total or per-arm analyzed headcount or exclusions accounting is printed in Tables 4-7 (pp. 10-13); the error df = 72 in Table 5 is retained without deriving an analyzed count. Authors must confirm that at least 50 people were analyzed. Criterion 3 is met by fluency, flexibility and originality scored on participants verbal TTCT responses (p. 6, instrument and scoring; pp. 10-11, Table 4). Unspecified AI access during tests prevents assigning after_ai versus with_ai, but does not negate random assignment to AI training or scoring on output (pp. 8-10, Table 2 and procedures). Main-sample scorer details and uncertainty appropriate to one randomized class per arm affect measurement coding and pooling, not these eligibility criteria. The randomized study is not observational context. |
| Beaty and DiStefano (2026), "Human direction drives creativity with large language models", PsyArXiv (https://doi.org/10.31234/osf.io/wkbms_v1) [c205] | 234; 205; 157; 1,941 other (WildChat users pursuing their own creative projects. The paper reports 1,941 unique users in product-creativity analyses, inferred from hashed IPs; it explicitly notes that shared or multiple IPs make this an imperfect proxy for people. The broader corpus has 2,053 user identifiers and 6,290 conversations; the product-scored subset has 5,772 conversations. The analyzed people field preserves the paper's reported user-level count, not a verified count of distinct individuals.) | Sample 1 has an AI-versus-solo within-person comparison, but the full text and supplement state only counterbalancing, not random assignment of condition order (p. 33, Sample 1 Methods; p. 16, Sample 1). Counterbalancing alone does not establish randomized order, so criterion 1 is unclear and design_class is other, not a confirmed RCT or confirmed non-random experiment. Sample 1 analyzed 234 people (p. 43, Table S6) and scored their final outputs for creativity (pp. 10, 16, 35), meeting criteria 2 and 3; under the short-screening rule, core is unclear and this paper goes to the two-screener pipeline. Samples 2 and 3 analyze measured Creative Direction under common AI access and observed median-split groups (pp. 17–18, 35, 37–38); Sample 4 uses existing naturalistic chats (pp. 40–42). Those associations are observational context and do not establish a randomized AI-assistance effect. |
| Bian et al. (2025), "Effects of AI-generated images in visual art education on students' classroom engagement, self-efficacy and…", Humanities and Social Sciences Communications (https://doi.org/10.1057/s41599-025-05860-2) [c034] | 78 | Criterion 1 is unclear because the abstract states random assignment (p. 1), the introduction calls the design quasi-experimental (p. 2), and neither the allocation unit nor procedure is described in the Methods or flow diagram (pp. 4-5). The equal class and arm sizes do not distinguish individual from class allocation. Class allocation would not by itself establish non-random assignment; the actual method must be confirmed. design_class is other and context_class remains none pending clarification, rather than asserting an RCT or non-randomized observational context. The AI contrast itself is in scope: supplied AI-generated example images versus human-made examples are ideas_supplied and ai_vs_active under codebook sections 5-6 (p. 6, Stage 3; p. 12, fourth limitation). Direct student operation of the generator is not required. Criterion 2 is met by 78 people analyzed, 39 per group (p. 4, Participants; p. 11, Table 9). Criterion 3 is met by teachers scoring each student's painting on Creativity and Originality (p. 6, Stage 4; p. 11, Table 9). Unreported availability of the example images during painting affects with_ai versus after_ai classification, not these two confirmed eligibility criteria or the existence of AI exposure. |
| Bohren, Hakimov, and Lalive (2024), "Creative and Strategic Capabilities of Generative AI: Evidence from Large-Scale Experiments", IZA Discussion Paper No. 17302 (IZA Institute of Labor Economics) (https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.4963992) [c035] | 1,250; 688; not reported; not reported | Criterion 1 is unclear. The paper states that writers 'were randomly assigned' to three treatments (PDF p. 9), but the HumanPlusAI item says 'In the description on Prolific, we explicitly requested participants who already possessed an OpenAI or Bard account' (PDF p. 10), and the paper never says whether that description applied to every writer or to HumanPlusAI only. If it applied to HumanPlusAI only, that arm was screened on owning an AI account while the other arms were not, and the HumanPlusAI vs HumanBaseline contrast is not shown to be randomized. It would then be observational context (quasi_people). Footnote 3's per-arm Prolific targets (700, 300, 300) and complete responses (702, 304, 315) do not settle it. Criterion 2 is met: 1,250 writers analyzed (688, 253, and 309; Table 1), 997 of them in the HumanPlusAI vs HumanBaseline contrast. About 140 people who took part are not in the writing analysis (open question 2), which does not change criterion 2. Criterion 3 is met: HumanPlusAI writers could use Bard or ChatGPT while writing (PDF p. 10), online raters and five research assistants scored each written text 0-10, and a unique-idea ratio is computed on the texts. S2 (ChatGPT-4 and Bard texts vs human texts) is ai_vs_human context. S3 (rater information) and S4 (strategic game) answer none of the three questions. |
| Boumous (2024), "Human Creativity Vs. Artificial Intelligence: A Comparison of Horror Fiction Crafting from ‘Bookish Minds…", Rupkatha Journal on Interdisciplinary Studies in Humanities (https://doi.org/10.21659/rupkatha.v16n3.01) [c218] | not reported | Allocation is described only as division into two groups; neither random assignment nor a specific non-random allocation mechanism is reported (p. 6, section 3.2; p. 1, Abstract). The reported study sample is 60 students (pp. 5-6, section 3.1), but the paper does not state the final participant analysis denominator overall or by arm (p. 9, section 4; pp. 10-12, section 4.1); criterion 2 is therefore unclear under the analyzed-people threshold. Originality, atmosphere, tension build-up, and character depth are scored on the stories with a 1-to-5 rubric (pp. 7-9, section 4; pp. 10-12, Figures 2-6), meeting criterion 3.
