(2025), "Harnessing generative AI for collaborative creativity: A study of university students’ engagement and…", International Journal of Innovative Research and Scientific Studies (https://doi.org/10.53894/ijirss.v8i3.7227) [c887] | 50 | Reflection denominator and timing of any control-group AI exposure (open_questions[3]): the text below Figure 2 calls fifty students ChatGPT-3 users, whereas the methods and quantitative results specify 25 AI students and 25 controls (PDF p. 7, section 4.6; PDF p. 4, section 3.1; PDF p. 5, section 4.1). Authors must clarify whether this is a description/denominator error or whether additional AI use occurred, and whether that use preceded or followed the scored AUT. This could affect criterion 1 if it changes the reported AI-versus-no-AI contrast during production; the explicit randomized quantitative comparison remains the basis for the current met judgment. |
| Rick et al. (2025), "How far afield should you go when being creative? Semantic area as a metric of AI’s effects on creative…", Proceedings of the 16th International Conference on Computational… (https://computationalcreativity.net/iccc25/wp-content/uploads/iccc25-proceedings.pdf#page=132) [c894] | 111 | Criterion 1 is unclear: all participants used both generative-AI configurations, but the paper does not state random assignment or randomized condition order (p. 3, Evaluating Creative Ideation with Semantic Area and Results; p. 5, Figure 6). The design is coded other because neither randomized nor explicitly non-random allocation is established. Criterion 2 is met: N = 111 users were analyzed from the original 115 (p. 3, Data preparation). Criterion 3 is met: a separate group of evaluators scored the final moodboards for innovation and fit, and a computed semantic-area measure scores the breadth of selected ideas (p. 3, Evaluating Creative Ideation with Semantic Area and Calculating and Using Semantic Area; pp. 4-6, Results). With assignment unclear, at least 50 people analyzed, and an output-scored outcome, core remains unclear and the paper goes to the two-screener pipeline. Semantic area here is within-person flexibility rather than across-person diversity. |
| Shiiku et al. (2026), "Hybrid Human–AI Societies Balance Creativity and Diversity", PsyArXiv (https://doi.org/10.31234/osf.io/wzh2x_v1) [c560] | not reported; not reported | Core remains unclear. Random assignment is stated for network node location, but allocation between human-only and human-AI network compositions is not described; no non-random allocation mechanism is stated either (PDF p. 3, Network Experiments on Collective Creativity). Criterion 2 is also unclear: 879 is explicitly a recruited storytelling count, and 625 human-only and 254 human-AI are reported condition Ns, without confirmed post-exclusion analysis counts (PDF p. 3, Participants and condition descriptions; PDF p. 2, Figure 1B). This is not evidence that fewer than 50 people were analyzed. Human creativity ratings and TF-IDF diversity scores assess produced stories, satisfying criterion 3 (PDF p. 4, Measures of Creativity and Results). The explicit AI-only versus human-only output benchmark qualifies as ai_vs_human context; the hybrid assistance comparison remains an unconfirmed design rather than a confirmed RCT or non-random comparison (PDF pp. 3-4, condition definitions and Creativity and Diversity; codebook sections 3-4). Hybrid summaries combine human and AI stories and do not establish a separate human-authored-output effect. The validation raters are not creative-task participants; their count conflicts between 100 in the methods and 94 in Figure 1C (PDF p. 4, Measures of Creativity; PDF p. 2, Figure 1C). |

(2025), "Interactions with generative AI chatbots: unveiling dialogic dynamics, students’ perceptions, and practical…", International Journal of Educational Technology in Higher Education (https://doi.org/10.1186/s41239-025-00508-2) [c165] | not reported (80 randomized) | Random assignment versus the quasi-experimental label: authors could provide the actual allocation procedure. Criterion 1 is currently met by the explicit Methods statement; evidence of a nonrandom mechanism would change eligibility (pp. 1, 3, 8). |
| Treglia, Mariani, and Vindigni (2026), "Exploring the Role of Generative Artificial Intelligence in Creative Thinking Processes: results from a…", Journal of Inclusive Methodology and Technology in Learning and… (https://www.inclusiveteaching.it/index.php/inclusiveteaching/article/view/537) [c590] | 56 | Random-assignment implementation and the conflicting quasi-experimental characterization remain unexplained (open_questions[2]). Authors would need to confirm the allocation procedure and reconcile the design terminology. This concerns criterion 1 and could change classification if allocation was nonrandom; the current met decision follows the explicit random-assignment statement (p. 3, Section 2.1; pp. 9-10, Section 5). |
| Wang and Panicker (2025), "The impact of AI-assisted composition tools on cultivating creativity among music students in Guangdong…", Edelweiss Applied Science and Technology (https://doi.org/10.55214/25768484.v9i6.8493) [c618] | 120 | open_questions[0]: How was the randomized assignment implemented, and were students allocated individually or through institutional/classroom units? The field_rct/individual coding follows the student flowchart and explicit randomization statement, but the method is unspecified and conflicts with the quasi-experimental label (PDF pp. 5-7). This concerns criterion 1: the explicit randomization claims support the present met decision, but author confirmation of the allocation procedure and unit, and an explanation of the quasi-experimental label, would settle the conflict. Non-random allocation would change eligibility; the exact clustering unit alone concerns analysis and precision. |
| Wang et al. (2025), "EEG assessment of artificial intelligence-generated content impact on student creative performance and…", Frontiers in Psychology (https://doi.org/10.3389/fpsyg.2025.1508383) [c619] | 64 | The allocation-sequence method is unreported, and the explicit participant random-assignment statement conflicts with the quasi-experimental label (p. 4, sections 3.1 and 3.2.1). This bears on criterion 1: authors could describe how the assignment sequence was generated and implemented and explain the label. Current eligibility follows the explicit random-assignment statement. |
| Wiles and Horton (2026), "More, but Worse: The Marketplace Effects of AI-Generated Job Posts", Author-hosted working paper (https://www.emmawiles.com/storage/jobot.pdf) [c635] | not reported; not reported; not reported; not reported | S1 randomly assigned the offer of generative-AI drafting at employer-account level (PDF p. 9, Section 3), with assistance available while producing the final job description (p. 10, Section 3.1). Semantic similarity is computed on published text (p. 47, Table 12), satisfying the codebook's writing-across-writers similarity scope and output-scoring criterion. However, the full analysis analyzed 180,324 employer accounts (p. 26, Table 3), and the similarity analysis analyzed 32,513 posts (p. 47, Table 12); neither establishes the number of distinct people analyzed. The complete saved document, including both appendices, provides no account-to-person mapping, so criterion 2 and core eligibility remain unclear. S2 reports no qualifying creativity/diversity outcome (pp. 66, 69-72), and S3 also lacks a verified distinct-person count (p. 68, Figure 15). S4 compares self-selected editors within S1 and remains observational (p. 17, Section 4.5.2; p. 50, Table 15). Setting unsupported word-count, dispersion, and skill-count measure codes to null does not alter the qualifying semantic-similarity outcome. |
| Wu, Weber, and Müller (2025), "One Does Not Simply Meme Alone: Evaluating Co-Creativity Between LLMs and Humans in the Generation of Humor", Proceedings of the 30th International Conference on Intelligent User… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3708359.3712094) [c168] | 98; not reported | S1 compares people who worked alone with people who could prompt GPT-4o, in a 'between-subject user study with three experimental groups' (p. 4, Section 3), but the paper never says how participants were allocated to the two human conditions. Randomization is named only for image-topic permutations (p. 4, Section 3.2) and for 'randomization of tasks, etc.' (p. 6, Section 3.4). If allocation was random, S1 is an online_rct with ai_vs_bau contrasts and enters the core. If it was not, S1 is observational context (quasi_people). Criterion 2: 98 people analyzed across the two human arms (124 recruited minus 26 non-completers, p. 6, Section 3.6), well above 50. The split by arm is not stated, the favorite counts imply 49 per arm, and the abstract's 'groups of 50' (p. 1) conflicts with Section 3.6. The printed per-participant Mann-Whitney-U tests do not fit 49 per arm, so they may not include all 98 people, but the printed statistics still need at least 78 people in the per-participant idea test and at least 91 in the Raw TLX test (pp. 6-7, Sections 4.1.1-4.1.2), so criterion 2 is met under any reading. Criterion 3: crowd ratings of the memes and idea counts are scored on the output. S2 (AI-only memes vs human memes) is ai_vs_human context. |
| Xu et al. (2026), "Human or model? A four-factor experimental study of design experience and image-generation alignment…", Research in Engineering Design (https://doi.org/10.1007/s00163-026-00497-x) [c135] | 60 | Criterion 1 unclear: every participant completed all four generative-AI conditions (p. 6, Sect. 3.2.2), and task sequences were assigned using a balanced Latin square (p. 7, Sect. 3.3). The paper does not state that participants were randomly allocated to sequences; 'random' refers only to Midjourney seed values (p. 6, Sect. 3.2.3). The codebook requires randomized order for a within-person design. The two image generators and two prompt types provide ai_design contrasts under codebook sections 3 and 5; a no-AI arm is not required, and conventional treatment/control labels do not prevent eligibility. Design experience and AIGC proficiency were measured grouping factors (p. 5, Sect. 3.1). S1 is coded other because a randomized design class is not yet supported. Criterion 2 met: 60 people were analyzed, above the 50-person threshold; the recruitment count and groups are stated in Sect. 3.1 (p. 5), Fig. 3 shows outputs from all 60 in all four conditions (p. 10), and Tables 4 to 6 report error df 54 (pp. 11, 12, 14). No participant exclusions are reported. Criterion 3 met: two blind experts scored participants' prompted and selected images for quality and novelty (p. 7, Sects. 3.3-3.4; p. 8, Table 2; p. 9, Sect. 4). Perceived usefulness is self-report and does not supply eligibility; the image-level attribute diversity score is not diversity across people (p. 8, Sect. 3.4.4; p. 16, Sect. 5). Context class remains none while allocation is unresolved; this is an experiment in which people used AI, not an AI-versus-human output comparison. Core eligibility remains unclear pending author clarification of allocation to Latin-square sequences. |
| Yang et al. (2025), "Large Language Models Improve Hypothesis Generation by Reducing Effort", SSRN (working paper) (https://doi.org/10.2139/ssrn.5339141) [c137] | 396; 213; 803; 400; not reported | Random assignment is explicit: Qualtrics administered random condition assignment (PDF p. 11, Materials and Methods), with individual assignment to the two LLM/no-LLM arms in Experiments 1-2 and four factorial cells in Experiments 3-4 (pp. 12-13). The four experiments analyzed 396, 213, 803 and 400 people after screenings, respectively (pp. 12-13); each independently exceeds 50. Actual randomized totals and individual-arm analyzed counts are not printed, and the PDF does not establish that the uninspected SI Table S2 supplies them. The main outcomes are objectively scored on the output participants produced: unique teams per person (p. 5), accurate-hypothesis counts and proportions (pp. 5-8), and unique teams across the crowd in each condition (p. 11). The unresolved point is scope, not whether scoring occurred. The task enumerates a finite set of sports-playoff forecasts (p. 4, Paradigm), judged by quantity and accuracy rather than novelty, originality or creativity. Codebook sections 1 and 3 do not explicitly settle whether these forecasts, including their crowd-level breadth, qualify as creative ideation or homogenization evidence. As in the reconciled form, this scope question is carried under criterion 3 rather than counted again under criterion 1. Core therefore remains unclear pending an internal scope decision; no additional source is needed to describe the main task. The ancillary occupation-guessing experiment cannot independently establish eligibility because its specific design, sample size, scoring method and timing are not given in the saved PDF (p. 11; SI Section 5 uninspected). The abstract total of 1,814 (p. 2) conflicts with the sum of the explicit analyzed study counts, 1,812; that discrepancy does not affect the per-study threshold. |

## Experiments with full text not obtained

These 227 papers have no full text on file: neither the retrieval script nor the retrieval agents could obtain one through a legitimate route. Triage called 224 of them experiments. The other 3 went to the context path at triage, but their abstracts or records do not rule out a randomized experiment, so they also wait for the full text. The middle column repeats what the abstract or the short form says, and the author labels come from the search records. `docs/creativity-screening/to-download.html` lists the 264 papers on the screening paths that have no full text on file, with every link found: the 227 papers in this table and 37 papers that appear in other sections. Files saved by hand into `Research/ai-lab/literature/creativity/_inbox/` are matched to papers by title (`tools/ingest_inbox.py`) and then enter the two-screener pipeline.

## Randomized experiments under 50 participants

These 28 papers randomize people to generative-AI assistance, but fewer than 50 people were analyzed, so under the current rule they are listed and left out of the dataset whatever their outcome. For 1 of them it is also unclear whether creativity or diversity is scored on the output. Review 1 decides whether the rule holds.

| Paper | People analyzed, by randomized study | Finding |

## Context: AI versus human output

| Paper | Finding |
| --- | --- |
| Arora et al. (2025), "Generative artificial intelligence models outperform students on divergent and convergent thinking assessments", Scientific Reports (https://doi.org/10.1038/s41598-025-21398-4) [c029] | All three chatbots beat 46 students on AUT originality and the RAT; for ChatGPT-4o the human-minus-AI median difference in median originality was -0.800 to -0.950 across items (p < 0.001), and RAT accuracy was 54.96 vs 19.13 of 57 (Tables 3 and 4). |

## Excluded after screening

| Paper | Reason |
| --- | --- |
| A. Welzel and Vincent (2026), "Overreliance in Writing Tasks: Exploring Similarity-Based Measures of AI Influence on Writing and Proposing…", Proceedings of the 2026 ACM Conference on Fairness, Accountability… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3805689.3812389) [c633] | The main experiment analyzed 47 people after 7 exclusions from 54 recruited, below the 50-person threshold (PDF p. 6, Section 3.4). The same 47 people completed both an AI-assisted and an unassisted task, so the two arm counts cannot be added (PDF p. 10, Section 4.1). Task order and AI assistance were counterbalanced, but random assignment or randomized order is not stated (PDF p. 6, Section 3.4; p. 9, Section 4). Outcomes compare each response with the task-specific AI suggestion, measuring AI-text adoption rather than creativity or diversity across writers; criterion 3 fails and context_class remains none (PDF pp. 6-8, Section 3.4; p. 13, Section 5). The pilot iterations report 5 and 11 participants and were used only for design development, with no reported outcome estimates; these counts must not be added to the main experiment (PDF p. 6, Section 3.3). The think-aloud study has 4 university students and an illustrative qualitative analysis (PDF p. 9, Section 3.5; p. 11, Section 4.2). |
| Castro, Gao, and Martin (2025), "Human-AI Interactions and Societal Pitfalls", arXiv preprint (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2309.10448) [c004] | Theory paper with numerical simulations; none of the three core criteria is met. Random assignment: the paper develops a Bayesian model of users' choices, with no people assigned to AI conditions (PDF p. 1, Abstract; pp. 8-9, Section 3). People analyzed: the users are a modeled continuum, not a human sample (PDF p. 8, Section 3); M = 1001 is the number of discretized preference support points and T = 100 is the number of simulation iterations, not people analyzed (PDF p. 49, printed e-17, Appendix F). Output scoring: homogenization is the variance of the model's output distribution (PDF p. 13, Section 4.2 and Theorem 1), not a creativity or diversity score on outputs produced by participants; the two GPT-4o abstracts in Figure 1 are illustrations without such scores (PDF p. 3). Codebook Section 3 excludes theory papers from the dataset, and no context class applies. |
| Chen and Jiao (2026), "Central Tendency Bias in Human Selection of AI-Generated Design Variations", 2026 2nd International Conference on Human-AI Interaction and… (https://doi.org/10.1109/haxd70072.2026.11620812) [c247] | Criterion 1 is not met because no generative-AI assistance was assigned: stimuli were manually constructed (PDF p. 2, III.B; p. 5, V, limitations). Within-person stimulus-set order was explicitly randomized (PDF p. 3, III.D), so assignment.randomized is yes for presentation order, but this is not random assignment of AI. Criterion 2 is met: Table I explicitly reports 50 participants in the analyzed trial totals, with the same people in both variance conditions (PDF p. 3, III.D and Table I note). Criterion 3 is not met: the outcomes are center-selection rates in preference and representativeness judgments of supplied posters, not creativity or diversity scored on participant-produced output (PDF p. 3, III.D-E and Table I; p. 4, IV.C). This perceptual-choice experiment is coded other and fits none of the codebook context classes. |
| Chen et al. (2024), "ChatScratch: An AI-Augmented System Toward Autonomous Visual Programming Learning for Children Aged 6-12", Proceedings of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3613904.3642229) [c252] | The main comparison analyzed 24 unique children, below the required 50; the same children completed both conditions, so the arm counts must not be added (PDF p. 11, sections 5-5.2; p. 14, Figure 6; p. 13, section 6.1). The methods describe counterbalancing and alternating tool/theme combinations but do not state random assignment to session orders or an explicit mechanism for allocating children to those orders, leaving criterion 1 unclear (PDF p. 11, section 5.2; p. 12, section 5.4.1). Criterion 3 is met because Scratch educators scored creativity and originality on the produced assets (PDF p. 11, section 5.3.2; p. 14, Table 3). The formative interviews do not test AI assistance, and the technical benchmark supplies neither a human sample size nor sufficiently detailed methods to classify a separate contextual comparison (PDF pp. 4-5, section 3.1; p. 7, section 4.3). |
| Choi et al. (2024), "CreativeConnect: Supporting Reference Recombination for Graphic Design Ideation with Generative AI", Proceedings of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3613904.3642794) [c742] | The main comparison analyzes 16 people, each using both CreativeConnect and the generative-AI baseline, below the 50-person threshold (PDF pp. 9-10, Sections 5-5.3; creativity-codebook.md, Section 3). Tool and topic order was counterbalanced, but random assignment of people or condition order is not reported, so design_class is other and criterion 1 remains unclear (PDF p. 10, Section 5.2). Random selection of sketches for expert evaluation is not random assignment of people. Expert-rated creativity and within-person sketch-set variety, and sketch counts, are scored on participant output (PDF pp. 10-13, Sections 5.3 and 6.2, Table 2). Both arms provide generative AI, making the comparison ai_design (PDF p. 9, Section 5). The formative study and the model-only technical evaluation do not supply an eligible AI-assistance comparison; their participants or annotators and their image/text counts must not augment the main sample (PDF p. 4, Sections 3.1-3.2; pp. 8-9, Section 4.3). Unspecified allocation does not establish non-random assignment, and no AI-versus-human or synthesis context is reported, so context_class remains none. |
| Choi et al. (2025), "Expandora: Broadening Design Exploration with Text-to-Image Model", Proceedings of the Extended Abstracts of the CHI Conference on Human… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3706599.3720189) [c743] | The comparative evaluation includes 8 participants (p. 4, Section 5; p. 9, Table 1), and the formative study recruited six (pp. 2-3, Section 3); neither meets the 50-person criterion. The evaluation counterbalances system and topic order without stating randomization (p. 5, Section 5); the formative study reports no AI comparison conditions (pp. 2-3, Section 3). Only prompt counts, prompt similarity, and survey outcomes are quantified in the evaluation; the similarity comparison set and aggregation are unspecified, and no scored image-creativity or confirmed across-person diversity outcome is reported, leaving criterion 3 unclear (p. 5, Sections 6-6.2; pp. 8-10, Appendices C-D). Neither study supplies an AI-versus-human output comparison, a qualifying observational comparison, or a synthesis, so context_class remains none. |
| Choi et al. (2026), "IdeaBlocks: Expressing and Reusing Divergent Intents for Graphic Design Exploration using Generative AI", Proceedings of the 2026 Designing Interactive Systems Conference… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3800645.3813005) [c263] | Study 1 analyzes 12 people, each using both AI configurations; the arm counts are repeated observations of the same people, not distinct samples (PDF pp. 9-10, Sections 6.1.1-6.1.3; p. 20, Table 4). The study states that topic and system order was counterbalanced but does not establish random assignment, so its design is coded other and criterion 1 remains unclear. Its image/block counts, within-session CLIP distance and linkography measures are computed from participant-created outputs (PDF pp. 10-12, Sections 6.1.4-6.2.3 and Table 1), meeting criterion 3, but its sample is below 50. The formative study has 7 people and the longitudinal study has 6, with no scored creativity/diversity comparison; the pipeline evaluation has 100 model suggestions and 3 raters, not people assigned assistance (PDF pp. 4, 9 and 13). P9/D3 is shared between Study 1 and Study 2 (PDF p. 20, Table 4 footnote); samples, raters and outputs are not pooled to meet the threshold. No randomized people study, AI-versus-human-output comparison, eligible observational contrast or synthesis is established; retain only in the screening report. |
| Cuveillier et al. (2026), "CreaRobot - Improving creativity using a social robot", Companion Publication of the 28th International Conference on… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3776591.3832523) [c046] | The abstract reports results from 49 participants, below the 50-person threshold (complete abstract, sentence 4; OpenAlex W7215983025, independently matched to Semantic Scholar d5b8a62090ce11046181a4ff848416cee3cc09be). Random assignment and between-person versus within-person structure are not stated in the complete abstract, so criterion 1 remains unclear. The prerecorded generic compliments constitute an active non-generative aid, making the contrast ai_vs_active (sentence 3; creativity-codebook.md, sections 1 and 5). The reported value of the creative product concerns the drawings and supports criterion 3, while its construct and scoring procedure remain unconfirmed (sentences 2 and 4). Core eligibility is no because the sample is below 50; context_class remains none because nonrandom assignment is not established. |
| De Simone et al. (2026), "Creo: From One-Shot Image Generation to Progressive, Co-Creative Ideation", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2604.13956) [c283] | The study has 15 unique analyzed participants, below the 50-person threshold; both conditions use the same people (p. 7, Sections 4.1-4.1.1; p. 8, Figure 4). Tool and prompt orders were counterbalanced using listed sequences, but random assignment or another sequence-allocation mechanism is not stated (p. 15, Appendix D.2 and Table 2). Design is therefore coded other rather than assuming either random or non-random allocation. An output-based embedding score is reported, so criterion 3 is met, but it measures within-session anchoring; the across-person homogeneity claim has only a visual comparison and no numerical diversity contrast (pp. 7-8, Sections 4.2-4.3.1 and Figure 4; p. 16, Appendix D.7). This small comparison is excluded from core and is not classified as established observational context. |
| Disley and Khan (2026), "Designing cognitive friction with concept suppression in large language models", Human–Computer Interaction (https://doi.org/10.1080/07370024.2026.2708617) [c053] | Within-subject design with randomized Latin-square condition order meets criterion 1 (PDF p. 17, printed p. 16, Section 5.3). Only 37 people were analyzed (47 recruited, 38 completed, one further participant excluded; PDF p. 16, printed p. 15, Section 5.2), below 50; the three conditions used the same participants. No creativity or diversity outcome is scored on their outputs: quantitative outcomes are self-reported workload and ownership (PDF p. 18, printed p. 17, Section 5.4). The authors explicitly report no empirical analysis of activity outputs (PDF p. 28, printed p. 27, Section 6.4) and state that fixation, novelty, and diversity were not operationalized (PDF p. 35, printed p. 34, Section 7.8). List in the screening report as a small randomized experiment with self-report outcomes; no eligible context class applies. |
| Fan et al. (2025), "LitLinker: Supporting the Ideation of Interdisciplinary Contexts with Large Language Models for Teaching…", Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3714111) [c767] | Experiment I analyzes the same 16 students in both conditions, below the 50-person threshold (p. 10, Section 5.1; p. 11, Table 2). Task-system orders are counterbalanced but random allocation is not stated; explicit randomization concerns the expert's rating order (pp. 10-11, Sections 5.2-5.3). Seven expert ratings are scored on the produced lesson plans (pp. 11-12, Sections 5.3-5.4.1 and Figure 4). The qualitative foundational interviews, prototype evaluation, and final-system interviews do not add people to that comparison or supply an eligible randomized output-score contrast (pp. 3-5, Sections 3.1-3.2; pp. 13-14, Sections 6-6.2). Retain in the screening report as a small experiment with unresolved assignment; no qualifying context class is established. |
| Gero et al. (2026), "From Planning to Revision: How AI Writing Support at Different Stages Alters Ownership", Proceedings of the 2026 Designing Interactive Systems Conference… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3800645.3813003) [c059] | Criterion 1: individuals were randomly assigned to no AI or one of three generative-AI conditions (p. 4, Section 3.3). It is coded met for the assignment. The codebook's 'creative or ideation task' clause raises the same scope question as criterion 3 and stands or falls with it. Criterion 2: 253 people analyzed, 60 to 66 per arm (p. 5, Section 3.4). Criterion 3 not met: the task is a 200-300 word argumentative essay, and nothing is judged for creativity, novelty, or diversity across people. The two measures scored on the output are an IELTS-based writing-proficiency composite graded by an LLM (pp. 6-7, Section 4.1) and the final word count, which the paper treats as an effort proxy (p. 7, Section 4.2). The headline outcomes (ownership, attribution of ideas and text) are self-reports, and n-gram retention compares one person's own texts across stages (p. 8, Table 2). The codebook's construct table defines usefulness to include 'quality of a single output' and elaboration as 'Detail or length of a single output', so a literal reading of that table could admit both measures. Its scope lists 'essays judged for creativity' among creative tasks, and this form reads that as excluding an essay scored only for writing proficiency. Review 1 decides (open question 1). Both screeners, the reconciler, and the final check coded criterion 3 not met. |
| Gmeiner et al. (2025), "Exploring the Potential of Metacognitive Support Agents for Human-AI Co-Creation", Proceedings of the 2025 ACM Designing Interactive Systems Conference (https://doi.org/10.1145/3715336.3735785) [c774] | Criterion 1 is not met: the contrast varies human-operated metacognitive support while all groups use the same Fusion360 Generative Design tool; no implemented generative-AI assistance or configuration is varied, and random assignment is not reported (p. 2, Section 1; pp. 6–8, Sections 4.3–5.4). Criterion 2 is not met: 20 people were analyzed, five per group (p. 7, Section 5.1; p. 9, Section 6; p. 10, Table 2 and Figure 3). Criterion 3 is met: submitted designs were scored on a five-criterion feasibility rubric, which falls under usefulness for a design task (p. 9, Section 5.5.1; creativity-codebook.md Sections 1, 3, and 6). Core remains no. No listed context class applies to this human-support comparison within a common AI-assisted task, so context_class remains none. |
| Guo, Pataranutaporn, and Maes (2024), "Exploring the Impact of AI Value Alignment in Collaborative Ideation: Effects on Perception, Ownership, and…", Extended Abstracts of the CHI Conference on Human Factors in… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3613905.3650892) [c781] | Random assignment to generative-AI configurations and randomized order of assisted and unassisted sessions are explicit (PDF p. 3, section 3.3). Criterion 3 is not met: GPT-4 scores value stance in the written output, while creativity, novelty, usefulness, and clarity are participant self-evaluations; no output-scored creativity or between-person diversity outcome is reported (PDF p. 4, sections 3.5-3.6; p. 5, section 4.2; p. 10, Appendix B). Core eligibility is no, and none of the codebook context classes applies. |
| Hamilton, Brisco, and Grierson (2024), "HOW CAN AI SUPPORT THE CREATION OF NOVEL IDEAS IN PRODUCT DESIGN", Proceedings of the International Conference on Engineering and… (https://doi.org/10.35199/epde.2024.23) [c787] | The paper reports division of a class into groups and permission to use ChatGPT, Google, or neither, but does not state random assignment or another allocation mechanism (PDF pp. 2–3, section 2.1); criterion 1 therefore remains unclear. Only 28 students were asked to participate (PDF p. 2, section 2.1), and 25 survey responses were analyzed (PDF p. 3, section 3.1), so the study cannot meet the 50-person threshold even though the novelty-analysis participant count is not separately stated. Concepts were scored for novelty by coders and averaged by team (PDF p. 3, section 2.2; PDF p. 4, section 3.2 and Table 2), satisfying criterion 3. Core is no because of sample size; observational context is not established because a nonrandom allocation mechanism is not stated. |
| Han et al. (2025), "POET: Supporting Prompting Creativity and Personalization with Automated Expansion of Text-to-Image Generation", Proceedings of the 38th Annual ACM Symposium on User Interface… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3746059.3747710) [c789] | The user study analyzes 28 people in total, below 50 (PDF p. 2, Abstract; p. 15, Conclusions; p. 12, Tables 4-5). It compares AI configurations within participants with counterbalanced order; random assignment of people or condition order is not stated (PDF pp. 8-9, Sections 5 and 5.1.1-5.1.2). No outcome scores creativity or cross-person diversity of participants' own outputs: Phase 1 rates supplied model-generated image pairs, while Phases 2 and 3 report prompting rounds and self-rated satisfaction (PDF pp. 8-12, Sections 5.1-5.3 and Tables 3-5). The statement 'every scenario was seen by 6 participants' (PDF p. 9, Section 5.1.2), across four scenarios, implies 24 people (6 x 4), conflicting with the reported total of 28; the paper does not explain this discrepancy. The separate Flickr30K evaluation is model-only: 1,000 sampled prompts and their generated images do not count as people, and ICAD compares images from the same prompt rather than different human creators (PDF pp. 7-8, Sections 4.1-4.2). Neither study supplies AI-versus-human output comparisons or eligible observational creativity/diversity evidence; context_class remains none. |
| Hassan and Nair (2026), "Searching together: Human–AI complementarities in innovation search", Research Policy (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.respol.2026.105597) [c064] | Criterion 1 is not met: this exploratory qualitative interview study used purposeful and network sampling, with no assignment to AI and no experimental comparison group (p. 4, Sections 3-3.1; p. 5, Section 3.1). Criterion 2 is not met: the authors report 41 interviews but no explicit total of distinct people analyzed (p. 5, Section 3.1). Counting Appendix 1 gives 40 listed interviewees, below 50: there are 39 position-code rows (F1-F5 and M1-M34), and M17A/B covers two people (p. 14, Appendix 1). This is an appendix-derived count, so participants.n_analyzed remains null. Criterion 3 is not met: the interviews and 64 secondary articles were analyzed into qualitative themes, with no creativity or diversity score on outputs produced by participants (p. 5, Sections 3.1-3.2; p. 6, Fig. 1; p. 11, Table 2; p. 15, Appendix 2). The paper fits no eligible context class: it compares no AI output with human output, compares no real outputs on creativity or diversity, and is not a review. It remains out of scope as a qualitative study of practitioners' experiences. |
| Hintze, Proschinger Åström, and Schossau (2026), "Autonomous language-image generation loops converge to generic visual motifs", Patterns (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.patter.2025.101451) [c008] | Both experiments are model_only. The paper describes models that "interact without human input" (PDF p. 2, printed p. 1, The bigger picture). There is no human participant sample; the reported trajectories and start prompts are not people. Scored drift and clustering concern model-generated prompt embeddings, not outputs produced by participants (PDF p. 11, printed p. 10, Methods, Embedding analysis and cosine similarity; PDF p. 4, printed p. 3, Results, Temperature-specific and pooled clustering analysis). All three criteria are therefore not_met. The discussion of prior human cultural-transmission studies does not contribute a human experiment or reanalysis here. No human-output comparison or observational human corpus qualifies the paper as context evidence. Under codebook section 3, model-only studies remain in the screening report and are omitted from the dataset. |
| Jeon et al. (2026), "HALO: Interactive Co-abductive Reasoning in Scientific Hypothesis Generation", arXiv preprint arXiv:2607.18564 [cs.HC] (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2607.18564) [c385] | S1 analyzed ten medicinal chemists who completed both conditions, below the 50-person threshold (p. 9, Section 6.1.4; p. 11, Section 7.4). Quality and within-person molecular similarity were scored on submitted outputs, although the quality analysis unit is unconfirmed (pp. 9-10, Sections 6.1.3 and 6.2.4). S2 is qualitative, with no scored creativity outcome or assigned assistance contrast (pp. 4-5, Sections 4.1-4.2); Table 4 marks P3, P4 and P5 in both human studies, so their samples cannot be added (p. 21). | Jin et al. (2026), "Cultivating design creativity of vocational students: A model of project-based learning in AI-enabled…", Research and Practice in Technology Enhanced Learning (https://doi.org/10.58459/rptel.2027.22029) [c388] | Criterion 3 is not met: the analyzed creativity outcomes are self-report questionnaires, and no reported research outcome was scored on student products (p. 15, Design creativity survey; p. 19, Table 2; pp. 23-24, Limitations and future research). These model-only experiments and the human evaluation of AI-made work do not qualify for the core dataset or any context class. Retain them in the screening report and omit them from the dataset. |

(2024), "ChatGPT and Me: Collaborative Creativity in a Group Brainstorming with Generative AI", 2024 ASEE Annual Conference & Exposition Proceedings (Portland… (https://doi.org/10.18260/1-2--48457) [c410] | 33 students in total (Table 2: 17 in ChatGPT groups, 16 in no-ChatGPT groups), below 50. | Kim et al. (2025), "Scaffolding Recursive Divergence and Convergence in Story Ideation", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2507.03307) [c809] | Randomization is not stated: the methods describe Latin Square counterbalancing of tool/story order without explaining allocation to sequences (p. 6, Section 5; p. 7, Table 1 and Section 5.4). This small randomized study with self-reported creativity is omitted from the core and from the codebook's context classes. |
| Kobak et al. (2025), "Delving into LLM-assisted writing in biomedical publications through excess vocabulary", Science Advances (https://doi.org/10.1126/sciadv.adt3813) [c009] | Criterion 1 is not met: this is an observational corpus analysis, with no assignment of people to AI assistance; LLM use is inferred at corpus level and cannot be identified for individual abstracts (p. 5, Discussion; p. 7, Sections 4.1-4.3). Criterion 2 is not met: the 15,103,888 observations analyzed are PubMed abstracts, not participants, and the paper reports no participant cohort or people count (p. 7, Sections 4.1-4.2); the people counts remain null. Criterion 3 is not met: excess marker-word frequencies are computed on abstract text, but estimate the prevalence of LLM-assisted writing, not creativity, novelty, or diversity across authors (pp. 3-4, Section 2.2). Homogenisation is discussed with citations to other studies (p. 6, Discussion), and the t-SNE supplement maps local LLM-use estimates rather than measuring an AI effect on diversity (p. 16, Figure S7). Codebook Section 3 excludes AI-text-prevalence studies without a diversity outcome from both the core dataset and observational context. The outstanding journal-version check concerns provenance and does not change these eligibility findings for the saved PDF. |

The number represented among the 36 shortlisted ideas is not stated (p. 13, Section 4.4.4), but the entire reported pool is below 50, so criterion 2 is not met. Criterion 1 remains unclear: pairing was random, but random allocation of teams to conditions is not stated (p. 16, Section 4.6.1). Control participants could use internet resources including ChatGPT, with actual ChatGPT uptake unreported (p. 16, Section 4.6.2; p. 33, Appendix D), so the comparison is structured ALIA support versus mixed-resource access. Criterion 3 is met because novelty and quality were scored on participants' ideas (pp. 13, 18). Retain context_class = none because the condition-allocation method is unresolved, rather than confirmed nonrandom. S2 is model-only (pp. 14-15). List S1 in the screening report as a small controlled experiment. |

Its reported composition is six groups per condition, two people per group except one control group with three, which is below 50 people (p. 6, Section 3.5); the precise analyzed-person denominator is left null because only feedback-response counts are plotted and the experimental idea-rating header conflicts with its bars (p. 7, Figure 4). The paper explicitly limits its report to participants' reactions (p. 2, Section 1), uses post-session self-assessments (pp. 6-7, Section 4 and Figure 4), and defers novelty/idea-output analysis to future work (p. 7, Section 5; p. 8, Section 6). The three preliminary pilots report functionality feedback without participant counts or scored creative outputs (p. 5, Section 3.3). Self-report-only evidence does not qualify for core or a context class under the codebook. |
| Kollia and Manias (2025), "Assessment of the Impact of Artificial Intelligence on Creative Storytelling for Enhancing the Ideation…", EAI Endorsed Transactions on Creative Technologies (https://doi.org/10.4108/eetct.10417) [c412] | Ten students analyzed, five per group (p. 2, Section 3.1; p. 5, Table 3; p. 6, Table 5), below the 50-person threshold. Random assignment is explicitly stated only for the four students with creative-writing experience, two per group; assignment of the remaining students is not stated (pp. 2-3, Section 3.1). Creativity was scored on AUT responses and narrative texts (p. 4, Sections 3.5-3.5.1; p. 9, Appendix B). List this small, partly randomized experiment in the screening report; no separate context class applies. |
| Komura and Yamada (2026), "Deepening ideas vs. exploring new ones: AI strategy effects in human-AI creative collaboration", PLOS One (https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0340449) [c078] | Criterion 3 is not met. No creativity or diversity score of the output, by raters or by a computed measure, is compared across arms. The outcomes are counts of ideas each participant would 'actually want to adopt' and their idea-level logit (the participant's own choice, whose creativity the authors say 'requires separate evaluation', p. 20), trust and Godspeed questionnaires, and perception of the AI's strategy. The only diversity evidence is a t-SNE plot with no metric or test. It pools participants' ideas with the AI's ideas, which follow each arm's prompt (pp. 9-10), and it omits the Random arm (Fig 8A, p. 16). Criterion 1 is unclear: all three arms use GPT-4.1 and differ only in strategy (an ai_design comparison), but the paper says only that participants were 'divided' into three groups (p. 3) and never states random allocation. 'Random' names an AI condition. S1 is therefore coded design_class other, as in the final c168. Random allocation would make it an online_rct and meet criterion 1, and the paper would still fail criterion 3. Criterion 2 is met: 148 people analyzed of 165 recruited (52, 50, and 46; Table 1, p. 11), well above 50. Context class is none: there is no AI-free arm, and the paper studies how people trust and adopt AI-made ideas, which the codebook lists in the screening report and omits from the dataset. |
| Kusumegi et al. (2025), "Scientific production in the era of large language models", Science (https://doi.org/10.1126/science.adw3000) [c083] | All four analyses are observational, with no random assignment to AI (pp. 2-7, Results and Limitations and Future Directions). The productivity analysis includes 168,553 authors, meeting the people-count criterion (p. 11, Figure 1 caption); this is an analyzed count, not a randomized count. Outcomes are publication counts, writing complexity, publication/review quality proxies, and characteristics of accessed or cited references (pp. 3-6, Results; pp. 11, 13-14, Figures 1, 3 and 4). None measures creativity, novelty, originality, or similarity/diversity across different authors' outputs. Under creativity-codebook.md sections 3 and 6, the reference characteristics do not qualify as across-people output diversity. The paper is therefore excluded from both the core dataset and observational context. |
| Le et al. (2026), "GraphStory: Collaborative Story Writing through Event-Based Narrative Editing", arXiv preprint (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2606.16102) [c423] | The main comparison explicitly used ChatGPT first and GraphStory second for everyone, so generative-AI condition order was not randomized (p. 7, Section 5.2). It analyzed 16 people with repeated observations in both conditions, not separate groups (p. 7, Sections 5.1-5.2; p. 8, Figure 5); the separate formative study recruited only five people and reported no distinct analyzed count (pp. 3-4, Sections 3.1-3.3). Neither study reaches the 50-person threshold. The quantitative outcomes are participants' self-reports about the tools and workload, with qualitative interview feedback; the paper reports no scored creativity or diversity outcome for participants' stories (pp. 7-9, Sections 5.2-5.4, Figures 5-7). Context class is none because studies with only self-reported outcomes are omitted under codebook Section 3, even though the main comparison has a non-random within-person design. |
| Lebron Flores (2026), "Effects of Human-AI Collaboration in the Ideation Stage of the Design Process", Master of Human-Computer Interaction thesis, Carleton University (https://doi.org/10.22215/etd/2026-17060) [c824] | The reported recruited sample is 26 people, so the study cannot meet the minimum of 50 analyzed people; the exact analyzed count is not explicitly reported (PDF p. 27, printed p. 19, §3.1; PDF pp. 46–47, printed pp. 38–39, §§4.1.1–4.1.2). All participants experienced all conditions in a fully counterbalanced order, but the text does not state random assignment of condition order or how sequences were allocated (PDF p. 30, printed p. 22, §3.4.1). Counterbalancing alone does not establish either random or non-random allocation, so criterion 1 remains unclear and design_class is other. Final-sketch originality is scored by expert clustering, and design fixation is scored from copied features, satisfying criterion 3 (PDF pp. 33–35 and 40, printed pp. 25–27 and 32, §3.4.2). Context class is none because a qualifying observational allocation mechanism is not established; the study is excluded from core on sample size regardless. |

(2026), "The Digital Divide in Generative AI: Evidence from Large Language Model Use in College Admissions Essays", Proceedings of the Thirteenth ACM Conference on Learning @ Scale… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3774398.3811620) [c084] | Not eligible for core or context under creativity-codebook.md, Sections 1, 3, and 6. The authors interpret convergence of group means as homogenization, but that interpretation does not meet the codebook's diversity definition. |

Criterion 1 is unclear: the abstract does not say whether condition order was randomized. Criterion 3 is met: originality is scored on the participants' AUT ideas. | Lin et al. (2025), "Inkspire: Supporting Design Exploration with Generative AI through Analogical Sketching", Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3713397) [c455] | The main comparison analyzed 12 people, below the 50-person threshold (p. 7, Section 5.1; p. 14, Section 7.1). The authors state that tool and task orders were counterbalanced but do not state random assignment to orders (p. 8, Section 5.3.2); this supports randomized=not_stated and design_class=other, rather than a confirmed randomized or explicitly non-randomized comparison. Creativity-support and final-design-quality scores are participant self-ratings (p. 8, Section 5.2; p. 11, Section 6.3). The computed BERTScore concerns user prompts, not scored creativity or diversity of the produced designs (p. 9, Section 6.2.2); interaction logs and qualitative observations do not supply a qualifying scored design outcome (pp. 10-11, Sections 6.2.3-6.3). The formative exchange involved seven designers but no comparison of assigned conditions or qualifying scored output (p. 4, Section 3), and is not combined with the main study for the participant threshold. Accordingly the paper is excluded from core and does not provide an eligible context comparison under the codebook. |
| Lira et al. (2026), "Coach not crutch: Evidence that AI can improve writing skill despite reducing effort", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2502.02880) [c458] | Random assignment to generative-AI rewriting practice is explicit in Studies 2-4 (PDF p. 2, Fig. 3; PDF p. 5, Study 3; PDF p. 9, Procedure), so the random-assignment component is met. The analyzed-people threshold is independently confirmed by the 1,294-person follow-up subsample analyzed in Fig. 6A (PDF p. 4) and the 608-person subsample analyzed in Fig. 8A (PDF p. 7); these are figure-specific analyzed counts, not assignment totals or asserted regression Ns. Exact main-regression people counts remain null. The task is professional cover-letter editing, and the scored outcomes assess concision, readability, navigation, formatting and ease of responding (PDF p. 12, SI A1, Table S1), hypothetical interview preference (PDF p. 12, SI A2), and writing-quality improvement per unit effort (PDF pp. 18, 37, Tables S10 and S32). No creativity, originality or diversity across people is scored. Thus the creative-task/outcome requirement is not met: criterion 3 requires creativity or diversity on the output, rather than any output score. AI is removed during the tests (PDF pp. 2, 5, 9). The ancillary AI-versus-editor comparison uses the same professional-writing outcomes (PDF pp. 25-26, SI D2, Fig. S8), so it does not qualify as creativity context evidence. No unresolved source discrepancy changes these decisions. |
| Liu et al. (2026), "AHAlogy: Agent-Supported In-Class Analogical Learning for Primary School Students' Divergent Thinking", Proceedings of the Extended Abstracts of the 2026 CHI Conference on… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3772363.3799384) [c090] | The abstract reports a total study sample of N = 38 elementary school students, below the 50-person threshold; it does not identify the exact number analyzed (sentence 3). Core eligibility is therefore no even though analyzed and randomized counts remain null. The between-subjects description does not establish random or nonrandom assignment, so criterion 1 is unclear, design_class remains other, and no observational context classification is established (sentence 3; entire abstract). The abstract names divergent-thinking fluency, flexibility, and originality but does not describe produced responses or scoring, so output-based scoring cannot be confirmed and criterion 3 is unclear (sentence 5). AI availability or removal during assessment, immediate versus delayed timing, and the analysis unit are not stated; question, availability, timing, and unit are null for all three outcomes. The originality statement does not identify a tested between-group contrast, so its direction is also null (sentence 5). Only the complete abstract was available; the publisher PDF returned HTTP 403. OpenAlex W7153871185, abstract reconstructed from abstract_inverted_index (https://api.openalex.org/works/https://doi.org/10.1145/3772363.3799384); independently retrieved identical Semantic Scholar abstract (https://api.semanticscholar.org/graph/v1/paper/DOI:10.1145/3772363.3799384?fields=title,abstract,openAccessPdf); read in full 2026-10-09; full text not available. |
| Livotov and Mas’udah (2025), "AI-powered inventive design: idea funnelling, concept creation, and hybrid problem-solving teams", Proceedings of the Design Society (https://doi.org/10.1017/pds.2025.10062) [c847] | Excluded as a model-only workflow comparison. For criterion 1, no human allocation mechanism is stated: the explicit sequential order concerns approaches applied to AI-agents (PDF p. 4, printed p. 482, section 3, Phase 4 and Table 1), and the authors are described as human operators (PDF p. 3, printed p. 481, section 3). Criterion 2 cannot be established because no analyzed person count is given; reported production counts are ideas, not people (PDF p. 6, printed p. 484, section 4). Output scoring exists, but criterion 3 of the codebook requires output from a human participant sample: this paper rates AI-generated concepts (PDF p. 4, printed p. 482, Phase 4; PDF p. 7, printed p. 485, Tables 5–6). It therefore does not establish an eligible human experiment or context comparison. |
| Mak and Walasek (2025), "Style, sentiment, and quality of undergraduate writing in the AI era: A cross-sectional and longitudinal…", Computers and Education: Artificial Intelligence (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.caeai.2025.100507) [c095] | S1 is observational: 4,820 reports by 2,000 unique students are compared across submission periods; AI assistance was not assigned and no student disclosed AI use (p. 2, Sections 1.3-1.4; p. 3, Methods). The student count meets the 50-person criterion. The outcomes measure lexical markers, linguistic style, sentiment, and academic quality, without a qualifying creativity, novelty, originality, or across-writer diversity outcome (pp. 3-4, Sections 2.5-2.8). MTLD measures vocabulary variety within one report, not diversity across people. S2 compares automated rewrites with student originals and assigns no people to AI help; its style and sentiment comparisons also fall outside the creative-work scope (pp. 4-5, Section 2.9; pp. 6-9, Sections 3.2-3.3). Creativity and epistemic diversity are proposed for future research (p. 12, Sections 4.5 and 5). Under creativity-codebook.md, Sections 1, 3 and 6, neither study qualifies for core or context evidence. |

Criterion 1 is unclear specifically as to in-scope generative-AI assistance: the author calls the tool generative (pp. 4-5, Section 2.4), but its implementation matches sketches to existing images and retrieves suggestions from a library using an image classifier (p. 2, Section 2.1; pp. 5-6, Section 3.1), which does not establish generative assistance under the codebook. Independently, only 31 people remained for the main evaluation (p. 15, Section 5), below the 50-person threshold; even the initial 35 is below it. Novelty and usefulness were scored on participants' sketches by trained evaluators (p. 7, Section 3.2; p. 10, Section 4.3), satisfying criterion 3. This is a small randomized experiment, not an observational or AI-versus-human-output context study. |
| Preuschoff et al. The 18 people analyzed are far below 50. The 20 Study 2 raters scored the outputs and received no AI help, so they do not count (criterion 2 not met). Blind external ratings of the group outputs meet criterion 3 (p. 409, Section 3.2). Core is no under any answer to criterion 1, because criterion 2 fails. The paper goes to the screening report as an experiment with fewer than 50 participants whose randomization is not stated. |
| Pu et al. (2025), "IdeaSynth: Iterative Research Idea Development Through Evolving and Composing Idea Facets with…", Proceedings of the 2025 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3706598.3714057) [c881] | The comparative lab study analyzed 20 people, each using both systems, below the 50-person threshold (p. 12, Sections 5.1.1-5.1.2). Both arms use generative AI, making the contrast ai_design. The reported creativity-related outcomes are participants' self-ratings; interaction logs measure system use, and no independent rater or computed creativity/diversity measure scores the briefs (pp. 12-15, Sections 5.2-5.3.5; p. 18, Section 7.4.2). The formative study has 8 participants and qualitative thematic analysis, with no randomized comparison or qualifying output score (p. 4, Section 3; p. 21, Appendices A-C). The deployment study has 7 participants drawn from the lab sample, no comparison group, and thematic interview outcomes (p. 16, Section 6). No study meets all core criteria or a context category. |
| Purnamasari et al. (2026), "Magic Easel: Environment-Grounded Visual Inspiration for Gen AI Creativity Support", Proceedings of the 14th Nordic Conference on Human-Computer… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3829807.3829832) [c104] | The abstract reports a within-subjects study with N = 32 (OpenAlex/Semantic Scholar abstract, sentence 3), below the 50-person threshold, so criterion 2 is not met and core remains no. The abstract names divergent thinking and perceived usefulness without establishing creativity or diversity scored on participant-produced output (sentences 4 and 6), so criterion 3 remains unclear. context_class remains none provisionally: the accessible source does not establish an observational output comparison, an AI-versus-human output study, or a synthesis. The paper remains in the screening report as a small within-subjects study; randomized status is unverified. See creativity-codebook.md, sections 3 and 4. Only the complete matching OpenAlex/Semantic Scholar abstracts and Crossref metadata were available; the ACM publisher landing page returned HTTP 403 on 2026-10-09. |

Study 2 analyzes 28 researchers in a non-randomized interview study with no scored creativity or diversity outcome (pp. 6–7, Section 3.2.1). (2026), "AI-assisted design synthesis and human creativity in engineering education", Frontiers in Artificial Intelligence (https://doi.org/10.3389/frai.2026.1714523) [c121] | Criterion 1 is met: each self-organized team drew a shuffled, opaque envelope that assigned it to ChatGPT-4 or to no LLM (p. 4, Section 2.1; p. 7, Section 4). It analyzes 7 AI and 5 non-AI teams (p. 5, Section 3) of two to three members each (p. 4), so at most 36 people, or at most 24 if the eight teams stated on p. 4 is right (arithmetic, not printed). Criterion 3 is not met: all four creativity measures are the participants' own post-survey answers about their team's designs (pp. 4 to 5, Section 2.2). The two raters only transcribed the answers, and the authors name reliance on self-reported measures as a limitation (p. 7, Section 4). The paper calls the team measures “objective creativity metrics” (RQ2a, p. 4) and the AI teams' performance “objectively lower” (p. 5, Section 3.2), but Section 2.2 shows that participants answered them in the post-survey, and Section 5 speaks of “relying solely on self-reported measures” (p. 8). The paper belongs in the screening report as a small randomized experiment whose only outcomes are self-reported. |

How smart replies impact language and agency in the workplace", Telematics and Informatics Reports (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.teler.2023.100062) [c953] | Core eligibility is no because no creativity or between-person diversity outcome is scored. Random assignment to human-written smart replies, AI-generated smart replies, or the same AI replies with time pressure is explicit (PDF p. 10, section 3.1), although the reply-generation provider and model are not identified. The reported sample of 346 participants and one technical exclusion establish that the study exceeds 50 people; randomized and final analyzed totals are not separately stated and remain null (PDF p. 11, section 3.3). The reported outcomes are response sentiment as an agency proxy, suggestion acceptance, and qualitative experiences (PDF pp. 10-15, sections 3.2 and 4.1-4.2). Language homogenization is discussed as a possible consequence without a measured diversity outcome (PDF p. 15, Table 5). Criterion 3 fails, and the study does not qualify for any context class (creativity-codebook.md, section 3). |
| Wu et al. (2026), "InkIdeator: Supporting Chinese-Style Visual Design Ideation via AI-Infused Exploration of Chinese Paintings", Proceedings of the 2026 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3772318.3791675) [c958] | The main comparison analyzed 12 unique people, each in both AI conditions (p. 11, Sections 6.1-6.2; p. 14, Figure 10), below the codebook threshold of 50. The 24 scored ideation outputs are not 24 independent people (p. 12, Section 6.4). Tasks and interfaces were counterbalanced with a Latin square, but randomized condition order is not stated (p. 11, Section 6.3; p. 12, Figure 7), so criterion 1 remains unclear and the design is other. Expert-rated clarity and appeal satisfies the output-scored criterion; the CSI items are self-reports (p. 12, Section 6.4). The formative interviews and extended use cases provide no qualifying scored-outcome comparison (p. 4, Section 3; pp. 15-16, Section 8). Corpus annotation and model-output evaluations are model_only; their raters, painting counts and generated-image variety do not add randomized creators or an across-person diversity outcome (pp. 6-10, Sections 4-5.2; p. 20, Appendix B). No study qualifies for the core dataset or an AI-versus-human, observational or synthesis context class; retain the paper in the screening report as a small experiment with randomization unstated. |
| Yang et al. (2025), "FlexMind: Supporting Deeper Creative Thinking with LLMs", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2509.21685) [c970] | The controlled comparison has 20 analyzed people completing both conditions, below the codebook’s 50-person threshold (PDF p. 7, Sections 4.1.2–4.1.3; p. 12, Figures 7–8). Counterbalanced task–condition order is stated, but random assignment of people or condition order is not, so criterion 1 remains unclear and design_class remains other (PDF p. 7, Section 4.1.3). Random selection of ideas for rubric calibration and chain-length analysis does not establish randomized condition assignment (PDF p. 8, Section 4.1.4; pp. 9–10, Section 4.1.6(2)). Blinded domain experts independently scored submitted ideas on novelty, feasibility and value, combined into overall quality, satisfying criterion 3 (PDF p. 8, Section 4.1.4; p. 11, Figure 6 and Table 2). Unreconciled idea counts concern outputs and do not change the participant count. The separate expert study has six people using FlexMind without a baseline and reports qualitative findings and self-reported experience, with no independently scored output outcome (PDF p. 10, Sections 4.2–4.2.5). Neither study qualifies for core inclusion; no codebook context class is established. |
| Yang et al. (2026), "Scanvas: Discovering and Developing Synergistic Opportunities in Generative Design Spaces", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2609.34062) [c650] | Study 2 analyzed the same 12 people in both conditions, below the 50-person criterion; the 58 Scanvas and 53 Baseline ideas are not additional participants (p. 11, Section 4.2.2; p. 14, Figure 5). Both arms used GPT-5.5, and task, condition, and presentation order were counterbalanced; the full document does not establish randomized assignment (pp. 10-11, Sections 4.2-4.2.3; Appendix 7, pp. 23-24). Independent expert ratings and embedding-based semantic spread are scored on outputs (p. 12, Section 4.2.4). Study 1 is model-only; its two experts are raters and its 90 observations are ideas, not people assigned to assistance (p. 10, Section 4.1). This paper remains screening-only: it supplies neither an eligible core study nor unassisted human-versus-AI output, observational, or synthesis context. |
| Yang, Ma, and Xiao (2025), "A Comparative Study on the Impact of AIGC and Traditional Inspiration Sources on the Design Process of…", Creativity, Innovation and Entrepreneurship, AHFE International… (https://doi.org/10.54941/ahfe1006116) [c969] | Twelve participants (6 Midjourney, 6 Pinterest), below the 50-person threshold (PDF p. 3, printed p. 105, Method). Design is other: two-group comparative experiment; assignment not stated. Neither the methods text nor Figure 1 describes how participants were allocated, so neither random nor nonrandom assignment is established; quasi_people requires nonrandom assignment (PDF p. 3, printed p. 105, Method and Figure 1; form-spec.md, Field notes, design_class). The outcomes concern search keywords, search behavior, and selected inspiration images; no creativity or diversity score is reported for participant-produced design output (PDF pp. 3-9, printed pp. 105-111, Results and Conclusion). The study does not qualify for core or context inclusion and belongs in the screening report only. |
| Yoshida et al. (2026), "Towards Cognitive Process-Aware Proactive Writing Support", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2608.30424) [c659] | The formative study is descriptive and unassisted (16 people), and the two user studies analyze 11 and 10 people; every study is below 50 participants (p. 5, Sec. 3.2; p. 11, Sec. 5.1). Both conditions in each user study use generative AI and are counterbalanced via Latin square; random assignment is reported only for allocation between studies, so randomization of condition order remains unclear (p. 11, Sec. 5.1). The formative study's 152 self-report events are observations, not people, and 14 user-evaluation participants overlap with that sample (p. 8, Sec. 3.6; p. 11, Sec. 5.1). No study reports an eligible comparison of creativity or diversity scored on human outputs: formative outcomes concern cognitive processes and support needs, the prediction evaluation classifies those processes, and the user evaluations use questionnaires and interaction logs (pp. 6, 8, 11-13, 19, 22). These studies belong only in the screening report, with no core or context evidence. |
| You et al. (2026), "Surprise2Refine: Axis-Centered Exploration-To-Refinement for Agent-Assisted Creative Scaffolding", arXiv (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2608.12605) [c660] | The evaluation analyzed 14 people, each using both AI configurations, below the 50-person threshold (p. 8, Section 6; p. 15, Table 1). The five formative participants are recorded separately; overlap is not stated and the counts are not combined (p. 4, Section 3). Evaluation order was counterbalanced, with no reported randomization (p. 8, Section 6; p. 11, Section 8). Criterion 3 remains unclear because the sole output-scored measure is LPIPS of each participant's nine initial AI generations; its status as a qualifying human-output creativity measure is unclear, and it does not compare different people (p. 15, Appendix B.1). Q8 concerns generated designs generally and Q12 perceived help with creativity; no separate creativity assessment of the final posters is reported (p. 9, Figure 8; p. 15, Appendix B.3; pp. 18-19, Figures 15-16). The formative study has no assigned AI comparison or scored creativity/diversity outcome. Neither study supplies a qualifying context comparison under the codebook. |
| Yu, Corino, and Phillips (2025), "Story Forge: A Card-Based Framework for AI-Assisted Interactive Storytelling", Electronics (https://doi.org/10.3390/electronics14152955) [c140] | Criterion 1 met: individuals were randomly divided between two generative-AI story tools, Story Forge and Squibler's AI Story Writer (p. 19, Section 4.3; abstract, p. 1), an ai_design contrast. Criterion 2 not met: 30 people were enrolled and randomized, and the form counts all 30 as analyzed because no exclusions are reported (pp. 19-21, 29). That is well below 50, and the paper gives no other count of people. Criterion 3 not met: no outcome scores the creativity, novelty, or cross-person diversity of the stories. The outcomes are interaction-record and path metrics and self-reported SUS, CSI, and UPI, and the authors say the evaluation lacks 'third-party objective assessment of the quality of the content that is produced' (p. 29). The one open point is causal relationship strength. The paper does not say how it is computed and reads it as users' comprehension of causal links (p. 22). If it scores each story's causal coherence, Review 1 decides whether coherence counts as quality under usefulness (final.open_items). The two trained raters on p. 21 rated collaboration levels, and no results from them are reported. Output metrics listed on p. 21 (word count, diversity of story elements) are also not reported. Core is no under any answer, because criterion 2 fails. |
| Zastudil et al. (2025), "Helping or Homogenizing? GenAI as a Design Partner to Pre-Service SLPs for Just-in-Time Programming of AAC", Proceedings of the 27th International ACM SIGACCESS Conference on… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3663547.3746384) [c142] | Counterbalanced within-person experiment; randomization not stated (pp. 8 and 10, Sections 4 and 4.2.2). Seventeen people were recruited and P5 was excluded for a control-software application error, leaving 16 analyzed people, below 50 (p. 8, Section 4.1; p. 12, Section 5; p. 20, Section 8). The same people completed both conditions. Output-based diversity and quality are measured (p. 12, Section 4.4.2; pp. 13-16, Sections 5.1.4 and 5.3.2-5.3.4), meeting criterion 3. Core eligibility is no on sample size. The complete text, including Appendices A-B, does not establish either random or explicitly non-random allocation; context_class remains provisionally none rather than assuming an observational design. |
| Zhang et al. (2026), "TreeWriter: AI-Assisted Hierarchical Planning and Writing for Long-Form Documents", arXiv preprint (https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2601.12740) [c987] | The comparative lab study randomly assigned within-person tool/content order between two generative-AI configurations (p. 14, Section 5.2; p. 27, Appendix D, Table 6), satisfying criterion 1. Its analyzed cohort is 12 people, evidenced by response counts in Figure 9 (p. 17), below 50; the separately randomized total is not reported. The lab outcomes are questionnaire ratings and qualitative feedback, with no creativity or diversity scored on essays or edits (p. 14, Section 5.4; pp. 26-27, Appendix C; pp. 30-32, Appendix G). The formative survey has six participants (pp. 5-6, Sections 3.1-3.2), and the descriptive field deployment has eight participants with two collaborating authors excluded from that count (p. 18, Section 6). Neither has comparison conditions or creativity/diversity scored on written output (pp. 25-26, Appendix B; pp. 32-33, Appendix H), and the samples do not overlap (p. 25, Appendix A). Thus no study meets all core criteria or a context-evidence category; retain the paper only in the screening report. |
| Zhao et al. (2026), "Experimental study on improving the quality of interdisciplinary design: Collaborate with AI psychology…", Design Studies (https://doi.org/10.1016/j.destud.2025.101371) [c144] | Abstract only. The study has 31 designers completing both conditions, with 62 evaluated design outcomes; these are 31 people, below the core threshold of 50. The abstract describes a within-subjects study without stating whether condition order was randomized, so criterion 1 remains unclear and design_class remains 'other'. Expert ratings of the produced designs meet criterion 3. Core eligibility is 'no' because the analyzed sample is too small. context_class remains 'none' while assignment is unresolved; a confirmed nonrandom comparison would qualify as observational context under the codebook. |
| Zhi et al. (2026), "What Does AI Do for Cultural Interpretation? A Randomized Experiment on Close Reading Poems with Exposure to…", Proceedings of the 2026 CHI Conference on Human Factors in Computing… (https://doi.org/10.1145/3772318.3791727) [c682] | Final review confirms individual random assignment to Control, AI-Single, or AI-Multiple (p. 4, Sections 3.1.1-3.1.2), but criterion 1 includes the creative/ideation-task requirement in codebook Sections 1 and 3. The task asks readers to identify stylistic features and explain their effects in existing poems, assessed as close-reading proficiency (p. 4, Section 3.1.3; pp. 5-6, Section 3.2.1; p. 15, Section 6.3.6), so the full criterion 1 is not met. Criterion 2 is met: 400 people were analyzed after exclusions (Control 141, AI-Single 115, AI-Multiple 144; p. 6, Section 3.3). Criterion 3 is not met: expert graders scored feature correctness, interpretation depth and insight, and writing clarity and coherence, without a reported creativity or across-person diversity outcome (pp. 5-6, Section 3.2.1). Textual overlap compares participants' answers with supplied AI interpretations (p. 11, Section 5.4.1); pleasure measures are self-reports (p. 6, Section 3.2.2). No test after AI removal was conducted (p. 15, Section 6.3.6). The outstanding documentation items do not affect these decisions. |
