## Reclassification of the current collection

The 25 papers of the May 2026 collection went through the same forms as every other candidate. The table gives each paper's label in the May JSON, its status under the new rules, the design found in the full text, and the basis of the decision.

| Paper | May 2026 label | New status | Design found | Basis |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Anderson, Shah, and Kreminski (2024) [c001], "Homogenization Effects of Large Language Models on Human Creative Ideation" | RCT; Anchor — homogenization | small | lab_rct | people analyzed: 33 |
| Ashkinaze et al. (2025) [c002], "How AI Ideas Affect the Creativity, Diversity, and Evolution of Human Ideas…" | RCT; Counterpoints / nuance | core | ai_vs_human/online_rct | Criterion 1 (met): S1 randomizes each participant to a sequence of five (item, condition) trials in which conditions do not repeat and their order is randomized (p. 5, Figure 2 and Section 3.4.2). Exposure to pre-generated ChatGPT example ideas (ideas_supplied) therefore varies within person, and the Control trial shows only human examples. Criterion 2 (met): 844 unique participants are analyzed (p. 7, Table 2), far above 50. The paper reports no per-arm counts, and participants completed 4.05 of 5 trials on average. The models print observations, not people (3,271 in Tables 14 and 18), and 11 participants lack the AI-feeling covariate (p. 20, Table 9). With at most one trial per condition per person, the models still hold at least 655 people. Criterion 3 (met): every outcome is scored on the submitted idea, by classifier-predicted originality and by SBERT diversity across people (p. 3; pp. 7-8). Caveats: the AI help is exposure to example ideas, not interaction with a chatbot. The landing page told every participant that some examples came from ChatGPT (p. 5, Figure 3), so disclosure varies only the per-idea labels. Unless Control comes first in the sequence, a participant's Control trial follows AI trials on other items. Ideas within response chains are not independent, and the diversity results carry no participant-level uncertainty. S2 is ai_vs_human context only. |
| Boussioux et al. (2024) [c003], "The Crowdless Future? Generative AI and Creative Problem-Solving" | RCT; Counterpoints / nuance | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human | Criterion 1 is not met: one author prompted GPT-4 in both configurations, while crowd solutions came from a separate challenge (PDF p. 8, journal p. 1595, Human-AI Collaboration and Prompt Engineering). Randomization selected solutions and assigned evaluators to rating blocks; it did not assign people to AI assistance (PDF p. 9, journal p. 1596, Evaluator Recruitment and Procedures). Criterion 2 is unclear: the abstract reports 125 global solvers for the challenge, but only 54 HC solutions were analyzed and the distinct solver count for that subset is not explicitly stated; the 180 analyzed HAI solutions were generated by one author, and the 300 analyzed evaluators are raters, not output producers (PDF pp. 2, 8-9 and 12, journal pp. 1589, 1595-1596 and 1599; Table 2). The analyzed number of people therefore cannot be confirmed against the 50-person threshold. Criterion 3 is met: blinded evaluators rated the produced solutions on novelty and value/quality using a five-point scale (PDF p. 10, journal p. 1597, Evaluator Recruitment and Procedures and Dependent Variables). The missing solver count can change criterion 2 but cannot make this study core-eligible because criterion 1 is not met. |
| Castro, Gao, and Martin (2025) [c004], "Human-AI Interactions and Societal Pitfalls" | not in the JSON | excluded | other | Theory paper with numerical simulations; none of the three core criteria is met. Random assignment: the paper develops a Bayesian model of users' choices, with no people assigned to AI conditions (PDF p. 1, Abstract; pp. 8-9, Section 3). People analyzed: the users are a modeled continuum, not a human sample (PDF p. 8, Section 3); M = 1001 is the number of discretized preference support points and T = 100 is the number of simulation iterations, not people analyzed (PDF p. 49, printed e-17, Appendix F). Output scoring: homogenization is the variance of the model's output distribution (PDF p. 13, Section 4.2 and Theorem 1), not a creativity or diversity score on outputs produced by participants; the two GPT-4o abstracts in Figure 1 are illustrations without such scores (PDF p. 3). Codebook Section 3 excludes theory papers from the dataset, and no context class applies. |
| Dolezal et al. (2026) [c005], "The Impact of AI-Generated Text on the Internet" | not in the JSON | context_observational | observational/other | S1 is observational context: archived web text is compared across monthly samples and detector-defined groups, and no person is assigned to AI assistance (p. 7, Section 3.1; pp. 9-11, Section 3.4). The sample-size criterion is not met for this output study because its observations are web pages and monthly sets, with no counted sample of people producing the scored output; the URL sampling target is not a participant count (p. 33, Appendix D). The output criterion is met by semantic and stylistic similarity computed on extracted web text (p. 9, Hyp. 1; p. 10, Hyp. 6). The separate S2 survey collected responses from 853 unique participants but assigns no AI and scores beliefs, with no creative output (p. 8, Section 3.2); its respondents cannot supply the corpus study's participant count. The 50 analyzed fact-checkers are raters for factual accuracy, not people producing creativity or diversity outcomes (pp. 9-10, Hyp. 2). No study meets all three criteria, and the unresolved reporting discrepancies do not change eligibility. |
| Doshi and Hauser (2024) [c006], "Generative AI enhances individual creativity but reduces the collective…" | RCT; Anchor — homogenization | core | online_rct | Writers were randomized individually to Human-only or to the option of one or up to five GPT-4 story ideas (p. 7; Fig. 1 caption, p. 2). In all, 293 writers were analyzed (p. 7), 100 and 98 of them in the two AI arms (p. 2). The Human-only analyzed count and the number randomized are not printed, and criterion 2 is met by a wide margin. Stories were rated for novelty and usefulness by evaluators before they were told the conditions, and scored for embedding similarity to the other stories in their condition; the AI ideas were available while writing (pp. 2-4, 8). AI use was optional (88.4 percent of the analyzed AI-arm writers requested an idea, p. 2). The paper calls its analysis intention-to-treat because AI-arm writers who requested no idea stay in their arm (p. 6), but it does not keep everyone assigned: 3 Human-only writers who admitted using AI and an unstated number of non-completers were dropped after assignment (p. 7). |
| Hintze, Proschinger Åström, and Schossau (2026) [c008], "Autonomous language-image generation loops converge to generic visual motifs" | Corpus; Cross-model / cross-modal | excluded | model_only | Both experiments are model_only. The paper describes models that "interact without human input" (PDF p. 2, printed p. 1, The bigger picture). No people were assigned to AI assistance: conditions vary sampling temperatures or generator-captioner combinations across model trajectories (PDF p. 11, printed p. 10, Methods, Iterative language-image loop construction; PDF p. 6, printed p. 5, Results, Other models). There is no human participant sample; the reported trajectories and start prompts are not people. Scored drift and clustering concern model-generated prompt embeddings, not outputs produced by participants (PDF p. 11, printed p. 10, Methods, Embedding analysis and cosine similarity; PDF p. 4, printed p. 3, Results, Temperature-specific and pooled clustering analysis). All three criteria are therefore not_met. The discussion of prior human cultural-transmission studies does not contribute a human experiment or reanalysis here. No human-output comparison or observational human corpus qualifies the paper as context evidence. Under codebook section 3, model-only studies remain in the screening report and are omitted from the dataset. |
| Kobak et al. (2025) [c009], "Delving into LLM-assisted writing in biomedical publications through excess…" | Corpus; Academic & scientific writing | excluded | observational | Criterion 1 is not met: this is an observational corpus analysis, with no assignment of people to AI assistance; LLM use is inferred at corpus level and cannot be identified for individual abstracts (p. 5, Discussion; p. 7, Sections 4.1-4.3). Criterion 2 is not met: the 15,103,888 observations analyzed are PubMed abstracts, not participants, and the paper reports no participant cohort or people count (p. 7, Sections 4.1-4.2); the people counts remain null. Criterion 3 is not met: excess marker-word frequencies are computed on abstract text, but estimate the prevalence of LLM-assisted writing, not creativity, novelty, or diversity across authors (pp. 3-4, Section 2.2). Homogenisation is discussed with citations to other studies (p. 6, Discussion), and the t-SNE supplement maps local LLM-use estimates rather than measuring an AI effect on diversity (p. 16, Figure S7). Codebook Section 3 excludes AI-text-prevalence studies without a diversity outcome from both the core dataset and observational context. The outstanding journal-version check concerns provenance and does not change these eligibility findings for the saved PDF. |
| Lee and Chung (2024) [c010], "An empirical investigation of the impact of ChatGPT on creativity" | RCT; Counterpoints / nuance | core | online_rct | All six experiments randomly assign individual US online participants (Qualtrics randomizers) to ChatGPT versus Google Web search and/or a no-aid Human-only arm (Methods, PDF pp. 6-8; Reporting Summary, PDF p. 11). Each analyzes 194 to 400 people, well above 50. The outcome is a creativity score that lay judges (and, as a robustness check, three expert judges) gave each submitted idea, written while ChatGPT was available. Per-arm counts are not printed. Experiment 2B also compares 100 unedited ChatGPT responses (no participants) with human ideas; that comparison is ai_vs_human context. Experiment 2B tests its randomized arms in a model that also holds those 100 responses (t297), which makes those outcomes' unit output but leaves all three criteria met. |
| Liang et al. (2024) [c011], "Monitoring AI-Modified Content at Scale: A Case Study on the Impact of ChatGPT…" | Corpus; Academic & scientific writing | context_observational | observational | Criterion 1 is not met: the authors collect existing public peer reviews and estimate AI use at the corpus level; no reviewers are randomly assigned to AI assistance (p. 6, Sec. 4.1; p. 42, App. G and Ethics Considerations). Criterion 2 is unclear: the reported counts are reviews and papers, and the number of distinct people whose reviews were analyzed is not stated (p. 6, Table 1; p. 42, App. G and Table 31); people counts remain null. Criterion 3 is met for the homogenization analysis: convergence/divergence is computed from each review's embedding proximity to the centroid of all review embeddings for the same paper, including the focal review (p. 9, Sec. 4.7 and Figure 10). Under codebook Secs. 1 and 3, writing compared across writers for similarity qualifies as output-scored diversity. Table 26 (p. 35) reports estimated AI shares for the similarity-defined groups, not similarity means or an effect of assigned AI assistance; this limits the result to observational context but does not negate the output-based similarity assessment. Core eligibility is no regardless of the unstated reviewer count because random assignment of AI is absent. |
| Liu, Wang, and Yang (2025) [c013], "Generative AI and Content Homogenization: The Case of Digital Marketing" | Corpus; Market consequences | context_observational | observational | Criterion 1 is not met: Italy's ChatGPT ban is a natural experiment, and the authors designate Milan and select four control cities rather than randomly assigning people to AI (p. 6, Sections 2.1-2.2; p. 13, Section 3). The paper explicitly describes its data as observational (p. 22, Section 6). Criterion 2 is unclear: the analyzed 2023 descriptive sample contains businesses (Milan 486, Paris 429, Lyon 432, Madrid 544, Valencia 315; p. 7, Table 1, Step 3; p. 8, Table 2), and regressions count business-weeks, not distinct human producers (p. 10, Section 2.3; p. 14, Table 4). No analyzed people count is stated. Criterion 3 is met: the paper computes pairwise text similarity on Instagram posts from different businesses, then aggregates it per business-week (pp. 8-10, Section 2.3). The paper remains observational context and is not core regardless of whether the missing people count could meet the threshold. |
| Meincke, Mollick, and Terwiesch (2024) [c014], "Prompting Diverse Ideas: Increasing AI Idea Variance" | RCT; Fixes / mechanisms | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human/model_only | Criterion 1 is not met: GPT-4 generates the AI idea pools directly, and the human comparison reuses student ideas from Girotra et al. (2023); no people are randomly assigned to AI assistance (pp. 5-6, Prompting Strategies for Innovation and Brainstorming). Criterion 2 is unclear for S1: the paper reports ideas, not the number of distinct students whose output is analyzed. Page 5 says 100 randomly chosen student ideas, whereas Appendix D lists Student Ideas as 10x100 (p. 20); neither establishes at least 50 people. Criterion 3 is met for S1: diversity is computed on the idea text, including the human-produced student pool, using Universal Sentence Encoder embeddings and average within-pool cosine similarity (p. 9, Technical set-up; p. 11, Table 2). S1 is ai_vs_human context, and S2 is model-only. Core eligibility is definitively no because criterion 1 fails, regardless of the unresolved student count. |
| Meincke, Nave, and Terwiesch (2025) [c015], "ChatGPT decreases idea diversity in brainstorming" | RCT; Anchor — homogenization | core | online_rct | Criterion 1 met: Lee and Chung randomized participants to complete creative challenges with or without ChatGPT, against web search or no aid; this paper states it once for the series (p. 1107), and Lee and Chung state it for every experiment (c010 PDF p. 2 and Methods, pp. 6-8). Criterion 2 met: this PDF prints no people count, but for Experiment 2A it reports 96 ChatGPT-aided ideas and says each participant submitted one idea (both p. 1107), and Lee and Chung asked each Experiment 2A participant for one idea (c010 PDF p. 2), so at least 96 people were analyzed in that arm alone. The Reporting Summary's 'Data exclusions N/A' (PDF p. 5) marks the item as not applicable to a secondary analysis; it does not say that no data were excluded, and the bound does not rest on it. Supplementary Table 1 (online, not in this PDF) gives n = 291 for Experiment 2a (SI p. 3), the sample Lee and Chung analyzed after excluding 7 of 298 complete responses (c010 PDF pp. 6-7). The one-idea statement does not hold for Experiment 1 (c010 PDF p. 6), which criterion 2 does not use. Criterion 3 met: the outcome is the fraction of unique ideas among the ideas participants produced, from Universal Sentence Encoder similarity (pp. 1107-1108, Fig. 1). design_class is online_rct for all six studies: under the form-spec rule, a reanalysis takes the original experiment's design class, and Lee and Chung randomly assigned MTurk or Prolific participants in the USA in every experiment (c010 PDF pp. 6-8; c010 form). This document does not itself say where the experiments ran. The participants are Lee and Chung's (2024; candidate c010), whose analyses rated each idea individually (p. 1107); under codebook section 8 rule 4 this reanalysis can supply the across_people record for that sample. The Experiment 2b ChatGPT-only contrast compares AI output with human output and is context only. |
| Moon et al. (2026) [c016], "The Link Between Diverse Words and Original Ideas Is Weakening in the AI-Era…" | Hybrid; Anchor — homogenization | context_observational | ai_vs_human/model_only/observational/other | For the PDF on file, no study meets all three criteria. Criterion 1 is not met: S1-S5 compare archival essays by admissions cycle relative to ChatGPT's release, without assigning people to AI (PDF pp. 32-33, 48). S9 uses each student's original essay and two AI essays generated by researchers after data collection; all participants wrote a generation prompt followed by a revision prompt, with no random assignment or randomized condition order (PDF pp. 86-88). S9 is coded ai_vs_human because the researchers produced the AI outputs; no participant received AI output during the procedure. S6 also compares researcher-generated AI essays with archived human essays (PDF p. 75), S7 validates raters without assigning writers to AI (PDF pp. 80-81), and S8 is model-only (PDF pp. 82-84). Criterion 2 is met through S9: 224 recruited, 5 incomplete or invalid responses and 16 attention-check failures excluded, 203 people analyzed (PDF p. 86); these are the same people across all conditions, not separate arm samples. Distinct applicant counts for S1-S5 are unreported; their essay totals are not people counts (PDF pp. 36-39, 48). Criterion 3 is met: semantic diversity and distinctness are computed on essay text, including the participants' original essays in S9 (PDF pp. 28-31, 88-89); natural-experiment creativity ratings are also scored on essays (PDF pp. 13-15, 85). The principal evidence is observational context. Version uncertainty is tracked separately. |
| Moon, Green, and Kushlev (2025) [c017], "Homogenizing effect of large language models (LLMs) on creative diversity: An…" | RCT; Anchor — homogenization | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human | Criterion 1 is not met: archival essays from 2018-2022 were sampled and compared with essays generated by GPT-4; no human author was assigned AI assistance (p. 3, Section 3.3; p. 5, Section 4.1; p. 7, Section 6.1; pp. 8-9, Discussion). Criterion 2 is unclear: the studies analyzed 200 essays (100 human and 100 GPT-4), 400 essays, and 1,600 essays, respectively, but do not state the number of distinct human authors (p. 5, Sections 4.1 and 5; p. 7, Section 6; p. 6, Fig. 1). Essay counts cannot establish a count of at least 50 people. Criterion 3 is met: DSI scores creativity on the essay text and DGR measures collective diversity from that output (pp. 3-4, Sections 3.4.1-3.4.3). The confirmed failure of criterion 1 excludes the paper from core irrespective of the unknown person count; it remains AI-versus-human context evidence. |
| Padmakumar and He (2024) [c018], "Does Writing with Language Models Reduce Content Diversity?" | RCT; Anchor — homogenization | small | online_rct | people analyzed: not reported |
| Raghavan (2026) [c019], "Competition and Diversity in Generative AI" | not in the JSON | excluded | model_only | Theory paper with model-only Scattergories simulations. The author samples language-model completions to simulate players (p. 19, Section 6; pp. 83-85, Section C.2); no people are randomly assigned to AI and no human participants are analyzed. The 25 game instances and 2,000 samples per model-temperature-instance combination are not people (pp. 17-19, Section 6). A seventh model judges the validity of the generated answers (p. 18, Section 6), so the scored output is not produced by human participants. Appendix C.3 also compares model-generated completions, not human output (pp. 88-89). All three core criteria are not_met. There is no AI-versus-human output comparison, observational human study, or synthesis; under codebook Section 3, the paper belongs only in the screening report. |
| Russell et al. (2026) [c020], "AI use in American newspapers is widespread, uneven, and rarely disclosed" | Corpus; Academic & scientific writing | excluded | observational | This is a corpus audit of detected AI text in published U.S. newspaper articles. The corpora were collected after publication and labeled by Pangram; people were not assigned to AI assistance (p. 3, Sections 2.1-2.3; p. 16, Appendix C). There is no participant sample in an AI-assistance study: article and corpus-author counts do not establish an analyzed sample of at least 50 participants (p. 3, Table 1; p. 16, Appendix C). The analyses concern AI-text prevalence, disclosure, factuality, and detector robustness, with no scored creativity or diversity outcome (pp. 3-9, Sections 2-6; pp. 16-27, Appendices B-D). Cosine similarity identifies republished-article clusters whose AI-label rates are counted; it does not measure an AI-related change in diversity across different writers (p. 20, Appendix D.1, Effects of republishing articles). The em-dash and specificity discussion supplies stylistic observations, not a creativity or diversity score (p. 17, Appendix C; p. 18, Table 6). Under the codebook, AI-text prevalence studies without a diversity outcome are excluded from both the core dataset and observational context. |
| Russell et al. (2026) [c021], "StoryScope: Investigating idiosyncrasies in AI fiction" | not in the JSON | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human | This is an AI-versus-human corpus comparison, not a study assigning people to AI assistance. Published Books3 stories are paired with stories generated by five LLMs from reverse-engineered prompts (p. 3, Section 2). Random selection of discovery prompts and random ordering of templates in LLM-facing comparisons do not assign people to AI conditions (p. 4, Section 2.1; p. 17, Appendix B), so criterion 1 is not met. No human creative-task participants were analyzed; the corpus and rarity sample counts are stories, not people (p. 17, Tables 4-5; pp. 22-23, Appendix G and Table 12). The two human annotators evaluate existing stories and produce no creative output (p. 19, Appendix C), so criterion 2 is not met. Rarity and dispersion are computed on story outputs (p. 9, Section 5; p. 22, Appendix G), which justifies scored_on_output=true for those outcomes and ai_vs_human context inclusion. Criterion 3 nevertheless is not met under the codebook requirement for output that study participants produce: the scored texts here are archival human stories and model generations. The unresolved story-count and dispersion-method details do not affect these eligibility decisions. |
| Si, Yang, and Hashimoto (2025) [c022], "Can LLMs Generate Novel Research Ideas? A Large-Scale Human Study with 100+…" | RCT; Academic & scientific writing | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human/model_only | For the saved arXiv v1, criterion 1 is not met: human-written ideas are compared with autonomous AI-generated ideas (with or without the first author's reranking), and reviewers, not writers receiving AI assistance, were randomly assigned (pp. 4-5, Section 2; p. 8, Section 4.4; Appendix J, p. 42). Criterion 2 is independently not met: 49 idea writers were analyzed after one of 50 submitted ideas was excluded, just below the 50-person threshold (p. 7, Section 4.1; Appendix J, p. 42). The 79 reviewers and the reported 104 people across writing and reviewing do not constitute 104 creative-task participants, and model-generated ideas do not add people (p. 7). Criterion 3 is met: blinded experts scored novelty on the written proposals using a 1-10 scale explicitly assessing creativity and differences from existing work (p. 4, Review and Evaluation; Appendix E, p. 32, item 9). Style normalization of all conditions before review does not create a randomized AI-assistance contrast (p. 4; Appendix D, p. 31). S2 is model-only and adds no human participants (pp. 13-14, Section 7.1). The unverified possibility of a later version with different analyzed counts is recorded in final.open_items; the decision here concerns the saved PDF. |
| Sourati, S. Ziabari, and Dehghani (2026) [c023], "The homogenizing effect of large language models on human expression and thought" | Review; Synthesis / review | context_synthesis | synthesis | The screened arXiv v2 is a narrative review (p. 2, Abstract; p. 5, scope; main text pp. 3-17). It reports no random assignment of people to AI assistance, no participant sample analyzed, and no scoring of participant outputs of its own. The creative-ideation results on p. 15 are summaries of cited experiments [124, 125], not a reanalysis. All three core criteria are therefore not met; retain as synthesis context in the reference list only, with no estimates to extract. The cited experiments are separately represented in candidates.json as c001 and c006. Comparison with the journal version remains a provenance follow-up; this decision is based on the supplied preprint. |
| Terwiesch et al. (2026) [c007], "Artificial intelligence and its impact on creativity and diversity: An…" | Hybrid; Fixes / mechanisms | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human | For the saved working paper, criterion 1 is not met: students produced ideas before widespread LLM availability, and the authors directly prompted GPT-4 to generate the comparison pools (pp. 10-12, Section 4). Random selection of student ideas and random presentation to raters are not random assignment of people to AI assistance (pp. 10 and 12). Criterion 2 is unclear independently of criterion 1: the source course had 50 students, but the paper does not state how many distinct students contributed the 200 sampled ideas analyzed (p. 10). People counts therefore remain null. The 200 and 201 analyzed raters (Appendix B, pp. 31 and 34) evaluate outputs and do not count as creative-task participants. Criterion 3 is met: novelty is rated on product ideas and similarity is computed from idea embeddings (pp. 14-18); purchase intent also evaluates the ideas (pp. 12-14). The saved document is AI-versus-human context evidence and remains outside the core regardless of the unresolved student count. This decision must not be transferred to the later journal version named in candidates.json without reviewing that version and any added experiments or reanalyses. |
| Wenger and Kenett (2026) [c024], "Large language models are homogeneously creative" | Corpus; Cross-model / cross-modal | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human/model_only/other | Criterion 1 is not met: creativity tests were administered separately to LLMs and people, with no random assignment of people to generative-AI assistance (pp. 4-5, Section 3.3; pp. 16-17, Appendix A). Criterion 2 is met: 114 people were recruited, 12 excluded, and 102 analyzed; the LLM counts are models, not people (p. 5, Section 3.3, Human subjects). Criterion 3 is met: originality and population-level variability are computed on the test responses (p. 6, Section 3.4 and eq. 1; p. 18, Appendix B, eqs. 2-4). S2 compares human samples and S3 compares models; neither supplies an eligible AI-assistance experiment. The paper is retained as AI-versus-human context evidence. |
| Wu, Black, and Chandrasekaran (2025) [c025], "Generative Monoculture in Large Language Models" | not in the JSON | context_ai_vs_human | ai_vs_human | Criterion 1 is not met: no people are assigned to AI assistance; models generate outputs for comparison with existing human corpora (p. 4, Section 4.1; pp. 5-7, Section 6). Criterion 2 is not met: there is no human participant sample; the reported 742 books and 100 coding problems are corpus units, and unique human authors are not counted (p. 6, Sections 6.1-6.2; p. 14, App. B.1). Criterion 3 is not met under the current codebook's requirement for output that study participants produce: diversity is scored on archival human texts and model generations, so the outcome-level scored_on_output fields remain true without establishing a qualifying participant outcome (p. 4, Sections 4.1-4.3; pp. 6-7, Section 6). Retain ai_vs_human context under codebook Section 3 because the paper directly compares AI-generated and human-generated outputs. No unresolved source question changes any criterion. |
| Zhou et al. (2026) [c012], "Creative scar without generative AI: Individual creativity fails to sustain…" | RCT; Fixes / mechanisms | core | lab_rct | Individuals were randomly assigned to ChatGPT 4.0 or no ChatGPT on AUT and product problem-solving tasks (p. 4, Section 2.1; Figure 1, p. 5). All 61 enrolled students were randomized (31 ChatGPT, 30 control; pp. 3-4), and the same 61 completed the seven-day experiment and were analyzed (p. 4; Table 1 note 'N=61', p. 12): above 50, but not by much. Outcomes are blind ratings of each AUT idea and each solution, a count of idea categories, and SBERT similarity of the answers (pp. 6, 13-16). The paper does not state how many took the Day 30 follow-up, which affects only the Day 30 records. The document screened is the 2024 preprint of the 2026 journal article that c012 names. The search record describes the same seven-day experiment in the journal version, which has not been checked. |

## The diversity measure

A diversity or similarity outcome is reported by 47 recommended or unsettled papers, 102 outcomes in all. The table gives, for each, the measure, what it was computed over, how its uncertainty was obtained, the unit of analysis, the statistics available, and the reported values with their location.

| Paper | Question | Measure | Computed over | Uncertainty | Unit | Statistics | Reported | Where |

The GPT Only mean line lies to the right of (above) the Control line: similarity higher, diversity lower (sign reversed). Three ways to show these effects on the site, with what each would display:

1. **A pooled standardized effect with a stated approximation.** Every similarity or diversity measure is converted to a standardized mean difference between arms, with a standard error that treats the paper's unit of analysis as independent. This gives one number per question and a forest plot in the learning section's format. It also pools measures that differ (cosine similarity of embeddings, semantic distance between ideas, rater-judged uniqueness, entropy of themes), and a standard error computed over pairs of outputs understates the uncertainty.
2. **A forest plot of standardized effects with no pooled number.** The same conversion and the same plot, without the summary row, and a caption that says why. Readers see the direction and the approximate size for every study without a headline number that depends on the conversion.
3. **Each paper's own measure with direction only.** A table that lists the measure, the unit, the reported values, and whether AI made outputs more alike or less alike, with no conversion. Nothing is approximated, and nothing can be pooled.

Two syntheses of the diversity question show why the choice matters. Holzner, Maier, and Feuerriegel (2025) pool six effect sizes from four studies and report that AI makes ideas less diverse, g = −0.86 [−1.33, −0.40]. De Rooij and Biskjaer (2026) pool 19 studies of the same question and report a homogenization effect of d = 0.33 [0.09, 0.57]. Both find that AI makes ideas more alike, but the magnitudes differ by a factor of about 2.6. The two syntheses include different studies and standardize different measures, so a pooled number depends on these choices.

## Outcomes measured after AI was removed

Creative work after AI was taken away is measured by 22 recommended or unsettled papers, 81 outcomes in all. These answer the `after_ai` question, the direct parallel of the learning section's default view.

## Implications for extraction

Record randomized, analyzed, and unique-participant counts separately, together with the number of clusters when assignment was by class or team. For each outcome, record who or what scored it, the unit of the paper's own analysis, whether AI was available when the output was produced, the delay for after-AI outcomes, and for diversity measures the metric, what it was computed over, and how its uncertainty was obtained. Record participant overlap across papers (Meincke, Nave, and Terwiesch 2025 reanalyze the participants of Lee and Chung 2024) and company involvement (the two Dell'Acqua et al. field experiments ran with Boston Consulting Group and with Procter and Gamble). Keep the version read, the latest version, and the retrieval date as separate fields, because many papers exist as a preprint and a journal article with different samples.
